- id: "02.15"
  phase: 2
  area: encoding
  symptom: |
    Detached headless boot (soak/API server launch) dies ~13 seconds in
    with exit code 1 and "SERVER DID NOT COME UP" in the log, even though
    the exact same launch works fine from an interactive terminal.
  cause: |
    A detached process (started via cmd.exe /c or Start-Process without an
    explicit encoding) inherits Windows' legacy cp1252 console codec, not
    UTF-8. Any print() in a startup-time script (prestartup_script.py, a
    custom node's module-level code, etc.) that emits a non-ASCII
    character -- most commonly a status emoji like a checkmark or
    cross-mark -- raises UnicodeEncodeError the instant it executes,
    killing the boot before the server ever binds its port.
  fix: |
    Set PYTHONUTF8=1 and PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8 in the environment of
    every detached/headless launch path (launcher .cmd/.ps1), not just
    the interactive one. Any new boot path (soak harness, CI runner,
    scheduled task) must set both vars before invoking python.exe.
  verify: |
    Boot the server via the detached launcher and confirm PYTHONUTF8/
    PYTHONIOENCODING are present in its environment; the boot log shows
    no UnicodeEncodeError and the server reaches a listening state within
    the normal ~20s window. Grep every launcher script for PYTHONUTF8=1.
  tags: [windows, encoding, cp1252, headless, boot, launcher, unicode]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260612-01

- id: "07.17"
  phase: 7
  area: vram
  symptom: |
    A full-episode VRAM soak measures a peak of 15.8GB against a 14.5GB
    ceiling, on both device modes, in a pipeline stage (LTX-AV) that
    combines a text encoder with a video generation pass.
  cause: |
    The text encoder (Gemma) stayed GPU-resident through the entire video
    pass instead of being released once its output was consumed, holding
    VRAM the video stage then had to compete for.
  fix: |
    Move the text encoder to CPU after its output is captured (attempted).
    A later re-measured soak PROVED this offload had NO effect on the
    measured peak, and the change was reverted. Record the disproof
    explicitly so the same offload isn't re-attempted blind on a future
    VRAM-ceiling rip -- re-derive the actual peak-VRAM contributor before
    trying an "obvious" fix.

    AMENDED 2026-08-20 -- SCOPE THE DISPROOF, IT IS NOT UNIVERSAL. The "no
    effect" result above is real for the case it was measured in: the peak
    was in SAMPLING, so moving the encoder changed nothing. On a lane where
    the OOM happens AT THE ENCODE NODE ITSELF the same offload is decisive.
    Measured on ltx25_video (Gemma-4 12B Q5 GGUF): a GPU encode transiently
    demands ~15.6GB (weights to card + GGML dequant scratch) and OOMs at
    `node 'neg' (encode)` with 13.76GB allocated / 1.88GB requested on a
    15.92GB limit; pinning the encoder to CPU took that encode's VRAM cost
    from ~13,760MB to ~0MB and the OOM stopped.

    THE DISTINGUISHING TEST, and it is one line of the traceback: read WHICH
    NODE raised. Encoder offload helps iff the OOM is at the encode. If the
    OOM is in the sampler, this entry's original disproof stands and the
    offload is a waste of a fix.

    AND THE OFFLOAD HAS A TRAP OF ITS OWN: `initial_device` alone does NOT
    pin an encoder to CPU. ComfyUI-GGUF's CLIPLoaderGGUF already passes
    `initial_device = text_encoder_offload_device()`, so "the loader loads
    to CPU" reads TRUE while `load_models_gpu` still pulls the patcher to
    `patcher.load_device` at encode time. Pinning requires `load_device`
    AND `offload_device` too. That false signal cost three wrong diagnoses
    before anyone checked what the loader actually does with the flag.
  verify: |
    Full-episode soak reports peak VRAM; assert the measured peak matches
    the currently-accepted (reverted) architecture, not the offloaded one.
    Before proposing an encoder-offload fix for a similar VRAM-ceiling
    breach, re-soak once with the offload OFF to confirm it's still the
    right baseline.
  tags: [vram, soak, offload, text-encoder, ltx-av, disproven-fix]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260616-01

- id: "11.27"
  phase: 11
  area: model-registry
  symptom: |
    Selecting a remote/cloud model slot (e.g. "openrouter:slot-a") crashes
    the episode at line-compose with a KeyError, even though the same code
    path works fine for every LOCALLY-curated model.
  cause: |
    The lookup did an exact-match dict access (rows[repo_id]) against a
    dict that was only ever populated from a CURATED, LOCAL model list.
    Remote/cloud model handles are never curated members of that dict, so
    the exact-match access always raises for them.
  fix: |
    Replace exact-match dict access with rows.get(repo_id) plus an
    explicit default (a generic/modern prompt profile) for any handle
    absent from the curated table, instead of assuming every valid model
    id is a member.
  verify: |
    Run with a remote/cloud model handle that is deliberately absent from
    the curated dict; assert the lookup falls back to the default profile
    and completes instead of raising KeyError. Grep any `dict[key]`
    lookup keyed by a user-selectable model handle and confirm it uses
    `.get()` with a documented default.
  tags: [llm-output, model-registry, keyerror, dict-lookup, remote-model, exact-match, llm]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260618-01

- id: "07.18"
  phase: 7
  area: audio
  symptom: |
    A dedicated visualizer/render soak forcing edge-case beats (0-frame
    beats, fully silent beats) surfaces a cluster of four distinct
    crashes/misbehaviors in one soak run: no floor on 0-frame beats, no
    handling for an idle scope on a silent beat, an audio_ref check that
    over-gates valid input, and a master-audio slice that is never fed to
    a downstream consumer.
  cause: |
    Four separate missing guards, each independent: (1) no minimum-frame
    floor before a beat reaches frame-dependent logic, (2) no explicit
    handling path for an "idle" beat scope, (3) assert_usable() gated
    audio_ref too aggressively, rejecting legitimate input, (4) the b000
    master-audio slice was computed but never actually passed to the node
    that needed it.
  fix: |
    Add an explicit 0-frame floor before any frame-dependent computation;
    add an idle-scope branch that handles a silent beat without erroring;
    loosen the audio_ref gate in assert_usable to accept the valid case;
    wire the b000 master-audio slice through to its consumer.
  verify: |
    Run the visualizer soak forcing 0-frame beats, silent beats, and the
    audio_ref/master-slice edge cases together; every beat reaches
    status=success with none of the four original failure signatures.
  tags: [audio, video, visualizer, soak, 0-frame, idle-scope, audio-ref, master-audio]

- id: "08.07"
  phase: 8

- id: "05.10"
  phase: 5
  area: python-scoping
  symptom: |
    Every episode crashes at execution with UnboundLocalError, but only in
    a specific heavy node the unit-test suite never actually exercises.
  cause: |
    A local `import os` statement anywhere inside a function body makes
    `os` a LOCAL name for the ENTIRE function, even on lines executed
    before that import line runs. A meta-stamp line earlier in the same
    function referenced os.environ, and Python resolved `os` as the
    (not-yet-assigned) local rather than the module-level import, raising
    UnboundLocalError.
  fix: |
    Move the local import to the top of the function (or remove it in
    favor of the existing module-level import) so no name inside the

- id: "11.31"

  cause: |

    only ~12 words wide -- far too narrow for normal LLM length variance
    at small targets, even though the same +/-20% is reasonable at large
    targets.
  fix: |
    Add an absolute slack floor (+/-25 words) that governs at small
    targets; let the proportional (+/-20%) band take over only once the
    target is large enough (>=125 words) that the proportional band is
    wider than the floor.

    absolute floor (not the proportional percentage) governs the

    unfixed, in another lane at small word targets -- generalize the fix
    rather than patching one call site.

    and exact ownership. A model could therefore return syntactically useful
    data in the wrong depth or cardinality, and a typed retry could repeat it.
  fix: |
    Define exact collection ownership and canonical depth at every nested row
    boundary. At the accepted typed-object boundary, lift or project only
    provably authoritative declared data into its canonical location, reject
    ambiguous, suffixed, or unknown shapes, and re-run the complete graph
    validator after projection.
  verify: |
    Test suffixed owned fields, a complete top-level collection nested in a
    caller row, and a complete child collection nested under the wrong parent.
    Assert the deterministic repair preserves authoritative values, produces
    the canonical shape for base and typed-repair responses, and fails closed
    for ambiguous or graph-invalid input.
  tags: [llm-output, schema-ownership, collection-placement, canonical-shape, typed-repair, fail-closed, llm]

- id: "11.42"
  phase: 11
  area: llm-output
  symptom: |
    A full score or script is regenerated to correct one localized semantic
    omission, causing context-window overflow or repeating the omission while
    needlessly risking already-valid structure and prose. The same failure can
    appear when a complete typed artifact has only a few author-owned strings
    over finite schema caps: a whole-artifact typed repair reauthors valid
    surface but repeats the local overflow.
  cause: |
    The repair ladder treats a missing fact in one explicitly owned leaf as a
    whole-document failure instead of distinguishing local semantic repair
    from structural or global-contract repair. It may also classify visible
    Pydantic string-length errors as local without proving the still-hidden
    compiler, graph, reference, or rewrite contract is otherwise valid.
    A lazy slot wrapper can also erase the concrete transport behavior needed
    to choose a safe bounded patch route, and generic attempt accounting can
    overwrite a direct patch's real parse/schema failure with the empty
    factory-result classification. An upstream base or rewrite prompt can also
    expose private schema caps as authoring targets and invite a broad rewrite,
    creating more local overflows than the proven patch envelope can carry.
  fix: |
    Derive a minimal typed patch plan from immutable contract evidence. Allow
    the model to replace only the named leaf fields; require exact one-for-one
    target coverage, required literal anchors, no out-of-scope edits, and a
    complete merged-artifact validation. For author-owned over-cap prose,
    derive exact typed validation locations and declared caps, preserve the
    complete original selected text in a bounded request, preflight a probe
    through the complete artifact validator before patch dispatch, and let the
    author make one bounded shortening decision. Unknown, broader, hidden
    structural, malformed, or still-over-cap defects remain fail-closed on the
    normal ladder; never Python-clip author prose. Admit any transport only by
    an explicit behavioral capability: exact-tokenizer prompt-fit enforcement,
    or complete-message delivery with terminal provider/context failure. Keep
    output limits strict across provider-wide floor overrides. Preserve JSON
    transport through lazy slot wrappers; unknown wrappers are not patch
    eligible. Let a direct patch own its bounded receipt so
    generic completion handling cannot rewrite its parse/schema/contract truth.
    Because models approximate character counts, expose only a conservative
    authoring ceiling below each immutable schema cap; keep the larger hard
    rejection edge private so the model cannot anchor on it, and never
    Python-clip authored text. Name source inputs as explicit transform tasks,
    not output-like fields that invite unchanged copying, and keep the patch
    contract concise rather than duplicating competing instructions.
    Apply those conservative ceilings at base, restart, full-repair, and rewrite
    authoring boundaries as well as the local patch. A rewrite must change only
    prose required by its review and preserve every other prior prose leaf byte
    for byte; do not solve broad overflow by arbitrarily widening patch capacity.
    Distinguish a model-authored replacement overflow from an invalid patch
    root in the bounded receipt without retaining rejected prose.
  verify: |
    Remove one immutable literal from an otherwise valid score and script.
    Assert a small typed patch repairs only its planned target, rejects missing
    or unplanned targets, leaves every other field byte-identical, and clears
    the complete artifact validator. For a live over-cap prose shape, exercise
    every whitelisted leaf/cap, reject mixed or oversized-target errors, prove
    hidden graph/compiler failure takes the normal repair, require exactly one
    author patch call with a bounded receipt, and reject malformed, duplicate,
    unknown, blank, or still-over-cap replacement rows without another reroll.
    Exercise a scheduler-wrapped full-message remote slot and assert a captured
    localized shape uses same-slot JSON-mode bounded repair, its strict output
    limit survives a larger global floor, and an over-envelope shape preserves
    full repair. Assert malformed direct-patch receipts retain their real
    parse/schema statuses. Capture the repair request and require its authoring
    target to have safety margin below the hard cap. Inject a replacement just
    over that cap and require a prose-overflow receipt code with no rejected
    text retained. Capture base, full-repair, and rewrite instructions and prove
    each exposes only conservative prose ceilings; require rewrite to preserve
    non-target prose byte for byte.
  tags: [llm-output, bounded-patch, typed-schema, localized-repair, context-window, fail-closed, llm]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260712-12

- id: "11.50"
  phase: 11
  area: llm-output
  symptom: |
    A whole-artifact pass or its typed repair reserves output tokens from a
    word target or fixed ceiling alone, truncates its own schema-bearing prompt
    or serialized response, and may echo the request envelope instead of
    returning the requested artifact root. A source-evidence artifact with
    unbounded rows, per-row spans, or serialized strings has no defensible
    fixed reservation even when its top-level list count looks bounded.
    The same failure occurs when a nested whole-score artifact leaves scenes,
    shots, beats, line IDs, or authored prose unbounded: a low word target does
    not bound serialized planning metadata.
    A generic string clamp can also shorten a literal provenance quote at a word
    boundary while retaining its old coordinate end, creating a synthetic
    source-span mismatch that a raw metadata repair cannot parse if the quote
    still exceeds the finite schema cap.
  cause: |
    Budgeting ignored accepted line/item count, required per-item metadata, the
    failed-artifact repair context, or the fact that the model-facing schema
    itself had no finite serialized surface. The root contract also failed to
    forbid input-envelope keys or a known scalar-vs-array repair shape explicitly.
    A generic over-length-string clamp can further hide an authored-artifact
    overflow by silently changing prose instead of advancing the repair ladder.
    The same compatibility clamp is unsafe at a source-grounded P0 boundary:
    quote text is deterministic metadata, but claims and tone remain authored
    content, so one generic coercion cannot safely handle both classes.
  fix: |
    Bound the model-facing artifact surface first: rows, nested evidence spans,
    and every material serialized string/item length. Then calculate output
    reservation from every remaining structural size driver, including accepted
    line or item count, after the authoritative graph is final. Keep typed
    repair context to the failed artifact plus only the locked facts the model
    cannot derive; do not duplicate a copyable JSON request envelope. Record
    the budget and surface receipt, require the requested root literal at
    response start, explicitly require scalar fields that the model previously
    returned as arrays, forbid request-envelope keys, and fail loud when prompt
    plus output cannot fit the actual context window. That refusal is the
    PRE-CALL capacity phase and it is correctly terminal, because the arithmetic
    that produced it is deterministic. Do not generalize it into "every capacity
    failure is terminal": a call that RAN and used its whole output allowance is
    a different phase and must advance the retry ladder (see 12.68).
    For a directly emitted nested score, local maxima are sufficient when they make every global
    scene/shot/beat/line bound finite; do not introduce a second draft/compiler
    representation merely to obtain those bounds. If a live model still
    exhausts a bounded direct score because it must re-emit deterministic graph
    mechanics (IDs, parents, order, anchors, or copied advisory state), a
    compact authoring draft plus compiler is permitted only when Python can
    derive every omitted field uniquely from accepted artifacts, the compiler
    returns the same strict final contract, and no dialogue or story decision
    moves from the model to Python. Disable generic string clamping at authored-
    artifact boundaries so over-limit prose reaches typed repair or fails closed.
  verify: |
    At fixed word steer, increase accepted graph width and assert either the
    reservation rises or the finite schema rejects the over-limit surface. For
    a source-evidence artifact, reject an over-limit row/span/string surface
    before the call. For a nested score, reject over-limit local collections,
    IDs, and prose, then measure a representative maximum base and repair
    prompt against the real tokenizer/context reservation. When a compact
    draft/compiler boundary is necessary, assert compiler rejection of unknown
    references/unused topology, `compile(project(final))` structural fidelity,
    and actual-tokenizer fit for base, restart, semantic repair, rewrite, and
    rewrite repair. Assert authored over-length prose is not silently clamped,
    the repair payload is not a copyable JSON request envelope or a duplicate
    accepted artifact, the effective prompt retains every required schema path
    and scalar-field instruction, envelope-root output is rejected, and an
    insufficient context cap fails before generation rather than silently
    truncating.
    For an exact literal source quote that alone exceeds a finite span cap,
    repair from the raw reply only after proving the complete quote occurs in one
    legal source field under the normal ambiguity rules. Reindex it, replace only
    the quote with that coordinate's exact bounded source prefix, and recompute
    the end. Disable generic string clamping for source-grounded P0 calls; never
    use this exception to shorten a claim, tone, or nonliteral/ambiguous text.
  tags: [llm-output, token-budget, context-window, structured-output, envelope-echo, fail-loud, xref-11.30, llm]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260711-12

- id: "12.53"
  phase: 12
  area: remote-llm-capability
  symptom: |
    A saved remote LLM works in earlier smoke legs but a later call fails with
    HTTP 400 before generation because a process-wide parameter disables a
    capability that the selected endpoint now marks mandatory.
  cause: |
    The client treats one global reasoning/tool/format preference as valid for
    every model and discards per-model capability metadata from its cached
    catalog. A stale or cold cache then has no way to prevent an incompatible
    request or learn from the provider's precise capability rejection.
  fix: |
    Preserve the provider's per-model capability contract in the explicit
    catalog cache and resolve global preferences against the selected model at
    call time. A mandatory capability overrides only the incompatible value for
    that model, using the least enabled supported setting. For a stale/cold
    cache, recognize only the provider's exact capability-specific 400, retry
    the same model once with a compatible setting, and remember that capability
    for subsequent calls in the process. Do not turn generic 400s into retries,
    substitute another model, or consume the transient retry budget.
  verify: |
    Cache a model with a mandatory capability and assert an incompatible global
    setting is replaced before the first request while ordinary models retain

    heuristic can then fabricate content in a window that does not exist.
  cause: |
    Presence and positivity are collapsed into one truthiness check. Zero is a
    valid boundary value, while None is the actual missing-state sentinel.
  fix: |
    Branch on explicit presence first. Treat every non-None onset as known,
    clamp its derived window to the legal nonnegative range, and reserve the
    missing-data fallback and warning for None. Preserve zero in receipts.
  verify: |
    Exercise positive, zero, and missing onsets. Positive timing creates the
    expected window; zero creates no positive window and no missing-data
    warning; None alone invokes the documented fallback. Include a corrupted
    negative input to prove clamping cannot create a reverse window.
  tags: [sentinel, zero, timing, truthiness, fallback, title-card, media-consumer]

  legacy_id: PBUG-20260721-15

- id: "12.68"
  phase: 12
  area: bounded-llm-authoring-transport
  symptom: |
    An LLM authors a modest script but cannot complete or repair the requested
    structured artifact inside its real context. A first response truncates at
    the output cap, a later response drifts one mechanical ID, and typed repair
    truncates again because it carries the failed whole artifact beside the
    broad original request. The pass exhausts and kills the episode even though
    the story text itself is small and repairable.
  cause: |
    The authoring boundary is defined by the downstream storage schema instead
    of field ownership. The model repeatedly serializes compiler-derivable IDs,
    parents, speakers, scenes, cues, boundaries, fact mappings, and neutral
    defaults. Repair duplicates those fields, schema authority, and request
    context. Output and prompt costs therefore scale with mechanical metadata,
    while fresh LLM retries repeat the same impossible envelope.
  fix: |
    Define a strict compact wire artifact containing stable IDs and only the
    fields the model owns. Require an exact unique bijection to the accepted
    graph, map by ID rather than position or fuzzy matching, and compile every
    mechanical field from accepted authority before running the complete
    downstream validator. Typed repair carries only a parsed compact draft and
    the smallest trusted context needed to correct it; omit malformed prefixes
    and raw schema/request echoes. Require the complete prompt and full dynamic
    output reservation to fit. Keep liveness finite and truthful: one bounded
    lane, at most one fresh independent lane, then only an explicit deterministic
    floor for advisory craft defects.
    A capacity failure must carry a PHASE, and the phase decides whether the
    ladder may spend another attempt. A pre-call refusal (the measured prompt
    leaves no room for the artifact) is deterministic: re-rolling re-derives the
    identical refusal, so it stays terminal. A call that RAN and consumed its
    whole output allowance without reaching a stop condition is stochastic, and
    it must advance the ladder rather than escape as an unhandled error on
    attempt one of N -- a ladder that advertises a budget and spends one call is
    lying about its own liveness. Being CATCHABLE is not being RETRYABLE: catch
    both phases at the attempt boundary, then re-raise the deterministic one
    untouched. Own the phase in the module that owns capacity arithmetic and let
    the ladder ask it, so the transport and the retry policy cannot hold two
    different opinions about the same failure. The truncated partial artifact is
    never fed to a prose repair, and any message that claimed no retry was
    possible must be corrected when the retry becomes possible.
  verify: |
    Exercise the maximum supported word and row surface with the exact production
    tokenizer. Require both base and semantic-repair prompts plus the complete
    output reservation to fit the real context. Assert authored text is preserved
    while compiler-owned graph fields are rebuilt, and reject missing, unknown,
    or duplicate IDs. Prove typed repair omits whole-request/schema echo and
    malformed raw prefixes. Record a creative-to-technical restart exactly once
    and truthful exhaustion after exactly two flat ladders.
    Drive the real ladder with a transport that raises each capacity phase.
    Prove the run-and-overflow phase reaches the NEXT rung and that a good next
    attempt returns a result; prove an unlucky pass spends its whole advertised
    budget and then dies as the ladder's own typed exhaustion carrying the
    capacity error, not as a bare transport error on attempt one. Prove the
    pre-call phase still propagates after exactly ONE call. Distinguish the rungs
    by an observable the fake transport can see, and assert that observable is
    not equal to any constant another rung uses -- a fixture value that collides
    with the constant under test hides a reverted gate.
  tags: [llm-output, structured-transport, compiler-ownership, context-window, output-budget, typed-repair, line-id, finite-liveness, fail-closed, capacity-phase, retry-policy]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260721-16

- id: "12.73"
  phase: 12
  area: capability-declaration-vs-runtime
  symptom: |
    A component declares a range of legal values, a planner commits real work
    against that declaration, and only the execution step discovers the
    component cannot produce most of that range. The failure is LATE and
    expensive: everything upstream of the executing step has already been paid
    for. It reads as a regression in the executing component, because the
    declaration has usually been wrong for a long time without anyone asking
    for a value outside the narrow band that happened to work.
  cause: |
    The declaration was hand-written and never checked against the runtime that
    must honour it. Three independent channels can each break the promise:
    (1) the declaration itself overstates the component; (2) an environment
    override read at execution time moves a bound the declaration froze, so the
    planner and the executor disagree even though the declaration is correct;
    (3) the bound silently depends on a SECOND dimension (canvas, resolution,
    device, precision) that is itself unpinned, so the declaration is true only
    under conditions nothing enforces. Closing one channel leaves the others
    open and produces a fix that appears to work.
  fix: |
    Make the declaration describe what the runtime DOES, not what the component
    is believed capable of, and use literals rather than deriving from a mutable
    constant -- a static declaration must never track a value that can move
    underneath it, because drift should fail a test visibly rather than be
    absorbed silently. Refuse, do not silently win: when an environment value
    disagrees with a declaration, raise a typed conflict naming the variable and
    the legal value, because quietly overriding an operator's explicit setting
    is its own defect. Pin every secondary dimension the bound depends on, and
    say in the declaration which measurement it came from.
  verify: |
    Feed each component's own declared minimum and maximum through the same
    resolver the execution path uses, and require the values to come back
    unchanged; for discrete menus test every member, not the endpoints, since
    inherited min/max are not menu members. Assert the typed refusal fires for
    a disagreeing environment value, passes for an agreeing one, and is not
    pedantic when the variable is unset. Prove the secondary dimension is
    pinned by setting it hostilely and requiring the declaration to win.
  tags: [capability-declaration, contract-vs-runtime, environment-override, static-contract, fail-closed, typed-error, late-failure, planner-executor-agreement, all-route]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260802-01

- id: "12.74"
  phase: 12
  area: fix-applied-to-an-unreachable-function
  symptom: |
    A defect is diagnosed correctly, a fix is written, reviewed and merged, the
    suite is green -- and production keeps emitting the identical defect,
    unchanged, for weeks. Every artifact of diligence exists except the one that
    matters: the shipped path never executed the new code. Because the evidence
    trail says the fix landed, the next investigation starts by doubting the
    diagnosis rather than the wiring, and re-fixes the same dead function.
  cause: |
    The edited function has no callers on the production path. Two shapes
    dominate. (1) The real logic lives INLINE inside a large orchestrator, and
    the well-named helper beside it is a leftover from an earlier refactor that
    was never deleted -- the good name attracts the fix. (2) A second,
    near-identical helper exists and the caller reaches the other one. Tests
    authored with the fix call the fixed function DIRECTLY, so they prove it is
    now correct while proving nothing about reachability; a green suite cannot
    distinguish "correct and connected" from "correct and orphaned". Coverage
    tools do not help, because the test does execute the line.
  fix: |
    Establish reachability BEFORE editing: find the callers, and if the
    production path is inline code, EXTRACT it into a named helper in the same
    change, so the thing under test and the thing that runs are one object.
    Extract the whole decision -- a helper narrowed to the easy part leaves the
    branches that actually failed still inline and still untestable. Every value
    the block reads from outside becomes an explicit parameter rather than
    migrating with it, and any name the CALLER still needs stays in the caller.
    Then treat the CALL as a separate deliverable from the behaviour: wiring and
    correctness are different claims and need different assertions. Delete the
    orphan rather than leaving it to attract the next fix.
  verify: |
    Assert reachability STATICALLY, over the shipped source rather than a booted
    object, so no stub can satisfy it: the production entry point calls the
    helper exactly once, passes the complete argument contract, and assigns its
    return value. Then exercise every ROUTE through the helper and assert the
    output is PRESENT -- a test that only asserts the bad value is absent is
    also satisfied by an empty result, which is how a silent failure passes as a
    pass. Include a control lane that does NOT reach the block, and confirm it
    genuinely does not, so the matrix cannot be quietly proving nothing. Finally,
    mutate the shipped code once per claim and require the suite to go red for
    each; a fix whose test stays green when the fix is removed is not covered.
  tags: [dead-code, zero-reader, reachability, extract-to-test, false-green, wiring-vs-behaviour, static-assertion, mutation-check, all-route]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260805-04
- id: "03.05"
  phase: 3
  area: loading
  symptom: |
    ComfyUI boots green and the node registers, but the same module raises
    ImportError -- attempted relative import with no known parent package, or
    beyond top-level package -- the moment anything outside the ComfyUI runtime
    touches it. Every pytest run, every CLI entry point, every offline repro
    dies at the import line. Because the pack works in the UI, the failure gets
    read as a broken test harness rather than a broken module, and the helper
    behind that import quietly stops being exercised by anything at all.
  cause: |
    A module has exactly one load identity at a time, and relative imports work
    only under one of them. A pack whose modules are reached by prepending the
    nodes directory to sys.path and importing ABSOLUTELY is loaded as a
    TOP-LEVEL module -- __package__ is empty, so there is no parent for a
    leading dot to resolve against. The import STYLE is therefore part of the
    module's load contract, not a matter of taste, and the contract lives in a
    docstring that the next edit does not read. Editors and refactor tools
    default to the relative form, so a single line added months later plants a
    latent break in a file that is otherwise pure absolute imports. It stays
    invisible under ComfyUI for either of two reasons -- the runtime may import
    the pack as a PACKAGE, giving the leading dot a parent to resolve against,
    or the ComfyUI path may simply never reach that branch -- and the defect is
    per-SITE rather than per-file, so a fix that repairs the two lines someone
    happened to notice leaves the sibling sites live.
  fix: |
    Pick ONE load identity per module, state it at the top of the file, and
    make every import in that file obey it -- absolute under sys.path-prepend,
    relative only in a module that is genuinely always a package member. Then
    stop fixing it by hand. The defect class is repo-wide the instant it
    appears once, so sweep every module under the contract in the same change,
    not just the file whose test went red, and delete the temptation by adding
    a gate that rejects the wrong form mechanically. Treat the untested helper
    as part of the bug: an import that only ever fails outside the runtime has
    been shielding its own function from every test you own, so restore the
    import and then cover what it imports.
  verify: |
    AST-parse every module in the pack and assert that no ImportFrom node has
    level greater than zero in any file declaring the sys.path-prepend
    contract; the assertion must name the file and the line so the next
    occurrence is a one-line diagnosis. Prove the gate can fail by planting a
    single relative import and requiring it to go red. Then import each module
    standalone in a fresh interpreter with only the nodes directory on
    sys.path -- runtime-shaped green is not proof -- and require at least one
    test to CALL the function behind the repaired import, because the import
    line was reachable all along; it was the caller that never existed.
  tags: [relative-import, absolute-import, load-contract, sys-path-prepend, top-level-module, latent-break, repo-wide-sweep, static-assertion]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-252
- id: "03.06"
  phase: 3
  area: loading
  symptom: |
    The boot log stutters. The pack's prestartup banner prints twice, the
    all-nodes-loaded line prints twice, the HTTP route lines print twice, and
    the import-times section names two different absolute paths ending in the
    same pack directory -- all inside ONE process, under one ComfyUI revision
    banner. The node menu looks perfectly normal, one entry per node, so the
    operator dismisses the duplicate lines as noise. Later symptoms do not name
    the cause: an edit to the pack has no effect, a cache or counter behaves as
    if something else is writing to it, a background job runs twice.
  cause: |
    ComfyUI scans more than one custom_nodes root -- the base install tree and
    the user directory are separate roots, and extra path config can add more.
    Each root is scanned independently and a pack present in two of them is
    loaded TWICE, as two distinct module objects with two distinct __file__
    values, because nothing dedupes by pack name. Prestartup scripts run once
    per copy as well, before any node code exists to notice. Every module-scope
    side effect therefore happens twice: environment mutation, sys.modules
    monkeypatching, aiohttp route registration against the one shared server
    singleton, thread spawns, log handlers. NODE_CLASS_MAPPINGS is a plain
    dict, so the second copy's classes silently overwrite the first's -- the UI
    is correct and the code that actually executes belongs to whichever copy
    was scanned last, which need not be the one being edited. The usual setup
    that produces this is the recommended one: keep a source repo and a synced
    deployed copy, then let both land under scanned roots. It is the mirror
    image of a key collision between two packs, and namespacing cannot touch
    it, because the colliding names are correct and identical by construction.
  fix: |
    Make the second load impossible to miss and harmless when it happens. Have
    the pack record a process-global sentinel carrying the __file__ of the
    first load; on a second load, log BOTH paths at error level and return
    before re-running any side effect, rather than proceeding quietly. Make
    every module-scope side effect idempotent on its own terms anyway --
    setdefault rather than assignment for environment variables, a
    router-contains check before registering a route, a named-thread lookup
    before spawning -- because idempotence is what keeps a duplicate scan from
    becoming a duplicate server. Then fix the deployment rather than living
    with the guard: exactly one scanned root may contain the pack, and a source
    repo kept beside a deployed copy belongs OUTSIDE every root ComfyUI scans.
  verify: |
    Query the roots ComfyUI actually scanned, not the ones you believe it
    scanned, and assert that exactly one of them contains the pack directory;
    on failure raise a typed error naming both absolute paths, since two paths
    in the message is the entire diagnosis. Prove it fires by copying the pack
    into the second root, booting, and requiring the error -- then remove the
    copy and require a clean boot whose registration banner appears exactly
    once. Separately assert the count of the pack's registered HTTP routes
    equals the count it declares, because doubled registration is the side
    effect most likely to be swallowed by a defensive try/except and reported
    as success.
  tags: [double-load, duplicate-registration, custom-nodes-roots, module-identity, prestartup, side-effects, idempotence, http-routes, deployment]
  legacy_id: LOG-20260723-double-load
- id: "04.14"
  phase: 4
  area: validation
  symptom: |
    A socket is right there on the node -- correct type, connectable, with a
    tooltip describing exactly what it does. The pack loads clean, the graph
    validates, the queue accepts it. The instant anything is wired to that
    socket, execution dies with TypeError -- got an unexpected keyword argument
    -- naming an input the node itself advertises. Worse than the crash is the
    quiet period before it: anyone planning new work reads that socket as
    evidence the node owns a feature it does not own, and builds on a promise
    the code never made.
  cause: |
    ComfyUI hands every resolved input to the class's FUNCTION method as a
    KEYWORD argument named by its INPUT_TYPES key. Nothing compares those keys
    against the method signature at registration -- INPUT_TYPES is just a dict
    returned by a classmethod, and the mismatch is discovered only when the
    executor actually calls. Declaration and signature drift independently, and
    two histories dominate: an input block copy-pasted from a sibling node
    whose method was never updated, and a migration that stopped after the
    declaration. Both leave a socket that is visible and unreachable. It stays
    latent for exactly as long as the canonical workflow declines to wire it,
    so a green production graph proves nothing about the sockets it does not
    use. The variant with **kwargs is quieter and worse: no TypeError, the
    value is simply swallowed, and the node reports success while ignoring an
    input the operator connected on purpose.
  fix: |
    Treat INPUT_TYPES as a contract the FUNCTION must satisfy, and enforce it
    once at the registry level rather than per node, because a defect that can
    exist on any node needs a check that visits every node. Walk the node
    mappings, collect every key in required, optional and hidden, and require
    each to be a named parameter of the class's FUNCTION method or absorbed by
    a declared **kwargs; check the reverse direction too, since a parameter
    with no default and no declaration is the same drift facing the other way.
    When the audit names a drifted socket, resolve it from history before
    touching it -- deleting the declaration and implementing the parameter are
    opposite changes with opposite blast radii, and picking one because it is
    smaller is a guess wearing a patch's clothes. Where **kwargs is genuinely
    wanted, read the declared keys out of it explicitly and reject unknown
    ones, so absorption never becomes silence.
  verify: |
    Run the parity sweep over the SHIPPED registry by introspection, and
    require the set of declared input names minus the set of signature
    parameters to be empty for every registered class unless that signature
    takes VAR_KEYWORD; the failure message must name the node and the offending
    key. Prove the sweep can fail by adding one bogus key to a single node's
    INPUT_TYPES and requiring red. Then take the specific socket all the way to
    execution -- wire it in a minimal graph, run it, and require a real result
    rather than an absence of error -- because a socket nothing wires is a
    socket nothing tests, and the whole defect lived in that gap.
  tags: [input_types, function-signature, signature-parity, declared-vs-accepted, latent-socket, kwargs-swallow, registry-wide-audit, static-assertion, late-failure]
  legacy_id: NEWBUG-20260731-scene-sequencer-music-sockets
- id: "05.12"
  phase: 5
  area: python-scoping
  symptom: |
    A feature that is Off by default has been dead for weeks. Every run with it
    enabled dies at execution with TypeError -- run() got an unexpected keyword
    argument 'os' -- naming a module the signature never mentions. Default runs
    are clean, the suite is green, and the commit that broke the feature touched
    a different part of the function entirely, so the blame lands on the feature
    rather than on the edit that killed it.
  cause: |
    A re-entrant call built its argument bundle from a bare locals() snapshot,
    filtered by a hand-written exclusion list of the names that existed on the
    day the gate was authored. locals() is not the parameter list -- it is every
    name bound in the frame so far, including function-body imports, temporaries
    and loop variables. Any later edit that binds a new local ABOVE the capture
    point silently adds a key to the bundle. Here a story-scaffold block landed
    one day after the gate and introduced a function-body import of os plus a
    scaffold temporary above it; both were forwarded as keyword arguments into a
    run() that has no **kwargs. The exclusion list is a denylist over an open
    set: it can only name what already existed, so it is guaranteed to go stale.
    Because the path was default-Off, nothing in the suite drove the gate, and
    the defect shipped latent behind a green board.
  fix: |
    Never build a kwargs bundle from a bare locals() snapshot. Derive the
    bundle's membership from the CALLEE, not the caller's frame: filter locals()
    against inspect.signature(type(self).run).parameters, an allowlist that a
    new local cannot poison no matter where it is bound. Keep the explicit
    exclusions only for names that ARE real parameters but must not be
    re-forwarded, such as the keyword-only internals that drive the loop.
    When the forwarded set is small, name the arguments explicitly and delete
    the reflection entirely. The general rule: an argument bundle assembled by
    introspecting the caller's scope is a time bomb armed by the next unrelated
    edit; assemble it from the target's declared interface instead.
  verify: |
    Drive the re-entry path with the option ON and the inner loop stubbed, then
    assert two things separately -- that the captured bundle contains no key
    outside the callee's signature, and that a value the caller set survives the
    round trip. The second assertion matters because a filter that throws the
    payload out with the noise also passes the first. Then bind a junk local
    immediately above the capture point and require both assertions to still
    hold; that is the mutation which reproduces the original defect. Finally,
    inventory the default-Off toggles and drive each one end to end at least
    once: a branch no test enters is a branch where an unrelated edit can be
    fatal without turning anything red.
  tags: [locals-capture, kwargs, signature-filter, denylist-vs-allowlist, re-entry, latent-defect, default-off-path, typeerror, python-scoping]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-416
- id: "05.13"
  phase: 5
  area: migration
  symptom: |
    After a ComfyUI upgrade, a custom node that invokes core nodes directly
    crashes partway down a loader chain, on exactly one link: node
    'AudioEncoderLoader' returned a comfy_api NodeOutput object, expected a
    non-empty tuple. Four earlier links in the same chain loaded cleanly. No
    workflow JSON changed, and the pack's own check is unremarkable -- it
    asserts that a node returned a tuple -- so the failure reads as a defect in
    that one node instead of what it is: an upstream API migration arriving one
    node at a time.
  cause: |
    ComfyUI is migrating core nodes to the V3 node API incrementally. A V3
    node's FUNCTION returns a comfy_api NodeOutput wrapper -- positional results
    stored on .args and exposed through the .result property, which is that
    tuple when non-empty and None otherwise -- rather than a bare tuple; an
    async V3 execute returns a coroutine of one. Because the migration is
    partial, V1 and V3 nodes coexist inside a single invocation chain, so a
    consumer that validates isinstance(out, (tuple, list)) keeps working for
    every unmigrated neighbour and breaks only for the node that moved. The
    underlying error is older than the upgrade: the custom node treated another
    node's return SHAPE as a stable contract. Reaching past your own declared
    sockets into a core node's FUNCTION means owning that node's return contract
    across every host version you claim to support.
  fix: |
    Normalize at the boundary. Put one narrow adapter between the invocation and
    the rest of the pack: run the coroutine if the return is one, unwrap a
    NodeOutput to its positional tuple, pass a plain tuple through unchanged,
    and let everything else -- including an EMPTY NodeOutput -- fall through to
    the loud typed error that names the node and the shape received. Duck-type
    on attribute shape rather than importing comfy_api, so the pack still
    imports outside ComfyUI and is not pinned to one internal module path.
    Record in a comment the host version the shape was confirmed against, and
    keep the adapter internal -- no INPUT_TYPES, socket, widget or output
    change, so saved workflows need no rewire and the fix carries no widget
    regression risk.
  verify: |
    Pin BOTH shapes, not only the one that broke. Four cases: a V1 plain tuple
    passes through identically, a V3 NodeOutput unwraps to its positional tuple,
    an empty NodeOutput still raises the loud error, and an unregistered node
    still raises. Build the doubles from attribute shape alone so the tests run
    with no ComfyUI import. Then re-run the real episode and workflow that
    crashed and require the chain to load past the migrated node into the next
    phase. Re-run that same matrix after every host upgrade and pin the version
    it last passed under: with a partial migration the set of core nodes
    returning wrappers grows silently, and the next one to move will be a node
    your chain already depends on.
  tags: [comfyui-core-api, v3-node-api, nodeoutput, return-contract, partial-migration, node-invocation, duck-typing, version-drift, boundary-normalization]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-266
- id: "05.14"
  phase: 5
  area: execution
  symptom: |
    A diagnostic the operator reads -- a generated-token count, a size, a
    duration -- comes back None on a run that plainly produced output, and every
    branch keyed on it stays silent. Nothing is logged, because the swallow was
    written to be quiet. In this incident three brand-new tests failed on that
    None while the shipped code was innocent: the value that could not survive
    the defensive fallback was the test double, not anything production ever
    passes.
  cause: |
    Three mistakes stacked in one line, getattr(ids, "shape", [len(ids)]) inside
    a bare except Exception. First, Python evaluates every argument BEFORE the
    call, so the third argument is built EAGERLY -- len(ids) runs even when
    .shape exists, and it raises on any object without __len__. A fallback
    written as an argument is not lazy; it is unconditional work that can fail.
    Second, the bare except cannot distinguish a genuinely exotic backend value
    from a TypeError manufactured by the fallback itself, so a programming error
    is laundered into a plausible None that every downstream reader accepts as
    data. Third, the double was LESS capable than the real object: a real tensor
    carries both .shape and __len__, the stand-in carried only .shape, so the
    tests invented a failure mode production does not have and sent the
    investigation into the wrong file.
  fix: |
    Make the fallback lazy by making it a branch. Read the primary first, then
    compute the alternative only on the path that needs it -- shape =
    getattr(ids, "shape", None), then int(shape[-1]) if shape is not None else
    len(ids) -- or pass a callable and call it in the miss case. Put no work
    that can raise into a default argument, a dict.get default, or the
    right-hand side of an or-expression you believe is unreachable. Narrow the
    swallow to the exception the exotic case actually throws, and when you do
    swallow, log the exception text and the value's type so a None always
    carries a receipt. Build test doubles to the real object's FULL touched
    surface -- every attribute and dunder the production line reads -- and when
    a failure appears only under a double, repair the double rather than
    hardening production against a shape it will never see.
  verify: |
    Exercise the line with an object that HAS the primary attribute and assert
    the alternative never runs: put a counter or a raising sentinel in the
    fallback position and require it untouched. That assertion is the one that
    fails on the eager form and passes on the branch form, so it is the proof.
    Exercise it again with an object that lacks the attribute and assert the
    fallback returns the correct value. Assert that a genuinely uncomputable
    diagnostic emits a log line naming the exception and the type, so no None is
    silent. Grep the tree for eager defaults of the same family -- a getattr or
    a dict.get whose default position holds a CALL -- and require each to be
    branch-shaped. Drive the whole check through the real object at least once,
    because a suite that only ever meets the double can only ever verify the
    double.
  tags: [eager-default-argument, getattr, lazy-fallback, bare-except, silent-none, diagnostic-field, test-double-fidelity, false-failure, python-semantics]
  legacy_id: HANDOFF-L1589
- id: "06.07"
  phase: 6
  area: caching
  symptom: |
    A QA plan promises a replay leg -- queue the same workflow twice with
    nothing changed and get the same episode back -- and the leg can never be
    made to pass. Every requeue authors a fresh story with fresh seeds and
    different output bytes. Nothing errors, no cache hit is ever logged, and
    nodes built specifically to FREEZE a decision re-take it on every run. The
    investigation goes hunting for hidden nondeterminism in the model or the
    RNG, when the graph has simply been told to discard its cache on purpose.
  cause: |
    The node's IS_CHANGED returns time.time(), which can never equal the
    previous run's value, so ComfyUI's cache always misses. As local advice
    that is fine, and this guide prescribes it for a live data fetch (see
    06.01). But a cache signature is not a local property: invalidation
    propagates to dependents, so one always-refresh node at the head of a
    subgraph forces its whole downstream cone to re-execute. When that head
    node is also nondeterministic -- an LLM writer that authors new content on
    each call -- the subgraph has no stable identity for anything downstream to
    replay, and a freeze or lock node that returns a wall clock as well pins
    nothing, because what it froze is re-taken before it is ever read.
    Replayability is forfeited by the wiring, not lost to chance, so no amount
    of seed discipline downstream can recover it, and a plan that promises a
    replay leg over such a subgraph is promising what its own graph forbids.
  fix: |
    Derive the signature from what the output actually DEPENDS on, never from a
    wish to run again. Return a digest of the resolved inputs plus the external
    state the node reads -- file mtime and size, the environment variables it
    consults, a version constant you bump when behaviour changes -- so
    identical conditions produce an identical string and a genuine cache hit
    becomes possible at all. Where a run truly must be forced, make the forcing
    an INPUT: a nonce or seed widget the operator bumps, which the graph
    records and a later run can restate, rather than a clock nobody can
    reproduce. Reserve time.time() for edge nodes whose output cannot be
    described by their inputs, and keep those off the deterministic spine,
    because every node downstream of one inherits its unrepeatability. If a
    promised replay leg sits under an always-refresh node, either change the
    signature or move the replay off the live queue onto a captured frozen
    artifact -- do not ship the promise unchanged.
  verify: |
    Call IS_CHANGED twice with identical kwargs and require the two values to
    be EQUAL, then change one field and require them to differ; failing the
    first half is a cache defeat, failing the second is a stale-output bug.
    Statically scan every IS_CHANGED body in the pack for a wall-clock call and
    assert none appears on any node the deterministic spine is declared to
    cover. Prove propagation in both directions: perturb one upstream signature
    and require the dependents to re-execute, leave it untouched and require
    they do not. Then run the claim end to end -- queue the unchanged workflow
    twice and require the second run's output digest to equal the first's.
  tags: [is_changed, cache-signature, determinism, replay, invalidation-propagation, freeze-node, wall-clock, content-derived-signature, xref-06.01]
  legacy_id: KIBITZ-2026-07-12-replay-leg
- id: "07.24"
  phase: 7
  area: audio
  symptom: |
    The default output of a generative slot collapses in quality after a
    release, and the operator can only describe it subjectively -- the music
    bed no longer sounds like music. The prompt builder's history is clean back
    past the last known-good render, the model loads, the node runs, clip
    lengths and sample counts are exactly right, and the suite is green.
    Nothing is red because nothing a machine was asked to check moved.
  cause: |
    The engine behind the slot was swapped and its prompt was not. A prompt is
    not a neutral instruction; it is an asset tuned to one model's training
    distribution and conditioning contract, and it does not migrate. Text that
    steered the old engine reads as noise to the new one, while the new
    engine's own required inputs go unfilled -- the old contract had no concept
    of them, so they take empty defaults: an empty negative prompt where a real
    one is expected, a structural context window left at a stock value instead
    of sized to the cue being rendered. Nothing dangles, so every grep, AST
    parse and import check stays green. The decision that produced the swap is
    the deeper defect: the new engine became the default because it was the
    only candidate that introduced no dependency conflict. Conflict-free is a
    reason a candidate is AVAILABLE, not a reason it should lead -- and the one
    automated gate that could have bracketed the regression, a byte-identical
    golden over the rendered audio, fights the repair by design, because any
    genuine improvement changes the bytes.
  fix: |
    Bind the prompt and conditioning bundle to the ENGINE rather than to the
    slot, so a swap must carry a re-tuned bundle or decline to be the default.
    Before promoting any default, state the evidence that the new engine is
    better on THIS content, and name convenience separately from quality; an
    availability argument must never be allowed to stand in for a comparison
    nobody ran. Having kept the new engine, fill its whole input contract
    rather than only the fields the old one had, deriving each new field from
    the real cue instead of a constant. Expose the settings it is sensitive to
    as overridable constants and trace the resolved engine, settings and prompt
    hash into the run log, because an A/B needs two labelled artifacts. Confirm
    which engine the failing lane ACTUALLY resolved before tuning anything -- a
    theme path and a bed path can produce similar-sounding output while only
    one of them moved. When a byte-identical golden guards the output, schedule
    its operator-gated re-baseline as part of the change instead of meeting it
    as a surprise red.
  verify: |
    Assert that each generative slot's default engine and the prompt bundle
    feeding it come from one declared pairing, so a swap that leaves the bundle
    behind fails at import rather than at listen time. Statically check every
    engine's required conditioning fields against what the shared builder
    supplies and fail on any field falling through to an empty default. The
    quality claim itself is not machine-checkable, so gate it honestly: keep a
    reference render from the last known-good engine, require an operator A/B
    against it, and re-baseline the golden only after that approval -- stamping
    the approval date, engine, settings and prompt hash beside the new golden,
    so the next investigation can tell an intentional byte change from a
    regression.
  tags: [audio, engine-swap, default-selection, prompt-model-mismatch, conditioning-contract, silent-quality-regression, golden-rebaseline, operator-ab, provenance]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-408
- id: "07.25"
  phase: 7
  area: video
  symptom: |
    An animated reveal -- a decode/scramble title card, a typewriter line, a
    wipe, a counter -- plays for the entire span it was given, and the resolved
    thing it exists to show is on screen for only the last frame or two before
    the next transition takes it away. Scrubbing into the middle of the card
    finds the text still mid-scramble where it should long since have settled.
    Frame counts are right, durations match the plan, every test passes: the
    card is simply never READ.
  cause: |
    Progress was written as p = (frame - start) / (end - start) across the
    whole allotted window, so p reaches 1.0 only on the final frame. The
    animation's span was silently equated with the slot's span, and dwell --
    the interval in which the payload is legible -- was never a quantity in the
    code, so it came out as whatever was left over, which is nothing. Every
    element driven by that same p inherits the defect at once: the characters
    revealed, a secondary carrier line, a cursor suppressed only once p is 1.0.
    The exit transition then fires in those same last frames, leaving the
    resolved state no hold at all. The WINDOW may be perfectly sized; this is
    pacing INSIDE the window, an independent defect easily confused with, and
    easily masked by, whatever earlier fix established the window's bounds.
  fix: |
    Separate the reveal span from the window span. Compute the reveal over a
    bounded FRACTION of the window -- the window length times a reveal
    fraction, floored at one frame, with a tunable default near 0.4 and an
    environment override -- then clamp p to 1.0 and HOLD the resolved state
    until the exit. Dwell is a design parameter and must exist in the code as
    one, with the exit still driven off the window end rather than off the
    reveal end. Extract the progress term into a pure helper the draw path
    calls, so pacing can be asserted without rendering a frame, and apply that
    one helper to every element sharing the animation and to the sibling cards
    on the same draw path, since a closing bookend usually carries the
    identical stretch. Confirm the layers around it keep non-overlapping frame
    ranges, so a longer hold cannot run into what follows.
  verify: |
    Assert BOTH ends of the hold in a pure unit test over the helper, with no
    rendering: fully resolved at start plus the reveal span, and still fully
    resolved two frames before the window ends. Either end alone is a false
    green -- the first admits a card that re-scrambles, the second admits one
    that resolves too late to read. Assert p is monotonic, never exceeds 1.0,
    and that cursor or blink suppression keys off resolution rather than off
    the window. Sweep the reveal fraction across its legal range and require
    the held frame count to stay at or above a stated minimum dwell at the
    shortest legal window, so a later retune cannot quietly reintroduce the
    bug. Then look at a real render at the fraction boundary and confirm the
    title is already solid there.
  tags: [video, animation-pacing, reveal-span, dwell-time, progress-fraction, title-card, procgen, pure-helper-test, look-qa, xref-07.13]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-409
- id: "07.26"
  phase: 7
  area: video
  symptom: |
    A generated clip starts sharp and ends as mush. Frame 0 is crisp, every
    later frame drifts further into blur, and it happens inside a single clip
    on every clip. Nothing errors -- no warning, no fallback, no workflow JSON
    change to point at -- and the sibling still-image family from the same run
    looks correct or even better than before. Because the first frame an
    operator inspects is the one the conditioning image pins, the defect reads
    as "the model got worse" and the hunt goes off after a lost sampler, a lost
    seed, or a lost LoRA.
  cause: |
    The model is being driven above its native resolution envelope. A small
    video model is trained at one canvas -- 480p for a 2B-class LTX -- and
    above it cannot reconstruct detail out of noise, so each freely denoised
    step drifts a little further than the last. The image-to-video conditioning
    anchors frame 0 to the supplied still, which is exactly why the degradation
    is progressive rather than uniform and exactly why the anchor hides the
    defect at the frame most likely to be checked. The canvas moved because ONE
    render canvas was shared across asset families -- a landscape change made
    correctly for the still-image family dragged the video engine along with
    it, so the regression has no commit of its own. The tuned
    image-conditioning strength then compounds it. At native resolution that
    setting anchored frame 0 while leaving room for motion; at the larger
    canvas the same setting re-noises far more signal than the model can
    restore, so the value that was right is now the thing producing artifacts.
    A constant tuned at one canvas is only valid at that canvas.
  fix: |
    Treat native resolution as a per-engine contract, not a property of the
    deliverable. Resolve the render canvas per engine family so each model
    renders at its own documented native size, then let the composite scale the
    result up to the deliverable -- upscaling a clean render beats sampling a
    dirty one. Never let a single shared canvas value serve families with
    different envelopes; a change made for one family must be structurally
    unable to move another. When the canvas does move, re-derive every quality
    constant whose meaning depends on resolution, and record beside each
    constant the canvas it was measured at, so the next reader can see the
    dependency instead of inheriting it.
  verify: |
    From one shared canvas input, assert the video engine's built request comes
    back at its native size while the still family comes back at the
    deliverable size -- one assertion covering both families, so a future
    global canvas change cannot silently move the video lane again. Assert the
    composite scales the clip up to the deliverable rather than rendering
    there. For a live clip, read the render resolution back out of the engine's
    own log or ledger and require it to equal the native canvas, because the
    request is a claim and the log line is the evidence. Judge the look on the
    LAST frame of a clip, never the first -- an image-to-video anchor makes
    frame 0 sharp whether or not the render is sound.
  tags: [video, native-resolution, quality-envelope, i2v, render-canvas, progressive-degradation, upscale-to-deliverable, per-engine-canvas, shared-default, tuned-constant]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-412
- id: "07.27"
  phase: 7
  area: vram
  symptom: |
    A quantized checkpoint loads at roughly double its on-disk footprint and
    the sampler falls from seconds per step to minutes per step with no OOM.
    GPU memory sits pinned at the card ceiling while shared system memory
    climbs, and the run blows its wall-clock budget several times over. The
    only direct evidence is one loader line reporting the weight dtype as
    float16 with no manual cast, against a checkpoint whose own filename says
    fp8. With no error to anchor on, the crawl gets read as "the weights are
    too big for this card" and the effort goes into offload policy, reserves,
    and smaller checkpoints.
  cause: |
    A global precision-forcing launch argument overrides the checkpoint's
    native dtype at load and casts every weight up. A natively fp8 checkpoint
    that loads at about 11 GiB becomes about 22 GiB; on a 16 GB card that
    cannot hold the upcast weights plus the text encoder plus the sampler
    working set, so the dynamic offloader pages weights to system memory once
    per step and the per-step cost rises by two orders of magnitude. The flag
    is a GLOBAL -- it knows nothing about which file is being loaded, so it
    silently defeats the entire point of a quantized checkpoint. It survives
    because it was inherited verbatim from a pre-quantization launcher recipe
    and never re-audited when the fp8 checkpoint was adopted, and because that
    recipe had been copied to several launcher sites, so a diagnosis names the
    one site a human remembers while the site that actually boots the server
    keeps the flag.
  fix: |
    Read the loader's own dtype line before changing anything about
    quantization, reserves, or offload policy. It states what precision the
    weights actually landed at and what the file asked for, and it is cheaper
    than any experiment. Then remove the precision-forcing argument from EVERY
    launcher site, enumerated by searching the flag string across the repo, the
    launcher scripts, the install tree, and any config or environment side-file
    that can inject arguments -- not by memory, because a partial removal
    reproduces the symptom and discredits the correct diagnosis. Leave a
    comment at each site saying why the flag is absent, so the next operator
    copying the recipe does not restore it. Re-audit precision globals whenever
    a quantized checkpoint is adopted; precision is a per-checkpoint property
    and a global cannot express one.
  verify: |
    Boot, then assert no process in the launched chain carries the precision
    flag in its command line -- walk the parent chain rather than trusting the
    script you edited, since a stale server can still own the port. Assert the
    loader logs the checkpoint's NATIVE dtype with its expected manual cast,
    and that the measured allocation delta matches the file's on-disk footprint
    within a small margin instead of doubling it. Require zero hits for the
    flag string across the whole tree. Keep the load-time axis and the
    runtime-pace axis separate and close them separately -- proving the weights
    now load native is an architectural result, and a per-step time that
    improved but is still far off target means a second independent defect is
    live. Do not mark the entry fixed on the first axis alone.
  tags: [vram, dtype-upcast, launch-args, quantized-checkpoint, fp8, offloader-thrash, no-oom-crawl, inherited-recipe, multi-site-fix, load-time-evidence, xref-07.16]

- id: "07.28"
  phase: 7
  area: video
  symptom: |
    An image-to-video clip renders clean and error-free but barely moves -- a
    near-still frame with a trace of drift -- long after the conditioning graph
    has been proven correct. There is no warning, no truncation notice, and no
    log line about the prompt at all, and the composed text sits comfortably
    inside the encoder's nominal token limit. Because a motionless clip looks
    exactly like a conditioning failure, the wiring gets rebuilt again and
    again while the lever that actually moved is never touched.
  cause: |
    A small model's EFFECTIVE attention window for conditioning text is far
    shorter than its nominal capacity. On a 2B-class video model the motion
    signal starts washing out somewhere past roughly 188 characters, and the
    prompt is composed scene-description core first with motion clauses
    appended, so the verbs that carry the motion sit at the tail and are the
    first thing diluted. Nothing is dropped and nothing is truncated; the
    motion tokens are simply outvoted by scene description. Every length
    defense in the stack guards a HARD ceiling -- token limits, context
    windows, output caps -- and not one of them fires, because this is a
    quality limit rather than a capacity limit and it has no failure surface to
    fire on. The over-long budget itself was inherited from a shared
    composition helper whose other callers author still images, where a long
    descriptive prompt is an asset rather than a dilutant.
  fix: |
    Measure the model's effective conditioning length empirically and encode it
    as a named per-model constant at the composition site, rather than sharing
    one prompt budget across artifact families whose models have different
    windows. Cap the composed prompt at that constant. Because the cap now cuts
    text, make the composition order match what matters -- name the clause that
    carries the load-bearing axis, protect it explicitly through the cap, and
    let scene description absorb the loss, since a cap that trims the tail is
    itself a regression when the tail is the signal. Carry the remaining budget
    alongside the prompt so a later style prefix or model-side rewrite cannot
    quietly spend past it.
  verify: |
    Compose the prompt for the affected engine and assert its length is at or
    below the measured constant, then assert the protected motion clause is
    still present in the final string -- both for a composition that fits and
    for one that overflows, since only the overflow case exercises the trim.
    Assert any style prefix or downstream rewrite is charged against the same
    budget rather than appended after it. Render an A/B at the cap and well
    above it and confirm the motion difference is visible to the eye; an
    empirical limit nobody can reproduce decays into superstition, so record
    the measurement, the model, and the canvas it was taken at in the
    constant's own comment.

- id: "07.29"
  phase: 7
  area: prompt-composition
  symptom: |
    Generated stills carry a colour cast that tracks the EPISODE rather than

  cause: |
    Two channels, both living inside prompt-building code rather than in any
    widget. (1) A shared prompt-composition helper was invoked with the scope
    profile meant for establishing shots, which appends the episode's ambient
    colour palette -- terms like neon blue or amber glow -- onto a per-SUBJECT
    face prompt. A text-conditioned image model has no way to know those tokens
    describe the world and not the face, so it tints the only subject in frame.
    (2) A style phrase naming a colour temperature was hardcoded into the
    string builder for one subject class, so every render of that class
    inherited the same skew. Neither looks like a defect while reading: the
    palette is real episode metadata and the lighting phrase is defensible
    prose. The defect is SCOPE. The same token is correct at one artifact class
    and wrong at another, and a hardcoded style term is a permanent bias with
    no control surface to turn it off.
  fix: |

    every call site state which class it is authoring. A per-subject prompt --
    portrait, face, object -- receives mood and lighting terms only and never
    the episode-wide colour palette; only whole-frame establishing prompts may
    carry the palette. Delete colour-temperature and lighting literals from
    string builders: a style term that cannot vary per episode is a bias rather
    than a default, and if a class genuinely needs lighting language it belongs
    in that class's profile where it is visible and testable. Keep the
    profile-to-class mapping in ONE table, so a reader learns which class gets
    which terms by reading the table instead of by opening every call site.
  verify: |
    Assert the mapping, not the picture. Build metadata carrying a loud
    saturated palette term, compose a prompt for every artifact class, and
    require the palette token to be ABSENT from each per-subject prompt AND
    PRESENT in the establishing prompt -- a presence-only test is equally
    satisfied by a profile that injects everywhere. Grep the shipped builders
    for colour-temperature vocabulary and require zero literal hits. Finally
    render one subject under two episodes at opposite ends of the era range and
    require the subject's colour not to move between them.
