  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-113
- id: "07.30"
  phase: 7
  area: render-planning
  symptom: |
    A planner returns a legal plan that is plainly worse than one an operator
    can build by hand -- four clips where two would do, so four model loads,
    four renders and four visible seams for a single beat. Nothing errors and
    no log line complains. The same planner refuses other targets that are
    demonstrably coverable, and on one shape it stops answering at all: 18
    seconds at one segment count, still running past 20 at a slightly larger
    one, so the render node hangs instead of failing.
  cause: |
    The search ran TWO full walks over the segment count. The first looked for
    an exact cover at every count from two up to the ceiling; only when that
    entire walk failed did a second walk restart at two looking for a cover
    with a trimmed tail. That ranks candidates by PHASE -- exact before trimmed
    -- when the stated objective is COST, fewest segments. So an exact cover at
    a HIGH count always beat a trimmed cover at a LOW one. A 184-frame beat on
    a min-33 step-4 ladder planned four segments because totals at count c are
    congruent to c modulo 4, making the first exact cover count four, while two
    segments with two frames trimmed had been legal the whole time. Three
    siblings share the shape. The tail-trim window was ASSUMED to be one
    quantum instead of derived from the ladder, which refused 832 beats that
    were coverable. The discrete-menu cover was a bare recursive descent whose
    node count is the menu size raised to the segment count, with no memo, so
    an unsatisfiable target consumed the thread rather than refusing. And the
    join-mode verdict declared a single render from a ceiling comparison
    without checking that a legal length at or above the target exists.
  fix: |
    Collapse the phases into ONE walk over the real objective. At each count,
    give every strategy the contract already permits its chance before the walk
    advances -- a tail trim the adapter itself declared legal is part of what
    that count can do, not a last resort reached after the ladder is exhausted.
    DERIVE every search window from the contract's own arithmetic; a guessed
    window silently refuses work that is legal, and the silence is the
    expensive part. Memoize or bound any recursive cover search before it
    ships, because a search that hangs is worse than one that refuses -- a
    caller can handle a refusal. Make any verdict function prove the plan it
    names exists rather than inferring it from a bound; see 12.73 for the
    general case of a declaration the runtime cannot keep.
  verify: |
    Do not read the search -- sweep it. Run a differential over the whole grid
    of contract and target pairs, old plan against new, and require every
    change to move in the intended direction and only that direction: here
    46,949 plans changed, every one a count REDUCTION, with zero refusals
    introduced and zero increases. Assert the invariant rather than the
    headline number -- a property test comparing segment COUNT alone is passed
    by a reversed fill order, so assert that assembled visible length equals
    the target and that every segment length is legal. Set the sweep floor from
    the real population size, not a round number. Bound the runtime explicitly:
    require a refusal to come back inside a fixed wall clock at the worst count
    the ceiling allows. Mutate each fix once and require the suite to go red
    for each.
  tags: [render-planning, combinatorial-search, objective-ordering, two-phase-search, memoization, unbounded-recursion, derived-search-window, differential-sweep, mutation-check, xref-12.73]
  legacy_id: HANDOFF-L2271, HANDOFF-L4173
- id: "07.31"
  phase: 7
  area: video
  symptom: |
    A scrolling text panel -- credits, a transcript roll, any pre-rendered
    canvas moved past a viewport -- reads as ABSENT to a viewer even though the
    renderer drew it and every unit test passes. Measured by counting lit pixels
    in the column across the finished video, the content is blank for the first
    seconds and blank again for the last, full only in the middle, and the
    fade-out lands on the empty end. The defect is invisible on long content and
    obvious on short content, so it survives every full-length episode and
    appears the first time a SHORT one is rendered.
  cause: |
    The scroll interpolated between CANVAS edges -- offset zero to
    canvas_height minus viewport_height -- while the canvas deliberately
    overstates the content at BOTH ends. The builder starts drawing a fraction
    of a screen down for breathing room, pads below the last line, and sizes the
    bitmap to a generous floor (at least three screens) because it cannot know
    the final height before it lays the text out. Canvas height is therefore an
    ALLOCATION, not a measurement, and using it as the travel span puts blank
    panel on screen at the start and end of every roll. Duration derived from
    the same canvas span inherits the error, so raising the scroll speed
    lengthens the dead tail rather than removing it.
  fix: |
    Have the builder report the real ink span -- the top of the first drawn
    glyph and the bottom of the last -- alongside the bitmap, and anchor both
    the scroll offset AND the duration estimate to that span instead of the
    canvas. Open on the first line and rest the last line on the viewport's
    bottom edge. When the content is SHORTER than the viewport the span clamps
    to zero and the panel HOLDS fully visible rather than drifting through
    emptiness; that clamp is part of the fix, not an edge case. Do not "fix"
    this by trimming the padding -- the breathing room and the generous
    allocation are both wanted; what is wrong is treating an allocation as a
    measurement.
  verify: |
    Assert the content span, not the pixels: build the panel from a fixture and
    require ink to be on screen at the FIRST rendered frame and at the LAST, for
    a transcript longer than the viewport and for one shorter than it. Guard the
    fixture itself with a companion assertion that a longer script produces a
    TALLER span -- a fixture whose items lack the key the renderer draws on
    produces an empty panel of constant height, and "nothing moved" then looks
    like a code defect while a test that renders nothing and asserts nothing
    moved passes forever.
