  legacy_id: "OTR-f70df546"
- id: "07.32"
  phase: 7
  area: video
  symptom: |
    Text burned into a layer that is composited with a LIGHTEN-ONLY blend is
    legible over dark scene content and effectively invisible over bright
    content -- measured 8.96:1 over a dark ceiling and 1.13:1 over a lit
    monitor in the same episode. Adding a black outline, a drop shadow or a
    scrim to the text layer changes NOTHING on the finished video, so the
    obvious fix appears to have silently failed to apply.
  cause: |
    The blend is `screen` (out = A + B - A*B), often narrowed further to a
    single channel. `screen(A, 0) = A`: a black pixel in the overlay is the
    blend's IDENTITY, so every darkening element -- outline, shadow, matte --
    is a mathematical no-op no matter how wide it is drawn. Contrast for the
    glyph is then bounded by the source pixel underneath it, and over a bright
    source there is no headroom left to separate from. Recolouring the glyph
    does not rescue it either: white screens to full white, which is still under
    2:1 against a bright source. The blend is also a single global operation
    over the whole frame with no per-region and no time gating, so a targeted
    scrim is unreachable without changing the blend node itself.
  fix: |
    Move the text to a layer composited AFTER the lighten-only blend, where
    normal alpha compositing applies and an outline is real -- typically a
    subtitle/caption burn that already carries OutlineColour, BorderStyle,
    Outline and Shadow. Do not attempt adaptive glyph colour or per-region
    dimming: both damage picture content to fix an overlay and neither delivers
    a true outline. Note the transport constraint before choosing an RGBA
    overlay between stages: yuv420p carries no alpha, so an intermediate
    encode destroys it. When the text becomes the RESPONSIBILITY of a
    downstream node, audit that node's passthrough exits FIRST -- a burn stage
    that returns the input untouched when it is disabled, finds no timed source,
    or catches its own error will now ship a title-less master with a clean log.
  verify: |
    Measure on the FINAL ENCODED artifact, never from blend arithmetic --
    chroma subsampling, range conversion and compression all move edge
    contrast. Name WHICH pixels: report the original scene, the outline/matte,
    and the final adjacent background as three distinct surfaces, because an
    outline can separate the glyph edge while the fill still measures its
    original ratio. Select the BRIGHTEST underlying-source frame from a
    title-free control render of the SAME upstream output, so a stochastic
    scene difference cannot be mistaken for a contrast change, then require the
    core glyph against its adjacent outline to clear 4.5:1 on every text
    interval.
  tags: [video, compositing, blend-mode, screen, legibility, contrast, outline-noop, yuv420p, caption-burn]
  legacy_id: "OTR-83eb759e"
- id: "09.07"
  phase: 9
  area: subprocess
  symptom: |
    A supervisor launches a server; the server comes up, binds its port and
    answers requests -- and the launcher refuses it anyway, reporting a PID
    mismatch on every healthy launch. Then the between-iteration cleanup
    sweep, instructed to kill everything except itself, kills the supervisor.
    The run dies at the iteration boundary with no traceback and no crash
    artifact, so the next investigation starts by suspecting the server.
  cause: |
    The venv's python.exe is a uv launcher STUB. It fork-execs the real
    interpreter out of the uv-managed cpython directory and then stops being
    the process that does the work. Three numbers a naive reading treats as
    one are in fact three distinct values: os.getpid() inside the child
    returns the real interpreter's PID, subprocess.Popen(venv_python).pid
    returns the STUB's PID, and the OS socket table names the real interpreter
    as the TCP port owner. Both processes report the same image name and both
    carry the supervisor's argv, so neither a name-based nor a
    command-line-based enumeration can separate them. A port-owner check that
    compares the handle Popen returned against the socket owner therefore
    mismatches on a perfectly healthy launch, and a keep-list built from
    os.getpid() alone omits the stub -- so an image-name sweep kills half of
    the supervisor's own identity.
  fix: |
    Stop treating a PID handle as a process identity. Resolve liveness and
    ownership from the RESOURCE -- the socket's owning PID, or the process
    tree walked by parent id -- never from the number Popen handed back. For
    self-identification capture the whole chain, os.getpid() AND os.getppid(),
    and pass all of it to any sweep as a variadic keep-list so a launcher stub
    cannot be stranded outside it. Make the keep-list comparison type-exact on
    both sides; a string PID never equals an integer PID and the miss is
    silent. Prefer a direct port preflight -- try to bind it -- over inferring
    readiness from a process handle. Note that the usual wrapper advice
    inverts here: 01.04 says kill the outermost wrapper and never target the
    interpreter, but a stub that fork-execs and stops owning anything is not a
    wrapper you can target, and following that advice here kills the wrong
    process or none at all.
  verify: |
    Print all three numbers on a real launch and require the check to COMPARE
    them rather than assume equality -- os.getpid(), the Popen handle's pid,
    and the socket table's owner for the bound port. Assert that the health
    check passes when the socket owner is a DESCENDANT of the launched handle
    rather than equal to it. Then run the destructive test on purpose: start
    the supervisor, run the sweep, and require the supervisor to be alive
    afterwards, because a keep-list is only proven by a sweep that would
    otherwise have killed it. Force-kill the worker tree and require no
    orphans and a recorded death -- but do not assert the worker's own exit
    hook wrote that record. A forced kill on Windows is SIGKILL-equivalent and
    atexit cannot fire, so the supervisor must synthesize the crash row from
    the MISSING artifact instead.
  tags: [subprocess, pid-identity, uv-launcher-stub, venv, port-ownership, process-tree, self-kill, keep-list, type-coercion, windows, forced-kill, xref-01.04]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-229
- id: "09.08"
  phase: 9
  area: platform-portability
  symptom: |
    A capability probe reports a graphics backend unavailable on a machine
    whose GPU is healthy, so an acceleration path stays gated off for a reason
    that is not true. Separately, an encode smoke meant to measure pipe
    overhead dies at process start complaining about its output path, on a
    machine where the equivalent command typed by hand works fine. Both read
    as environment problems, and neither shows up as a failing test.
  cause: |
    The tooling carries Linux defaults into a Windows-only target. The
    headless-context helper is asked for an EGL backend because EGL is the
    documented headless path on Linux; on Windows the platform path is WGL and
    the EGL library is usually absent, so context creation fails and the probe
    reports a MISSING capability when the real fault is a WRONG REQUEST. The
    encode sink is handed the POSIX null device as an output path, but ffmpeg
    needs the null MUXER plus the platform's own null device name, and a Unix
    path is neither. One defect wearing two faces: a platform-specific
    constant written literally into code whose target platform is fixed and is
    not the platform the constant came from. Both stay invisible to a green
    suite, because a probe that answers not-available and a smoke that is
    skipped both look exactly like passing.
  fix: |
    Branch on the platform at the point the constant is chosen, and name the
    branch that was taken in the log. Ask for the platform's native context
    path first -- default/WGL on Windows -- and attempt a foreign backend only
    where it is actually relevant. Build a null sink from the null muxer plus
    the platform's device name taken from the standard library, never a
    hardcoded POSIX path. Then make the probe REFUSE with a distinguishable
    verdict instead of falling back quietly: backend absent and backend
    requested wrongly must not produce the same answer, or the wrong one gets
    believed and a working path stays switched off. Define what passing means
    before shipping the gate -- context creation, shader compile, framebuffer
    draw, non-zero readback, clean exit -- and run it in the same detached
    process shape the server uses, because a context that succeeds in an
    interactive session can fail in a detached one.
  verify: |
    Grep the shipped tree for platform-locked literals -- POSIX device paths,
    backend name strings -- and require zero hits outside one platform
    selection helper. Assert that helper directly on both branches by FAKING
    the platform rather than by skipping: on Windows it returns the native
    context path and the Windows null device, on POSIX the other pair. Run the
    probe detached on the target machine and require a non-zero readback, then
    force the foreign backend and require a DIFFERENT, named verdict rather
    than the same not-available. Run the null-sink smoke as a marked live test
    on the target OS, because a portability defect that only a skipped test
    would have caught is not covered at all.
  tags: [platform-portability, windows, gpu-context, egl-vs-wgl, null-sink, ffmpeg, hardcoded-constant, capability-probe, silent-fallback, skipped-test]
  legacy_id: KIBITZ-20260708-scope-accel
- id: "10.08"
  phase: 10
  area: procedural
  symptom: |
    A generated character is internally incoherent: a male-coded name on a
    female slot and a female-coded name on a male slot (MALIK HIBBERT voiced
    female, PHYLLIS OKAFOR voiced male). Nothing is red. No pool-exhaustion
    warning fires, the gender quota is exactly the requested split, and the
    VOICE follows the gender correctly every time -- only the NAME is wrong,
    and only on some seeds. Because the voice table is the last thing that
    touched gender, the investigation starts there and finds it innocent.
  cause: |
    Two correlated attributes of ONE entity are produced by two independent
    generators that never reconcile. The gender plan is bound to slots
    POSITIONALLY -- a quota computed, then shuffled across slots -- while the
    name is drawn from a flat, gender-blind pool. Each generator is correct in
    isolation and each is individually testable, so every per-generator test
    passes. Consumers that read the gender attribute (voice preset, age band)
    stay coherent; the consumer that reads the NAME is a human listener, who
    has no assertion. The pool-sizing audit that covers the neighbouring defect
    passes trivially here because supply was never the problem. Generalize:
    when attribute A and attribute B of the same entity come from separate
    draws, correctness of each does not imply coherence of the pair, and no
    test scoped to one generator can see the gap.
  fix: |
    Make one attribute authoritative and reconcile the other against it after
    both generators have run and BEFORE the entity is frozen. Partition the
    existing pool by the correlated attribute rather than authoring a second
    pool -- partition the SAME names so the flat pool stays byte-identical --
    add an explicit classifier for the attribute, and guard the partition
    against drift at import time so the two views cannot diverge later. Draw
    the repair from a per-entity ISOLATED rng seeded on the run seed plus the
    entity id, never from the main sequence: a repair that consumes from the
    shared rng reshuffles every downstream draw and takes any byte-identity
    contract with it. Exempt source-owned entities -- an adaptation's Sir Toby
    Belch is not a coherence defect, and renaming him to satisfy the rule loses
    the character the lane exists to perform. Finally, sync every parallel
    table that restates the same attribute; a stale descriptor table is a
    third, silent generator of the same fact.
  verify: |
    Sweep many seeds and require zero binary-gender slots carrying a name the
    classifier tags as the other binary gender. Pin a KNOWN-INCOHERENT seed and
    require the reconciliation to repair it, and pin a KNOWN-COHERENT golden
    baseline and require the entire cast byte-identical -- that pair is what
    proves the repair fires when needed and is a true no-op when not, so it
    cannot have perturbed the rng. Assert the repaired value is still unique
    within the ensemble, and that an explicitly named or source-owned entity is
    NOT renamed. Then disable the reconciliation and require the seed sweep to
    go red; a coherence test that stays green without the reconciler is
    measuring the generators, not the pair.
  tags: [cast, coherence, correlated-attributes, independent-generators, reconciliation, positional-binding, isolated-rng, byte-identity, golden-seed, xref-10.06]
  legacy_id: BUGLOG-L11-cast-coherence
- id: "10.09"
  phase: 10
  area: rng
  symptom: |
    A text normalizer is deleted as cosmetic dead code -- nothing consumes its
    output except a hash -- and every derived image in the pipeline changes.
    Not a cache miss to be re-warmed: different pictures. A re-render of an old
    episode no longer reproduces its artwork, and one character's face drifts
    across every scene that character appears in. Nothing errors, nothing logs,
    and the diff is a single deleted string cleanup, so the change under
    suspicion is never the one that moved the output.
  cause: |
    The cache key IS the RNG seed. The dedup key and the generator seed are
    both derived from the same prompt hash, so editing the key derivation does
    not merely miss the cache -- it MOVES the artifact. Worse, derived hashes
    CHAIN: a portrait's prompt hash is folded into every scene-character seed
    derived from that portrait, so one upstream prompt containing one stray
    character shifts every still downstream of it. The blast radius is not the
    row that changed. The general shape: any function whose output reaches both
    an identity and a generator input is a member of the determinism contract,
    however cosmetic its body looks, and "no reader" is false when the reader
    is a hash.
  fix: |
    Trace a normalizer's output FORWARD to every hash before editing or
    deleting it, and where that hash reaches a seed, say so at the definition
    so the next reader does not mistake determinism for formatting. If the two
    roles must move independently, separate them: derive the dedup key from the
    full prompt digest and the seed from a stable identity a prompt edit is
    allowed to leave alone. Do NOT launder the input back into its old shape to
    hold the seed still -- that restores the defect the normalizer removal was
    fixing. When the coupling is intended, and it usually is because a changed
    prompt deserves a new picture, the correct action is to DOCUMENT rather
    than to fix: write down, where an operator will look, that a prompt edit is
    a re-render and that pre-edit episodes are no longer reproducible.
  verify: |
    Pin the SEED, not merely the key: feed a fixed prompt through the shipped
    resolver and assert the exact integer, then change the normalizer and
    require the test to go red -- a determinism contract with no pinned value
    is not a contract. Assert the CHAIN explicitly: move one upstream prompt
    and require every seed derived from it to move while every unrelated
    object's seed stays put, so the blast radius is measured rather than
    assumed. Keep a fixed-seed lane as a control and require it not to move for
    any prompt. Finally, enumerate every function whose return value reaches a
    hash on a seed path and require each to carry the contract annotation, so
    the next reader inherits the tracing instead of repeating it.
  tags: [determinism, seed-derivation, prompt-hash, cache-key, derived-identity, hash-chain, blast-radius, apparent-dead-code, reproducibility, xref-12.55]
  legacy_id: KIBITZ-20260805-prompt-hash-seed
- id: "11.57"
  phase: 11
  area: llm-output-validation
  symptom: |
    A verbatim-grounding gate kills lane after lane at the first structured
    pass -- most of a campaign's P0 deaths -- and every reading of the evidence
    blames the model: it paraphrased, it quoted the wrong region, it invented a
    span. The prompt is rewritten, the validator is loosened, the retry ladder
    gains a rung, and the death rate does not move. The corpus looks clean when
    a human opens it, because the viewer renders the markup the ingest already
    discarded.
  cause: |
    The source payload was built by stripping HTML tags with ONE substitution
    to the empty string. Inline tags must vanish with no separator so that
    H<sub>2</sub>O and anti-<em>microbial</em> keep their literal spelling --
    but BLOCK tags carry the only sentence boundary those documents have, so
    the last word of one block welds to the first word of the next:
    Polygons</p><p>NASA/JPL- becomes PolygonsNASA/JPL-, of</li><li>Engine
    becomes ofEngine, .</p><p>Let's becomes .Let's. No model can quote a token
    that exists in no language, so a CORRECT verbatim quote is rejected as
    non-literal and the lane dies with the model blamed. The standard remedy
    for this family -- whitespace-normalize both sides of the comparison -- is
    inert here: there is no whitespace to collapse, the words are genuinely
    fused, and a pipeline in full compliance with the wrap-tolerant fix still
    dies exactly this way.
  fix: |
    Audit the payload BUILDER's block-boundary join before touching the model,
    the prompt, or the validator. Tag removal is two rules and never one: an
    explicit allowlist of block and break tags substitutes a SEPARATOR, and
    every remaining tag is removed without one. Then treat the change as what
    it is -- a coordinate migration. The normalized text is the sole coordinate
    system the source digest pins and that every accepted span offset is
    measured in, so widening what the stripper touches silently re-indexes
    already-accepted artifacts; keep the allowlist narrow, apply it upstream of
    the digest, and scope the migration to future payloads rather than
    rewriting frozen ones. Leave entity spellings to the downstream normalizer
    so exactly one component owns what a space is. Cross-reference the
    wrap-tolerant normalization entry in both directions: whitespace where
    there should be none and none where there should be whitespace are one
    subject, and a reader who has met only one polarity will re-derive the
    other the expensive way.
  verify: |
    Build the regression from the REAL payloads that killed the campaign, not
    from synthetic markup. Require every block boundary in them to survive as a
    separator AND require the inline counterfactuals to survive without one --
    H<sub>2</sub>O must come out H2O, never H 2 O. Assert the old catch-all
    substitution as an explicit counterfactual so the test names the defect it
    prevents. Prove the production call site reaches the new helper, then
    mutate that call site back to the old strip and require the wiring test to
    go red. Pin digest stability last: normalization must run BEFORE the
    digest, and two payloads differing only in what you now normalize must hash
    exactly as the migration intends.
  tags: [llm-output, text-ingest, html-stripping, block-boundary, separator, verbatim-grounding, source-payload, coordinate-system, digest-stability, counterfactual, xref-11.26]
  legacy_id: HANDOFF-L1580
- id: "11.58"
  phase: 11
  area: llm-output
  symptom: |
    Composed text comes back with a term doubled or compounded -- "hand
    grenade" arrives as "banana banana bomb" -- from a substitution table whose
    every row is correct when read alone and correct when tested alone. The
    table is already ordered longest-first and every pattern is word-boundary
    anchored, so the two defenses everyone reaches for are both in place and
    neither one is the defense this needs. Because the corruption only appears
    for inputs that contain one particular seeding term, a sample corpus can
    pass indefinitely while the table is broken by construction.
  cause: |
    The applier walks the rows IN ORDER and reassigns the working string on
    every row, so each row after the first reads text that earlier rows already
    rewrote. That makes the table a rewrite SYSTEM, not a lookup -- its input
    alphabet includes its own output alphabet -- and any row whose REPLACEMENT
    contains a later row's SOURCE will fire on the table's own product. In the
    incident, ("hand grenade", "banana bomb") sat above ("bomb", "banana
    bomb"), and the later row matched the earlier row's output. Longest-first
    ordering does not help, because it defends source-against-source overlap
    and says nothing about a source appearing inside a replacement.
    Word-boundary anchoring does not help either, because the replacement
    introduces a boundary-complete occurrence of the term. The whole cascade
    completes inside ONE application, so reasoning that starts from "we only
    call this once" concludes wrongly that it is safe.
  fix: |
    Stop feeding the table its own output. Preferably apply it as a SINGLE
    non-overlapping sweep over the ORIGINAL text -- build one alternation of
    every source, longest-first, and pass one replacer function to a single
    substitution call, so the scan advances past each replacement and no output
    is ever re-read. That converts the table from a rewrite system back into a
    lookup, and ordering then resolves ambiguity only, never cascade. If
    sequential application must stay, make CLOSURE an explicit written contract
    of the table -- no replacement string may match any row's source under the
    applier's own matcher -- and enforce it as a standing test, because closure
    is an obligation every future row has to re-earn while a single sweep is
    structural and earns it once. Either way, state the invariant next to the
    table so the next author adding a row knows what they are promising.
  verify: |
    Test the TABLE, not a corpus. For every ordered pair of rows, run row j's
    exact production matcher -- same escaping, same boundaries, same case
    folding, built by the same code path the applier uses -- against row i's
    replacement string and require no match, so the test can never pass on a
    stricter matcher than the one that ships. Then assert single-application
    correctness on the known cascade seeds by exact equality, not containment:
    "hand grenade" must yield exactly "banana bomb". Assert the fixed point is
    reached in one pass by feeding every source AND every replacement back
    through and requiring apply(apply(x)) to equal apply(x). Finally add a row
    that deliberately violates closure and require the table test to go red;
    a closure test that stays green when closure is broken is not a test.
  tags: [llm-output, rewrite-table, ordered-substitution, closure-under-own-output, single-pass-sweep, idempotence, word-boundary, table-contract, property-test, mutation-check]
  legacy_id: KIBITZ-20260806-banana-route
- id: "11.59"
  phase: 11
  area: llm-output-validation
  symptom: |
    One beat silently produces no image. Nothing raised, nothing crashed, the
    episode completed -- there is simply a hole where a still should be. The
    log shows a path-detection guard fired and skip evidence was recorded, and
    the prompt named in that evidence is ordinary prose: a character wearing "a
    black/white striped scarf", a scene at "the corner of 5th/Main". The same
    prompt is clean on a POSIX box, so the defect is invisible to anyone not
    running the shipping platform.
  cause: |
    The "is this a path?" test was assembled out of characters that are LEGAL
    INSIDE CONTENT -- os.sep in the string, or os.altsep in the string, or an
    image extension at the end. On Windows os.altsep IS the forward slash, so
    that predicate rejects any prose containing a slash: a costume description,
    an intersection, a fraction, an "and/or". The guard's actual job is a
    socket-crossing check, and a crossed socket delivers a value that IS a
    path, never prose that merely CONTAINS one, so substring containment was
    the wrong shape of question from the start -- it asks whether the text
    mentions a separator when the claim under test is whether the whole value
    is a path. The cost was total rather than cosmetic because the guard's
    disposition was skip-and-continue: a false positive spent a render and left
    a missing artifact as its only symptom. Producers then grew local
    sanitizers to launder slashes out of prose, which is the worse repair --
    laundering a REAL path converts the guard's one true positive from a loud
    refusal into a quiet garbled render.
  fix: |
    Never build a content-versus-path discriminator from characters that are
    legal inside content. Discriminate on the SHAPE OF THE WHOLE VALUE:
    anchored, ordered most specific first -- drive root, UNC root, POSIX root,
    file URI, explicit relative, and a whitespace-free string ending in an
    image extension -- and rule that prose containing a separator is CLEAN.
    Note the same rule settles the extension case: "host.png" is a filename,
    "a portrait of the radio host.png" is a sentence, and whitespace is what
    tells them apart. Delete the producer-side sanitizers in the same change
    rather than adding another; laundering the input to satisfy a bad guard
    destroys the only true positive the guard exists for. Make the rejection
    observable: a guard that fires must raise, or record a named structured
    verdict carrying the arm, the offending token, its index, an excerpt and
    the canonical content hash that joins the ledger row -- never a bare
    continue. And treat platform constants that are None on one OS and
    meaningful on another as platform FACTS to be read and pinned, not as
    harmless spellings.
  verify: |
    Pin the platform fact first -- assert that on this OS os.altsep is the
    forward slash -- so the test says out loud why it exists. Then feed the
    incident prose through and require BOTH that the guard returns clean and
    that the dispatcher actually mints the still; asserting only that the
    predicate returned None proves nothing about the artifact. Sweep every arm
    with a genuine path and require the exact arm name back, so widening for
    prose did not also reopen the socket-crossing hole: a drive-rooted path, a
    UNC root, a POSIX root, a file URI, an explicit relative, and a bare
    filename must each classify, while a sentence ending in an image extension
    must not. Force one true positive and require the recorded evidence to
    carry the arm and the joining hash, and require the render count to drop by
    exactly one with a named reason rather than silently. Then restore the
    substring predicate and require the prose case to go red.
  tags: [llm-output, path-guard, os-altsep, windows, whole-string-classification, discriminator, false-positive, silent-skip, missing-artifact, observable-refusal, socket-crossing, mutation-check]
  legacy_id: KIBITZ-20260805-bug-sprint-path-guard
- id: "11.60"
  phase: 11
  area: source-selection
  symptom: |
    Sibling outputs derived from a single call disagree with each other. An
    adapted scene quoted one identical mid-passage on every beat, so no beat
    ever saw the opening or the ending; in a neighbouring size band some beats
    kept their own region while their siblings collapsed to the whole body, so
    starts were unsorted and duplicated and the arc could run BACKWARDS -- beat
    6 of 7 quoting the opening while beat 3 quoted later material. Every window
    is legal read alone, so no per-item assertion can see it; only the family
    is wrong. The band is narrow and perfectly deterministic -- roughly 741
    (length, beat-count) pairs in ordinary short-story territory -- so it
    reproduces every time and no test happened to land inside it.
  cause: |
    The degradation decision -- "this work is too short to slice, fall back to
    the whole body" -- was evaluated PER KEY, inside the loop, against that
    key's own bucket. Integer division makes bucket sizes differ by one
    character, so when total divided by key count sits near the floor some
    buckets clear it and their siblings do not, and the threshold flips item by
    item instead of once for the family. The invariants that matter here --
    disjointness, sorted starts, monotonic progression -- are properties of the
    SET, and no member can hold them; nothing in the loop was computing the set
    at all. A second failure hid inside the same fallback: it offered only two
    cases, slice or show-everything, so a work too short for floor-sized
    disjoint regions but too long to show whole fell through to a single shared
    window for every key. A sibling module already decided this once up front;
    this one had diverged from that pattern and reintroduced the bug it avoids.
  fix: |
    Decide a degradation ONCE, before the loop, from family-level inputs --
    total length, key count, floor -- and pass the decision in. The loop may
    only apply it, never re-derive it. Then fill the case the two-way fallback
    left open: a work too short to slice but too long to show whole gets
    SLIDING overlapping windows, offset across the room the budget leaves, so
    progression survives even where disjointness cannot. Name the collective
    invariants explicitly and assert them on the RETURNED FAMILY rather than on
    its members. Do not mistake sharing for the defect: when the whole work
    genuinely fits one window, every key seeing all of it is correct -- a
    single short scene is the material for every beat in it. The defect is
    siblings DISAGREEING. When a neighbouring module already makes the same
    call once up front, adopt its shape instead of writing a second one.
  verify: |
    Sweep the integer-division band exhaustively instead of sampling it: for
    every (total length, key count) pair straddling the floor -- the incident's
    band was about 741 of them -- build the family and assert the collective
    invariants every time, that starts are strictly increasing, that no two
    keys share a start, that every window lies inside the body, and that either
    all keys collapsed or none did. Assert STATICALLY that the decision is
    computed once above the loop and appears exactly once in the shipped
    source, so a later edit cannot quietly move it back inside. Regress the
    sliding case on the real incident shape -- 14 beats over an 1,116-character
    scene must yield 14 DISTINCT windows, the first touching the opening and
    the last touching the ending -- and require the distinct-window count to
    equal the key count. Then move the decision back inside the loop and
    require the band sweep to go red.
  tags: [derived-slices, per-item-vs-collective, degradation-threshold, integer-division, off-by-one, boundary-band, collective-invariant, monotonic-progression, sibling-divergence, source-window, exhaustive-sweep, static-assertion, mutation-check]
  legacy_id: GITFIX-cdf8bffb
- id: "12.75"
  phase: 12
  area: rewrite-dropped-quality-input
  symptom: |
    A generation subsystem is rewritten rather than edited. Renders still
    succeed, the suite stays green, no reference dangles, no import breaks --
    and the operator says the output looks flatter than a build from weeks ago,
    naming a specific saved artifact as the reference. The obvious knobs are
    checked first and they all match: same checkpoint, same steps, same cfg,
    same sampler. Because every parameter that LOOKS like a parameter agrees,
    the investigation concludes nothing changed and starts blaming the model,
    the prompt text, or the operator's memory.
  cause: |
    A rewrite is an insertion plus a deletion, not an edit, so there is no
    line-level diff between the old behaviour and the new one to read. The
    inputs that survive a rewrite are the ones that look like inputs -- a
    widget, a named argument, a config key. The quality-BEARING inputs do not
    look like inputs, so they are the ones that vanish: a graph node that was
    never a workflow widget and existed only as a wire inside the internally
    built graph (here a guidance node at 3.5 between the positive encode and
    the sampler; grep for guidance in the new engine returns nothing at all);
    prompt suffixes that lived as module-level string constants appended to
    every prompt; and a fixed seed pinning one deterministic asset. Nothing
    references them any more, so every dead-reference scan, cross-import check
    and AST parse stays green -- the absence is invisible precisely BECAUSE it
    is complete. The pipeline keeps producing output, so nothing goes red.
  fix: |
    Before deleting the old implementation, enumerate its EFFECTIVE inputs
    rather than its declared parameters. Walk the graph it constructs node by
    node and record every node that carries a value; record every module-level
    string constant that is appended or prepended to a prompt; record every
    literal seed. Put that inventory in the change itself and tick each item
    against the replacement. Treat an input with no widget as HIGHER risk than
    one with a widget, because nothing in the UI or the JSON will ever remind
    you it existed. Port each recovered value as a named constant with an env
    override rather than inlining it, so a future disappearance is greppable.
    Keep the old implementation's last commit reachable: the saved
    widgets_values in a HISTORICAL commit of the workflow JSON may be the only
    surviving record of values that never lived in code at all.
  verify: |
    Diff INVENTORIES, not files. Assert statically over the graph the new
    engine builds that each recovered lever is present and positioned -- the
    guidance node exists, sits between the positive conditioning and the
    sampler, and the sampler reads the GUIDED conditioning rather than the raw
    encode. Assert each restored prompt suffix appears in the composed prompt,
    in the correct layer order, and ONLY on the paths meant to carry it;
    scoping is part of the contract, and a tail appended everywhere is a
    different regression wearing the same fix. Pin the deterministic seed by
    value and prove its env override. Then A/B against a PRESERVED reference
    artifact from the good build, because this class has no red test to offer:
    when the pipeline strips its own output metadata, the archived reference
    plus the source is the entire ground truth. Archive that reference BEFORE
    the rewrite, not after you need it.
  tags: [subsystem-rewrite, silent-input-loss, no-dangling-reference, graph-node-not-widget, prompt-suffix-constant, deterministic-seed, look-regression, reference-artifact-ab, historical-widgets-values]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-411
- id: "12.76"
  phase: 12
  area: cleanbreak-default-parity
  symptom: |
    A cleanbreak removes a legacy path and a newer engine for the same model
    family takes over. Everything still runs, the suite is green, and the
    workflow JSON is byte-identical before and after. Weeks later the operator
    says the output was better in an earlier era and names two reference clips
    from that era. There is no diff to point at: the JSON did not change and
    the deleted module is gone from the tree, so the natural first move --
    diff the workflow -- returns nothing, and the investigation stalls on the
    belief that no configuration changed.
  cause: |
    The tuning did not live where tuning is normally looked for. The saved
    workflow widgets carried only the mundane values (a seed, an encode
    method, a duration cap) while the sampler, the step count, the CFG, the
    conditioning strength, the render dimensions and the loop treatment were
    all CODE defaults inside the deleted module. The replacement is a
    different implementation of the same model family, so it ships its own
    defaults -- each individually defensible, all of them different. A
    cleanbreak therefore rewrites BEHAVIOUR without rewriting CONFIGURATION,
    and every habitual diff target (workflow JSON, requirements, node
    registrations) stays clean. The drift compounds quietly: one replacement
    default was left at a placeholder with a code comment admitting the
    correct value and promising a sweep that never ran.
  fix: |
    Capture the doomed component's effective defaults as a golden recipe
    BEFORE deleting it -- read them out of the source at the last commit that
    still has it, and write them into the change as data, one named constant
    per value, not as prose in a commit message. Make parity against that
    recipe an explicit acceptance criterion of the cleanbreak, and where a
    value must legitimately differ, say in the code WHY and against what
    measurement. Never land a default as a placeholder with a comment
    promising to fix it later; a comment is not a task and outlives the person
    who wrote it. Record the CONDITIONS a value was measured under alongside
    the value, because a conditioning strength tuned at one canvas is a
    different strength at another, and a recipe copied without its conditions
    reproduces the number while losing the result.
  verify: |
    Assert each recipe value in the replacement by name against the captured
    golden constant, in a test that names the deleted implementation and the
    commit the value was read from, so provenance travels with the assertion.
    Make every knob env-overridable and exercise the override path, so an
    operator can A/B a candidate without a code change. Then require the human
    comparison the numbers cannot substitute for: re-render the same asset and
    put it beside a preserved artifact from the good era. Finally, mutate one
    recipe value and require the parity test to go red -- a parity test that
    merely reads the new engine's own defaults and compares them to themselves
    is asserting nothing, and will stay green through the next regression.
  tags: [cleanbreak, recipe-in-code, default-parity, deleted-implementation, golden-recipe, unchanged-json-unchanged-behaviour-fallacy, placeholder-default, measurement-conditions, look-regression]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-412
- id: "12.77"
  phase: 12
  area: environment
  symptom: |
    A tooling or test step that writes a scratch file works for months, then
    starts failing on one machine only. The traceback is OSError Errno 22,
    Invalid argument, raised by open() itself -- before a single byte is
    written, and with mode wb. Cleanup fails too: unlink raises
    PermissionError saying the file is in use by another process. Re-running
    sometimes succeeds, so the failure reads as flakiness and invites a retry
    loop or a skip marker instead of a diagnosis.
  cause: |
    The scratch file was written inside a cloud-synced tree (OneDrive,
    Dropbox, Google Drive) or another externally watched directory -- a backup
    agent, a search indexer, an antivirus scanner. Size is the trigger: a
    multi-megabyte file takes long enough to upload that the sync client still
    holds a handle when the next run re-opens the path. On Windows, opening an
    existing file for truncating write must first shrink it, and that
    operation is refused while another process holds the file -- so the error
    surfaces at OPEN, which is why write-time error handling never sees it and
    why switching to wb changes nothing. The errno is misleading: the argument
    is fine, the file is busy. Because the outcome depends on sync timing, the
    failure is intermittent, machine-specific, and absent from CI.
  fix: |
    Move the scratch file out of the observed tree. Write large or frequently
    rewritten temporaries to the OS temp directory, and expose the location as
    an env-overridable setting so the convenient in-repo default survives for
    manual use while automation points somewhere unsynced. Where a file
    genuinely must live in a synced tree, never truncate in place -- write a
    fresh sibling and rename it over the target, because rename is atomic and
    does not require shrinking a held file. Wrap open and unlink on such paths
    in a bounded retry with backoff and treat OSError and PermissionError as
    one transient class. Do not call a sync-client exclusion the fix: an
    exclusion is host configuration that no clone and no new machine inherits.
  verify: |
    Assert the PATH, not the outcome. Take the temp location the tooling
    actually resolves and require it to lie under the OS temp directory and
    outside every synced or watched root, and prove the env override is
    honoured rather than trusting the default to be read. Prove the
    write-then-rename path by holding an open handle on the target and
    requiring the write to still succeed. Prove the retry by making the first
    open fail and requiring a second attempt, and require it to give up loudly
    with the real path in the message rather than spinning. Then run the tool
    twice back to back with a payload large enough to still be syncing on the
    second pass -- a small-file test passes cleanly against a broken
    implementation, which is how this shipped in the first place.
  tags: [cloud-sync, onedrive, windows, file-lock, truncating-open, errno-22, permission-error, temp-file-placement, write-then-rename, bounded-retry, machine-specific-flake]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-293
- id: "12.78"
  phase: 12
  area: testing
  symptom: |
    A cluster of tests fails in the full suite and every one of them passes
    when its own file is run alone. The error names a missing attribute on a
    host module that none of the failing tests mentions -- module folder_paths
    has no attribute get_filename_list -- raised from a node the failing file
    never imports. Run the failing file first and the failures move somewhere
    else. The suite looks nondeterministic, so the reflex is to mark the tests
    flaky, pin an order, or rerun until green, and the real defect survives
    every one of those remedies.
  cause: |
    sys.modules is one process-global namespace shared by every test in the
    run. Several test modules each install their own stand-in for a host
    module at IMPORT time, each guarded by an if-not-already-present check, so
    whichever file pytest collects first wins and the rest become no-ops. Each
    stub carries only the attributes its own author needed, so the winner is
    PARTIAL -- and a partial stub is worse than no stub. With nothing
    installed, the module under test fails to import, the code that enumerates
    it silently drops it from the set under test, and nothing runs and nothing
    fails. With a partial stub the import SUCCEEDS, execution reaches the
    attribute nobody supplied, and the failure lands in an unrelated test that
    merely happened to be collected later. Collection order is a function of
    file names and selection, which is why the symptom moves whenever the
    suite changes shape.
  fix: |
    Give the process-global namespace exactly one owner. Install a single stub
    in conftest, before any test module is collected, and make it carry the
    COMPLETE surface the code under test touches -- not the subset the first
    author happened to need. Leave the per-module guards in place as harmless
    no-ops so a file can still be run standalone, but stop treating them as
    the installation point; when the stub must grow, grow the canonical one.
    The same single-owner rule governs every other process-global a test can
    mutate: environment variables, working directory, warning filters,
    logging configuration, and any registry or mapping dictionary the host
    exposes. One owner, installed once, torn down deterministically.
  verify: |
    Prove ownership, not luck. Assert that the canonical stub exposes every
    attribute the production import path calls, enumerated FROM THE SOURCE
    rather than from memory, so a new call site fails one focused test instead
    of nine scattered ones. Assert that the module under test imports
    successfully under that stub, and that the enumerator actually lists it,
    because a silently dropped module is the exact state this defect hides
    behind. Then attack the order: run the suite with collection reversed and
    with a shuffled seed and require the same result, since green in isolation
    is not green. Finally, remove one attribute from the canonical stub and
    require the suite to go red; if everything stays green, the stub you are
    maintaining is not the stub the tests are using.
  tags: [sys-modules-pollution, process-global-state, partial-stub, conftest-ownership, order-dependent, green-in-isolation, silent-skip, collection-order, test-infra]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-251
- id: "12.79"
  phase: 12
  area: gate-input-population
  symptom: |
    An operator adds a new module or test file, runs the pre-commit gate --
    forbidden-identifier sweep, lint, license scan -- and gets green. The
    commit then turns HEAD red, naming a violation in a file that had been on
    disk and passing for days, on a commit that changed nothing about the gate
    or its rules. Because the failure surfaces at commit time, the
    investigation blames the commit's other content or the CI runner rather
    than the gate, and the same file will pass green again the moment it is
    untracked. This one cost two separate red HEADs, the second diagnosed from
    scratch by someone who then found it already written down.
  cause: |
    The gate builds its input from a version-control delta -- git diff against
    a baseline -- and a diff covers TRACKED files only. An untracked file has
    no index entry, emits no hunks, and contributes zero lines to the sweep,
    so the gate is green for exactly as long as the offending code stays
    untracked. Git add is what makes the file visible, which is why the first
    commit both admits the file and trips the gate, pointing the investigation
    at the wrong change. The general defect is that the gate's input
    population is narrower than the population the commit will carry. Any
    selector derived from VCS state -- diff, index, changed-files list,
    files-touched-in-this-PR -- is a different set from the shipped source
    tree, and the gap is silent: a gate that swept nothing reports the same
    green as a gate that swept everything and found nothing.
  fix: |
    Make the gate walk the set the commit will carry -- tracked plus
    untracked-and-not-ignored -- rather than a VCS delta. Where the checker's
    parser genuinely needs diff shape, synthesize it: enumerate untracked
    files with git ls-files --others --exclude-standard and render each as an
    added-file hunk. Emit the COMPLETE hunk shape, header included; a plus
    line outside any hunk header is silently dropped by the diff parser, which
    yields input that looks perfectly correct and sweeps nothing. The
    --exclude-standard flag honours .gitignore, so scratch trees stay out
    without a second exclusion list. Scope the addition repo-wide rather than
    to a couple of directories: an untracked listing never returns the
    untouched tracked tree, so narrowing it buys no speed and leaves runtime
    code under config, scripts and the repo root blind. Fail loud -- a git,
    read or decode error inside the input builder must raise, never return an
    empty string, because silently sweeping nothing is the exact failure being
    closed.
  verify: |
    Plant a file containing a known-forbidden identifier, leave it UNTRACKED,
    and require the gate to go red; remove it and require green. That single
    test is what distinguishes a gate that sees the working tree from one that
    sees only the index, and a suite exercising tracked files alone cannot
    make the distinction at all. Assert the collected input is non-empty and
    carries a well-formed hunk header for every synthesized file, then assert
    the exact hit count for the planted file rather than merely that it is
    non-zero, so a parser that never enters a hunk fails instead of passing
    quietly. Assert an ignored path is absent from the collected set, and that
    a failing git invocation raises rather than yielding an empty sweep.
  tags: [false-green, gate-scope, untracked-files, git-diff, input-population, pre-commit, fail-loud, static-analysis, all-route]
  legacy_id: HANDOFF-L2291
- id: "12.80"
  phase: 12
  area: concurrency
  symptom: |
    Two agent windows work the same checkout at the same time. One commits a
    documentation change; the push reports success in that window, and origin
    is left broken -- carrying deletions the OTHER window had staged but not
    finished, or an untracked scratch file the neighbour had not decided to
    keep. Here the swept deletions removed an adapter module while the
    registry it fed kept declaring five engines, so the remote roster audit
    read missing equals four against a floor of thirty, and origin stayed red
    about ninety minutes. The committing window's own diff review looked
    clean, because it reviewed its own edits rather than the index.
  cause: |
    The git index is process-global state belonging to the working tree, not
    to a session. Anything one window stages -- git rm, git add -A on a
    directory, any early git add -- sits in the same index every other window
    commits from, and a commit is defined by the index, so the first window to
    commit carries away whoever else's staged work is sitting there. An -A add
    additionally sweeps the neighbour's UNTRACKED files under that path.
    Neither window sees an error: staging takes no lock and raises no
    advisory. The result is an internally inconsistent commit -- half of a
    two-file change, a deletion without its registry edit -- which is
    syntactically valid, passes local review, and lands on the REMOTE past the
    point where the author would look. The window that caused it has already
    reported success and moved on.
  fix: |
    On a shared tree, treat staging as part of the commit rather than a
    preparatory step: stage NOTHING until your own commit moment, and stage by
    explicit pathspec -- name the files -- never git add -A or git add dot,
    and never an early git rm held across other work. If deletions must be
    planned ahead, record the paths in a note and run the removal inside the
    same minute as the commit. Read git status --short as a whole immediately
    before committing and treat any staged entry you did not just create as
    another actor's work: stop and reconcile instead of committing over it.
    Where two agents genuinely must write at once, give each its own worktree
    so the indexes are physically separate and the shared-state question
    disappears.
  verify: |
    Assert the commit's file list equals the pathspec you intended, read from
    the commit itself rather than from memory of the edit: git show --stat
    HEAD must contain exactly your files and nothing else, and in particular
    no deletions you did not author. Then verify the PUSHED state rather than
    the local one -- fetch, confirm origin HEAD matches local HEAD, and run
    the repo's own cross-file consistency checks against the fetched tree:
    registry roster count, import resolution, AST parse. A mixed commit is
    valid Python and only a cross-file invariant will name it. Keep those
    floors as real numbers, so a registry that quietly loses members fails an
    assertion instead of degrading into a smaller but still plausible roster.
  tags: [concurrency, shared-index, multi-window, git-add-all, pathspec, staged-state, cross-file-invariant, remote-verification, all-route]
  legacy_id: HANDOFF-L206
- id: "12.81"
  phase: 12
  area: module-reload-class-identity
  symptom: |
    A test passes eleven of eleven alone and fails when it runs right after a
    sibling test file. Same code, same machine, same seed -- the verdict depends
    on collection order. The failure looks impossible: an exception escapes a
    handler that plainly names its class, or an isinstance check returns False
    against an object whose repr matches exactly. Because a targeted rerun of
    the failing file is green, the red line gets re-diagnosed as a flake by
    every person who meets it, and full-suite ordering can hide it for weeks
    between rediscoveries.
  cause: |
    An earlier test called importlib.reload on a shared module. Reload
    re-executes the module body and REBINDS its names to NEW objects, so the
    reloaded class is a different object from the same-named class imported
    before it. Every symbol captured earlier still points at the old object:
    except clauses the sibling file bound at COLLECTION time, isinstance checks,
    registry keys, sentinel classes, cached type maps. After the reload the
    module raises instances of the new class and the old handler no longer
    matches, so the exception sails straight past a correct-looking except.
    Nothing is logged, and no fixture can undo it -- teardown cannot un-rebind a
    class object that other modules already hold. The general shape is
    in-process module surgery that mutates state outliving the test which
    performed it, where the damage is visible under one file ordering only.
  fix: |
    Do not reload modules in-process. When the property under test genuinely
    requires a fresh import -- proving an import is lazy, proving no file is
    read at import time -- run it in a SUBPROCESS, so whatever it does to class
    objects cannot reach the parent interpreter. Pass the repo root as argv
    rather than interpolating it into a -c source string, because a Windows path
    is full of backslashes and every one of them would need escaping. Install
    the probe's guard inside the child: pytest monkeypatch is in-process and
    does not cross the boundary. Scope that guard to the repo under test -- a
    blanket ban on Path.read_text trips third-party import machinery reading its
    own packaging metadata, which tests the interpreter rather than your module,
    and only shows up in a fresh child because the parent had already
    initialised it. If a reload is truly unavoidable, re-import every captured
    symbol afterwards and accept that symbols other modules already bound are
    beyond your reach.
  verify: |
    Assert class identity across the test itself: capture the class before, and
    afterwards assert the module attribute IS that same object. That is the real
    invariant, and it fails on the reload directly instead of waiting for a
    sibling file to trip over it. Then run the suite in BOTH file orders and
    require both green -- one order passing is precisely the evidence that
    misled the previous investigation; here one order was a failure and the
    other was eleven of eleven, and both orders became twenty-eight of
    twenty-eight once the reload left the process. Assert the subprocess
    contract explicitly: exit code zero AND a marker string in its stdout, so an
    early exit cannot pass as success, under a timeout whose failure message
    carries the child's stdout and stderr. Treat any test that passes alone and
    fails adjacent to a sibling as module-state contamination and go find the
    writer, never as a flake to retry.
  tags: [test-isolation, importlib-reload, class-identity, order-dependent, module-state, subprocess-isolation, except-clause-mismatch, false-flake, all-route]
  legacy_id: HANDOFF-L1070
- id: "12.82"
  phase: 12
  area: hygiene
  symptom: |
    Every git status reports thousands of untracked files -- 6,308 files and 272
    MB here -- so nobody reads it any more. Genuine untracked work hides in the
    noise, the post-push lockstep verification that depends on a clean status
    becomes useless, and an agent reading status output either truncates it or
    reasons over a sample. The .gitignore that was supposed to cover the scratch
    directory contains a rule that LOOKS like it covers it, and reviewers have
    read past that rule repeatedly without seeing anything wrong.
  cause: |
    The ignore pattern is a near-miss on the real path. A rule of the form
    /tmp_* anchors at the repo root and matches the PREFIX tmp_, so it caught
    the 93 MB tmp_audit_results.json at the root and never matched the tmp/
    DIRECTORY sitting beside it, which has no underscore. Git reports nothing:
    a pattern with zero effect and a pattern doing its job are indistinguishable
    from the file, and the author's evidence that it works is usually the single
    path they happened to test. The general class is that an exclusion rule is a
    silent contract whose correctness cannot be inferred by reading it -- a
    leading slash anchors to the repo root, a TRAILING slash is what restricts a
    rule to directories, a star does not cross a path separator, and a prefix
    pattern is not a directory pattern. The same file carried two earlier
    near-misses of this same class, so it recurs rather than being a one-off.
  fix: |
    Prove every exclusion rule fires against a real path instead of trusting the
    glob. Add the directory form explicitly -- /tmp/ alongside /tmp_* -- and
    leave a comment naming which path the old rule missed and why, so the next
    author does not have to re-derive it. Prefer one rule per intent over a
    clever pattern meant to cover both a file family and a directory at once;
    the clever pattern is what fails silently. Remember that an ignore rule does
    not affect TRACKED files: anything already committed under the newly ignored
    directory keeps reporting its modifications exactly as before, which is
    correct behaviour and must not be "repaired" by removing those files from
    the index in the same change.
  verify: |
    Run git check-ignore -v on a concrete path inside the directory and require
    it to print the rule and the line number that matched. A rule matching
    nothing prints nothing and exits non-zero, which is the falsifiable signal;
    do this once per directory the rule claims to cover. Then measure the
    population before and after: count entries from git status --porcelain and
    require the untracked count to fall to a small reviewable number -- 6,308
    down to ten here -- while requiring the count of TRACKED modifications to be
    unchanged, which proves the rule removed noise rather than history. Keep the
    untracked-count check in the hygiene suite with a real ceiling, so the next
    scratch directory is caught by a number instead of by somebody's patience.
  tags: [hygiene, gitignore, exclusion-pattern, silent-no-match, git-check-ignore, untracked-noise, tracked-vs-ignored, near-miss-glob, all-route]
  legacy_id: KIBITZ-20260805-gitignore-tmp-directory-miss
- id: "12.83"
  phase: 12
  area: unescaped-content-in-the-evidence-stream
  symptom: |
    The log reads as well-formed and tells a lie. A record nobody wrote appears
    in it -- a plausible WARNING or SKIP line, correctly shaped, at column zero
    -- or a guard's evidence line arrives split across several records, so the
    field a reader greps for lands alone with no context and the surrounding
    detail looks like it belongs to a different event. Line counts drift, a
    log-scraping regression asserts against a record the writer never emitted,
    and the operator reconstructs a sequence of events that did not happen.
    Nothing raises. The stream is syntactically perfect.
  cause: |
    A free-form value -- an LLM-authored prompt, an episode title, an exception
    message, a filename -- is interpolated into a line-delimited record without
    escaping. The stream's record separator is the newline, and the content is
    permitted to contain newlines, so the CONTENT CAN EMIT THE SEPARATOR.
    Everything after an embedded newline becomes, to every reader of that
    stream -- grep, the harness, the operator's eye -- a record of equal
    standing to the ones the program wrote. Carriage returns, ANSI escapes and
    the format's own field delimiter do the same one level down, forging FIELDS
    instead of records. The bug is invisible in normal operation because
    well-behaved content carries no separator; it surfaces exactly when content
    is hostile or merely unlucky, which for model-authored text is routine.
    Truncation is not a mitigation -- an excerpt like prompt[:200] changes WHICH
    forged text lands in the log, not whether it can, and it hides a late match
    entirely.
  fix: |
    Escape on the WRITER side, at the instant the value enters the stream, never
    on the reader side -- a reader cannot un-forge a record it has already been
    handed as fact. In Python that is repr: interpolate value!r so newlines,
    carriage returns, tabs and control characters become two-character sequences
    that cannot terminate a record, and keep the quotes it emits, because they
    also mark where the untrusted span begins and ends. Escape EVERY free-form
    field, including the ones that cannot contain a newline -- exception text,
    paths, model ids -- since the value that surprises you is the one nobody
    audited. When a reader needs the whole value, do not widen the excerpt:
    write it to a side file and log the path plus a hash. Center any excerpt on
    the index that matters rather than on offset zero, and name which arm
    matched, so the line is diagnostic as well as safe.
  verify: |
    Push a value carrying a newline, a CRLF, a tab and a convincing fake record
    prefix through the real logging call, then COUNT RECORDS in the emitted
    stream and require exactly one -- counting characters proves nothing here.
    Assert the fake prefix appears inside the escaped span, not at column zero
    of a new line. Feed the same stream to the parser or grep the harness
    actually uses and require it to find one record with the injected text in
    the field it belongs to. Keep a control case of ordinary content and assert
    escaping left it readable, so the remedy cannot pass by making every line
    useless.
  tags: [logging, log-record-forgery, untrusted-content, repr-escape, record-separator, writer-side-escaping, evidence-stream, llm-authored-text, telemetry]
  legacy_id: KIBITZ-20260804-log-record-forgery
- id: "12.84"
  phase: 12
  area: powershell
  symptom: |
    A CLI run launched from PowerShell dies before it does any work, with
    "error: unrecognized arguments:" followed by a fragment the operator
    recognizes -- the tail of an episode title and the whole prompt behind it.
    The identical command with a different title runs fine, so the failure looks
    like a flake. Then the sibling invocation that DID start is found carrying
    the wreckage as canonical state -- episode_title='SPAN_TE_PI;LLA28' on the
    ledger writer's start line, and the same mangled fragment slugged into the
    run's own artifact filenames. A quoting accident has become permanent data
    that no later run will question.
  cause: |
    A free-text value was placed on a command line. PowerShell and cmd parse the
    line BEFORE the process exists: a semicolon ends a statement, ampersand and
    pipe chain, dollar expands, backtick escapes, and unquoted whitespace splits
    one intended argument into several. By the time argparse sees argv the
    damage is done and is not recoverable -- argparse can only report the tail
    it did not recognize, which is why the error names the Python script and
    sends the investigation to the parser instead of the launcher. Two harms
    compound. The split kills one run loudly; where a sibling invocation happens
    to survive it, the truncated or metacharacter-bearing fragment is accepted
    as the real value and written into filenames, ledger keys and telemetry, so
    a transient shell event turns into durable corruption. Title, premise and
    prompt are the three fields most likely to carry a separator, because they
    are the three authored by a human or a model rather than by the program.
  fix: |
    Stop putting free text on a command line. Pass variable-length,
    human-or-model-authored values through a file, stdin, or an environment
    variable and keep only fixed program-authored tokens in the argument vector
    -- --brief-json C:\runs\brief.json cannot be split by anything. Where an
    argument genuinely must be passed, BUILD THE INVOCATION AS AN ARRAY and hand
    it to the process without a shell (subprocess with a list and shell=False;
    from PowerShell the call operator with an array, or --% to stop parsing), so
    no parser gets a second look at the string. Never assemble a command by
    string concatenation, and never rely on the caller to have quoted correctly.
    Then close the second harm separately from the first: validate the value at
    the receiving boundary and refuse or normalize shell metacharacters in any
    field destined for a filename, a ledger key or a persisted title, so a
    mangled value fails loudly on arrival instead of being adopted as truth.
  verify: |
    Launch through the real launcher with a value containing a space, a
    semicolon, an ampersand, a dollar sign and a backtick, and assert the
    program receives that value in ONE argv element, byte-identical to what was
    handed in -- have the entry point echo what it received and diff it against
    the source, rather than accepting "it started" as proof. Then assert
    PERSISTENCE, not just startup: read the value back out of the ledger, the
    log line and the output filename and require all three to match the
    original. Keep the hostile string as a fixture so a future rewrite of the
    launcher cannot quietly reintroduce concatenation.
  tags: [powershell, shell-tokenization, argument-quoting, argparse, free-text-argument, metacharacter, launcher, durable-corruption, stop-parsing, all-route]
  legacy_id: RUNLOG-20260723-span-lla28
- id: "12.85"
  phase: 12
  area: zero-consumer-declaration-integrity
  symptom: |
    A scripted repo-wide refactor lands and everything agrees it is fine: the
    suite is green across thousands of tests, imports resolve, the pack boots,
    the banner reports all N nodes loaded with zero skips, and no log line is
    out of place. Weeks later a human opens one module and finds its export list
    naming things that do not exist -- entries like self.recipe_id, literal
    strings naming nothing, sitting in the module's own public contract. No tool
    ever reported it, because no tool ever read it.
  cause: |
    A mechanical string replacement -- sed, a regex codemod, a scripted
    attribute rename -- rewrote text inside a declaration that has ZERO
    consumers on any executed path. __all__ only takes effect on a star-import;
    if nothing star-imports the module, the list is never resolved, never
    dereferenced, never compared to anything. What the script produced is
    syntactically valid Python: it AST-parses, it imports, it is a list of
    strings. So every guard the project owns passes honestly -- the compiler is
    satisfied, the import succeeds, coverage marks the line executed because it
    IS a module-level assignment, and the boot signal that catches ghost
    registrations is silent because nothing failed to load. The script itself
    cannot tell an identifier in an expression from the same identifier inside a
    quoted string, so the self. prefix it correctly added to attribute accesses
    was applied to the string literals beside them. The class generalizes past
    __all__: any declaration whose elements are STRINGS NAMING CODE -- export
    lists, registry key tables, plugin manifests, doc automodule lists, test
    collection lists -- is a place a scripted edit can leave garbage that
    nothing will ever dereference.
  fix: |
    Give every zero-consumer declaration a reader. A parametrized static test
    that walks each module and resolves every name its __all__ lists against
    that module closes the whole class in a few lines, and parametrizing over
    discovered modules rather than a hand-kept list means a new module is
    covered the day it is added rather than the day it breaks. Treat a scripted
    replacement as an untrusted transformation in its own right: constrain it so
    it cannot match inside string literals -- operate on the AST, not on the
    text -- and READ THE FULL DIFF of a mechanical edit before committing,
    because the entire premise of a codemod is that nobody reads it, which is
    precisely why the one place it can hide is the place nobody reads. Delete
    declarations that have no consumer instead of maintaining them dead; an
    export list nothing imports is a liability with no reader to notice when it
    rots.
  verify: |
    Assert statically over the shipped source rather than a booted object: for
    every module, each string in __all__ resolves to a real attribute of that
    module, and no entry is duplicated. Prove the assertion can fail by
    injecting one bogus name and requiring the suite to go red -- a test over a
    construct nothing reads is itself easy to write in a form that reads
    nothing. Then sweep the tree for the codemod's own fingerprint in places it
    should never appear -- a self. prefix inside a string literal, a
    substitution token inside a docstring or a comment -- and require zero hits,
    because a scripted edit that reached one quoted region reached others.
  tags: [dead-declaration, zero-reader, star-import, codemod, scripted-refactor, false-green, static-assertion, export-list, ast-over-text, mutation-check]
  legacy_id: GITFIX-80935627

- id: "12.86"
  phase: 12
  area: receipt-keyed-on-a-string-the-producer-never-emits
  symptom: |
    A telemetry field, receipt, or prompt-context slot is permanently empty or
    permanently False, and nothing ever complains. Reports read clean while the
    condition they describe is happening constantly. Because the wrong value is
    the SAFE-looking one -- False, "", absent -- no alarm fires, no test fails,
    and the surface can stay dead for weeks or months. It is usually found by
    accident while chasing something else, and the giveaway is a field nobody
    can remember seeing set to anything.
  cause: |
    The consumer reads a NAME the producer never emits. Three shapes dominate.
    (1) An attribute read with a default -- `getattr(obj, "hook", "")` -- where
    the dataclass has never defined `hook`; the default silently supplies the
    empty value forever. (2) A membership test against a flag string the emitter
    does not produce: `"open_safe_fallback" in flags` where the composer emits
    `announcer_intro_structural_fallback`. (3) A dict/key lookup for a key that
    was renamed or never existed on that row shape. In every shape the two sides
    were written at different times, or one was refactored and the other was
    not, and the language offers no error because a default or a False is a
    perfectly legal answer. Refactors that RENAME an emitted flag are the most
    common origin, since nothing links the emitter to its readers.
  fix: |
    Make the mismatch LOUD instead of empty. Read required fields by DIRECT
    attribute access, not `getattr` with a default, so a rename raises
    AttributeError at the read instead of silently emptying the value; reserve
    defaults for fields that are genuinely optional. Where a string must be
    matched, define it ONCE as a shared constant that both the emitter and the
    reader import, so a rename cannot desynchronise them. Where the producer's
    vocabulary is closed, validate against that closed set at write time. Then
    delete the orphaned reader rather than leaving it -- a field nobody sets is
    a trap for the next person, who will assume it means something.
  verify: |
    Assert every stamped flag string against its ACTUAL producer, not against
    itself: for each `X in flags` test, prove some code path emits X. Assert the
    label BINDS the value -- a prompt-context test must check that the field's
    CONTENT reached the prompt, not merely that the label appears. Cover the
    negative and the positive separately, because a permanently-False receipt
    passes any test that only ever asserts False; include at least one case
    where the field MUST be True and one where it must be False. Finally, mutate
    each receipt predicate once and require the suite to go red -- a receipt
    whose test stays green when its predicate is reverted was never covered.
  tags: [telemetry, dead-receipt, silent-failure, getattr-default, flag-string-drift, refactor-desync, fails-safe-so-nobody-notices, prompt-context, mutation-check, all-route]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260807-01

- id: "12.87"
  phase: 12
  area: gate-reports-success-from-its-own-error-path
  symptom: |
    A validation step prints a clean result and exits 0 while its headline
    check never executed. The reassuring line is real -- the script did run,
    the JSON did parse, the secondary audits did pass -- so the receipt is
    quoted in commit messages and acceptance sections as proof of the ONE thing
    it did not do. Nothing looks broken: there is no traceback, no red, no
    missing artifact. The only trace is a note on stderr saying the check was
    skipped, which is invisible to anyone reading an exit code, a CI status, or
    a pasted "OK" line. The failure can persist for weeks because every symptom
    of it looks like success.
  cause: |
    An error path returns the SAME sentinel the success path returns. A
    problem-collecting function that answers with an empty list means "nothing
    wrong", so `except: return []` converts "I could not check" into "I
    checked and it was fine". The caller sums problem counts, finds zero, and
    reports OK.
    Two things make it durable. First, the skip is usually DELIBERATE and
    documented as a convenience -- "skip rather than fail when the package is
    not importable" -- so a reader who finds it assumes it is a rare
    degradation rather than the normal path. Second, the condition that
    triggers it may be PERMANENT rather than occasional: an import keyed on a
    name that can never resolve (a directory with a hyphen imported as an
    identifier, a package the host loads BY PATH), an environment variable that
    is never set in the context the gate runs in, a service that is never
    reachable from CI. When the trigger is permanent the check has run ZERO
    times in its entire life, while its output has been cited as evidence
    throughout.
    The strongest tell that this has already happened: somewhere in the tree
    another component has quietly reimplemented the same check for itself,
    with a comment explaining that the shared one cannot be trusted. Someone
    hit the hole, solved it locally, and left the shared gate lying.
  fix: |
    Make "could not check" a FAILURE, never a silent pass. The error path must
    return a problem, raise, or exit non-zero -- and its message must say the
    check did not run, not merely that something went wrong, because those
    prompt different responses from the reader.
    Fix the resolution itself rather than only the reporting: if the check
    cannot find what it needs, find out WHY before making the skip loud, or the
    change simply converts a silent false green into a permanent red. Load
    resources the way the host actually loads them -- by path when the host
    loads by path -- instead of by a name that cannot resolve.
    Take a `--strict` flag only if every call site can be updated in the same
    change; a flag that defaults to the old behaviour leaves the lie in place
    behind a new option. Prefer making the honest behaviour the default.
    When a gate reports partial results, say which parts ran. A summary that
    cannot distinguish "all checks passed" from "the checks that ran passed"
    is not a gate. And when you route AROUND a broken gate to get a real
    answer, fix the gate in the same change -- the workaround is proof the gate
    is broken, and shipping only the workaround guarantees the next person
    inherits the false green.
  verify: |
    Force the failure and assert the EXIT CODE, not the log text: make the
    resource unresolvable -- point the root at an empty directory, unset the
    variable, break the import -- and require a non-zero exit. Pair it with a
    control on the real target that must still pass, so fail-closed does not
    silently become fail-always.
    Then prove the check RUNS in the normal case, which is the assertion the
    original gate never made: assert on a positive artifact only the real check
    can produce -- a count of validated items, a resolved path, a named
    contract result -- rather than on the absence of problems. Absence of
    problems is exactly what the broken version reported.
    Grep the repo for reimplementations of the same check before closing:
    another module carrying its own copy is evidence the shared gate has been
    failing open, and tells you how long for.
  tags: [gate, false-green, fail-open, exit-code, skipped-check, empty-sentinel, permanent-trigger, import-by-path, ci, acceptance-evidence, workaround-shipped-gate-left-broken, xref-12.86]
  legacy_id: OTR-2026-08-09-validate-canonical-workflow-false-green

- id: "12.88"

  cause: |

    which is correct only for installs that keep their models under the comfy
    tree. A box whose extra_model_paths.yaml points somewhere else gets a
    confident wrong NO from every off-runtime caller.
  fix: |

    construction: it cannot alter an answer that was already a hit, only turn a
    false negative into the truth. Put the root resolution in ONE named helper
    so a second copy of the chain cannot drift from the first.
  verify: |
    Unset every model-path env var for the engine and assert the installed
    weight still resolves. Then point the models-root env at an EMPTY temp
    directory and assert it reads as absent, so the probe is proved to be
    probing rather than always answering yes.
