- id: "12.89"
  phase: 12
  area: receipt-asserts-a-fact-the-render-disproves
  symptom: |
    A telemetry field is confidently WRONG rather than missing, and the wrong
    value is the assertive one -- True, a feature name, a version. Nothing looks
    empty, so the checks that hunt for null fields walk straight past it, and the
    value propagates: a rollup groups on it, a summary reports it, and eventually
    a PUBLISHED artifact states it to an end user. The work itself is correct
    throughout; only the description of the work is false. This is the inverse
    polarity of the permanently-empty receipt and needs its own hunt, because it
    cannot be found by looking for fields nobody ever sets.
    A separate OTR occurrence was published by My Story pairlock_02 on
    bd814148: actual IndexTTS2 references were selected for blank-gender cast
    rows, while the credits named legacy Bark presets. That run also had a
    source-gender defect; truthful engine/reference reporting does not by
    itself establish correct casting or source fidelity.
  cause: |
    The runtime and the receipt read the same underlying value through DIFFERENT
    rules. Code that decides behaviour uses a named predicate -- "is this
    component switched off?" -- while code that writes the receipt uses raw
    truthiness on the same token. They agree for every value except the one that
    means "off", and a config that switches a component off BY NAME ("none",
    "off", "disabled", "0") is a non-empty string, which is truthy. So the branch
    that skips the component and the field that reports it running are reading
    one variable and reaching opposite answers.
    What makes it durable is that the nearest test asserts the field's SHAPE --
    isinstance(value, bool) -- and a wrong bool is still a bool. That test passes
    forever while the value is inverted for a whole tier.
    Another route skips primary assignment when a source attribute is unknown,
    then selects a real asset at render time. Without the delivered stamp, a
    legacy default is mistaken for the actual selection by the publishing reader.
  fix: |
    Give the fact ONE authority and make every consumer read it. Where a
    predicate already exists because the runtime needed it, the receipt calls
    that same predicate rather than re-deriving the answer from the raw token.
    Never let a receipt compute a fact the runtime already decided.
    Then push the check up to the GATE rather than the one component: a preflight
    assertion comparing each receipt field against what the code actually does
    makes every later component inherit it, because this recurs wherever a
    skip-token idiom is adopted.
    For alternate assignment paths, use the existing durable assignment owner
    to stamp the same selected asset before dispatch. Keep an unknown source
    attribute separate from the now-known selected reference; do not invent the
    attribute, add a content gate or create another post-freeze ledger writer.
  verify: |
    For every tier, assert the receipt field EQUALS what the runtime does with
    the same token -- not that it is the right type, and not against a literal.
    Add a second assertion at the CONSUMER that publishes it, so the fix is
    pinned where the falsehood actually surfaced and not only at its source.
    OTR's test_genderless_cast_wire_disk_render_and_credits_share_the_actual_reference
    executes CastLock, durable disk/singleton/wire persistence, real reference
    resolution and the credits builder. The Bible catalog retains this behavior
    coverage; the OTR suite executes it without model synthesis.
  tags: [receipts, telemetry, truthiness, sentinel, published-artifact, gate-hole, xref-12.51, xref-12.90]

- id: "12.90"
  phase: 12
  area: a-test-that-pins-the-bug-it-should-have-caught
  symptom: |
    Fixing a real defect turns the suite RED, and the failing tests are the ones
    whose names describe the invariant just restored. The suite argues for the
    broken behaviour, confidently and in numbers, so the fix reads as the
    regression. This is the most expensive shape a stale test takes: it does not
    merely fail to catch the bug, it actively defends it, and an engineer who
    trusts the suite will revert a correct fix.
  cause: |
    Two ways in, both of which look like diligence at the time. Either the test
    was written from the CODE rather than from the requirement -- someone
    captured what the function currently returned and froze it -- or the body
    drifted from its own name while the name kept stating the rule. The second is
    worse, because the name is what a reviewer reads: a test called "unknowable
    is not the same as satisfied" whose body asserts the unknowable case returns
    satisfied passes review by everyone who does not open it.
    A related instance: a test that pins a DERIVED value (a half-size, a computed
    offset) instead of the rule producing it. When the input changes legally, the
    pinned derivation is wrong and the test calls the new correct value an error.
  fix: |
    When a fix turns tests red, read each failing test's NAME against its BODY
    before touching either. If the name states the rule just implemented, the
    body is the bug -- rewrite the body to assert the name. If the test was using
    the fixed function as a PROXY for a different subject, give it what the
    stricter code now needs (a fixture, an injected state) rather than weakening
    the code back to make it pass.
    Assert rules and invariants, not captured outputs. Where a value is derived,
    assert the derivation against its source so a legal change to the input moves
    the expectation with it.
  verify: |
    After rewriting, confirm each test FAILS against the old behaviour -- a
    rewritten test that passes both ways is pinning nothing. Grep the suite for
    literals duplicating a value the code declares, and replace each with a read
    of the declaration.
  tags: [tests, regression, stale-assertions, invariants, review, false-red]

- id: "12.91"
  phase: 12
  area: defect-filed-against-one-component-lives-in-every-component-sharing-the-mechanism
  symptom: |
    A defect is found, fixed, written up and closed against ONE component -- and
    an identical instance is still live one file away. Worse than an ordinary
    miss: the ledger now says this class was dealt with, so the second instance
    is protected by the record of the first being fixed. It resurfaces later
    looking novel, in a component whose own tests are green.
  cause: |
    The defect was attributed to where it was NOTICED rather than to what CAUSED
    it. When the cause is a shared mechanism -- a common helper, a third-party
    node several graphs call, an idiom copied between siblings -- the blast
    radius is every consumer of that mechanism, not the one that failed first.
    A second effect hides it: the same code can be LEGAL in one configuration and
    illegal in another, so a sibling that has not moved yet looks fine and
    genuinely is. The defect arrives there later, with no change to that file,
    when its configuration moves to a value the mechanism cannot accept. A
    component can break because something it depends on was reconfigured
    elsewhere, and no diff on that component will ever show it.
  fix: |
    Before closing a defect, name the MECHANISM that caused it and enumerate
    every consumer. Fix or explicitly clear each one, and record the defect
    against the mechanism rather than the component.
    Then encode the rule as a check over the whole registry rather than the one
    component: derive the constraint the mechanism imposes and assert it for
    every consumer carrying the mechanism's fingerprint, so a component adopting
    the idiom later is covered on arrival instead of when someone remembers.
  verify: |
    Grep for the mechanism's signature -- the helper call, the node class, the
    idiom -- and assert the derived constraint for every hit, driven off the live
    registry rather than a hand-written list. Prove the check by pointing one
    consumer at an illegal configuration and confirming it fails.
  tags: [shared-code, blast-radius, registry-wide, attribution, configuration, gate-hole]

- id: "12.92"
  phase: 12
  area: a-config-pin-that-becomes-a-bug-by-remote-action
  symptom: |
    A configuration value that was correct when written starts causing damage
    without anyone touching it, its file, or its component. There is no diff to
    blame and no commit that introduced the failure -- the change happened in a
    DIFFERENT module and the pin's meaning changed underneath it. Symptoms are
    downstream and diffuse: work chopped into fragments, a ceiling refusing what
    it used to allow, a budget applying at the wrong stage.
  cause: |
    The pin constrained one mechanism, and a later change moved the value into a
    second mechanism's path -- typically by adding the component to a list that
    gives an existing knob new authority (a planning cap, an admission gate, a
    scheduling tier). Compensation elsewhere that made the pin harmless is often
    ripped in the same change, so the protection disappears at the exact moment
    the pin gains reach.
    Comments make it durable: the component's own documentation still describes
    the protection that was deleted, so a reader who checks concludes the pin is
    safe. And the tests pinning the value are numerous and confident, because it
    WAS correct when they were written.
  fix: |
    When adding a component to any list that gives an existing knob new
    authority, enumerate every config setting that knob for the component and
    re-derive whether each is still correct under the new meaning. Treat list
    membership as the breaking change it is.
    Assert tunable numbers against the source of truth they come from rather than
    against copied literals, so the value can move without the tests lying in
    either direction. Audit any comment describing a protection: a comment
    describing deleted protection is how the next reader is talked out of the
    correct diagnosis.
  verify: |
    For each component in a capability list, assert its configs are legal under
    the semantics that list confers, and that the emitted work matches what was
    requested rather than the pin. Prove the value reaches the stage you think:
    set it, run, and check the produced segmentation, not the config file.
  tags: [config, profiles, planning, stale-comment, remote-change, tests-defend-bug]

- id: "12.93"
  phase: 12
  area: a-fix-lands-on-the-path-the-tests-run-not-the-path-the-install-selects
  symptom: |
    A capability is added, reviewed, tested and shipped, and the running system
    still behaves as if it were never added. The test suite is green and
    exercises the new code honestly. Nothing appears in any log, because the
    code that was fixed is genuinely correct -- it simply never executes on this
    machine.
  cause: |
    The component has two or more render/execution paths and selects between
    them IMPLICITLY at runtime, from installed assets rather than from
    configuration a reader can see: a variant detected from a weight filename, a
    family probe, a capability sniff, a default that differs per install. The
    fix was applied to the path the author had open, which is usually the one
    the CPU test fixtures instantiate; the installed assets select the other.
    A test suite cannot catch this, because with fakes BOTH paths are equally
    reachable and equally green. The selection is a property of the machine, not
    of the code, so only a live run on that machine disagrees.
  fix: |
    For any component with more than one execution path, resolve WHICH PATH THE
    INSTALLED CONFIGURATION SELECTS before deciding where a fix belongs, and
    apply it to every path that can produce the artifact -- or make the paths
    share one seam so there is only one place to fix. Treat "which branch does
    this box take" as a fact to look up, never to assume from the code order.
    When a change claims to enable a downstream capability ("X cannot measure
    without this"), prove the capability on the path a live run actually takes
    before believing the claim.
  verify: |
    Enumerate the component's execution paths and, for each, assert the new
    field/guard/receipt is present. Then run ONE live job and read the produced
    artifact -- not the test output -- for the thing the change was supposed to
    add. A green suite is not evidence that the shipped path changed.
  tags: [multi-path, implicit-dispatch, install-dependent, receipts, live-proof]

- id: "12.94"
  phase: 12
  area: a-missing-measurement-silently-replaced-by-a-weaker-one
  symptom: |
    A metric is present, plausible, and wrong -- consistently too low or too
    smooth. Nothing is null, nothing warns, and the number is used to calibrate
    a budget or a limit. Later that limit admits work that then fails at
    runtime, and the calibration data cannot be told apart from good data
    because both are plain numbers in the same field.
  cause: |
    A consumer reads the strong measurement with a fallback to a weaker one in a
    single expression -- `value = precise() or approximate()` or an equivalent
    default-on-missing. The fallback exists for genuine reasons (an older
    producer, an off-hardware path) but it converts ABSENCE into a DIFFERENT
    KIND OF NUMBER rather than into a missing value. A sampled instantaneous
    reading substituted for a windowed maximum is a LOWER BOUND, and a budget
    derived from a lower bound under-predicts demand, which is precisely the
    direction that admits work that then exhausts the resource.
  fix: |
    Never let a fallback change the KIND of a measurement. If the precise source
    is absent, propagate absence -- null, not a substitute -- and let the
    consumer refuse or skip explicitly. Where both kinds are genuinely useful,
    record them in separate fields with the source named, plus a boolean saying
    whether the row may seed a calibration; a weaker reading stays readable
    without becoming usable.
  verify: |
    Assert that a result carrying no precise measurement canonicalizes to null
    rather than to any number. Separately, assert a calibration refuses to be
    seeded from a row whose provenance flag is unset. Diff the two sources on
    one live job: if they differ by a large factor, the fallback was never
    interchangeable and any historical row built on it is suspect.
  tags: [measurement, provenance, fallback, calibration, silent-substitution]

- id: "12.95"
  phase: 12
  area: a-constant-measured-under-one-configuration-is-not-a-property-of-the-component
  symptom: |
    A component enforces a magic number that looks conservative and costs far
    more than it should -- work rounded up to a floor, a minimum batch, a forced
    size -- with no failure to justify it. Removing it feels reckless because
    nobody remembers what it protects, so it survives every review as prudence.
  cause: |
    The number was MEASURED, correctly, against a configuration the component no
    longer uses: a different canvas, resolution, precision, batch shape or
    backend. Constants do not carry their provenance, so a measured constraint
    and an arbitrary guess are indistinguishable once the author has moved on --
    and the conservative-looking one is never challenged. When the configuration
    changed, nothing re-derived the constraint, because the constraint lived in
    a different file from the thing that moved.
  fix: |
    Record the configuration each enforced constant was measured under, in the
    constant's own comment, and state that it is UNKNOWN at any other
    configuration. When the configuration moves, re-measure rather than
    inheriting; treat a configuration change as invalidating every constant
    derived from the old one, in both directions. Where a declaration and a
    runtime constant must agree, assert one against the other so the pair cannot
    drift silently.
  verify: |
    Sweep the constrained dimension at the CURRENT configuration and record
    which values actually fail -- including the values that failed under the old
    configuration, which are the informative ones. Assert the enforced constant
    equals the measured boundary, and that a declaration derived from it matches
    the runtime value rather than a copied literal.
  tags: [constants, provenance, configuration-drift, measurement, over-conservatism]

- id: "12.96"
  phase: 12
  area: staging-a-file-that-already-lives-in-the-staging-destination
  symptom: |
    A job dies at a file-staging step, before any real work, with a Windows
    sharing violation -- "The process cannot access the file because it is being
    used by another process" (WinError 32) -- naming a path but nothing about
    what was being staged. No other process holds the file. On POSIX the same
    code silently truncates the file to zero bytes instead of raising, which is
    worse.
  cause: |
    A helper copies an input into a tool's working/input directory and returns
    its basename. When the caller supplies a path that is ALREADY inside that
    destination, source and destination resolve to the same file and the copy is
    a self-copy. It is easy to hit precisely because the destination is a
    natural place for such files to live -- a previous run staged one there, or
    an operator picked an example from it.
  fix: |
    Compare source and destination by RESOLVED identity before copying
    (os.path.samefile, or realpath equality when the destination may not exist
    yet) and skip the copy when they are the same file -- the input is already
    staged and its basename is the correct answer. Do not wrap the copy in a
    broad try/except: that hides genuine permission and disk failures, which is
    the same class of defect one level up.
  verify: |
    Stage a file that already lives in the destination directory and assert the
    helper returns its name without raising and without changing its bytes
    (compare a hash before and after). Assert a real permission failure still
    propagates.
  tags: [windows, file-staging, self-copy, winerror32, fail-loud]
- id: "12.97"
  phase: 12
  area: a-model-field-name-that-collides-with-its-base-class
  symptom: |
    A pydantic field is declared required, yet instances validate WITHOUT it and
    the missing value comes back as a bound method. The next json.dumps dies
    with "Object of type method is not JSON serializable", naming neither the
    model nor the field. The quieter half is worse: the JSON schema generated
    from that model stops listing the field in `required`, so an LLM prompted
    against it is never asked for a documented value, and the method repr
    reaches the prompt as text.
  cause: |
    The field name also resolves on the base class or its METACLASS. Pydantic's
    ModelMetaclass inherits ABCMeta, so a name like `register` resolves on
    BaseModel without appearing in vars(BaseModel). Pydantic does not reject the
    collision -- it adopts the inherited attribute as the field's DEFAULT, which
    silently turns a required field optional. `copy`, `json`, `schema`, `dict`,
    `validate` and `construct` are the same trap. A warnings filter that
    silences the shadowing warning hides the only signal.
  fix: |
    Declare the field explicitly with `Field(...)` so the class body shadows the
    inherited attribute and restores required-ness. The wire name is unchanged,
    so a contract term that cannot be renamed stays put. Do NOT "fix" it with a
    default: that stops the crash while leaving the field optional and the model
    never asked for it. Renaming is correct only when the name is not part of an
    external contract.
  verify: |
    Assert `Model.model_fields[name].is_required()` is True and that the name
    appears in `model_json_schema()["required"]`. Then sweep EVERY pydantic
    model in the package: for each field default, `json.dumps(default)` must not
    raise. Guard the sweep with a companion assertion that it actually found the
    models, so a broken import cannot make it vacuously green.
  tags: [pydantic, schema, serialization, structured-output, silent-default]
  legacy_id: "PBUG-20260812-02"
- id: "12.98"
  phase: 12
  area: a-constant-its-own-documentation-disqualifies-is-still-live
  symptom: |
    A guard refuses real, previously-shipping work. The refusal message is
    specific and confident -- it names a budget, a measured input and a
    computed ceiling -- so it reads as a resource problem, and the reported
    resource figure is usually low enough to make that story plausible.
    Investigation chases the resource. Meanwhile the comment block DIRECTLY
    BENEATH the offending table already says the table is wrong, names the
    production leg it previously killed, and states what the correct value is.
    Nobody reads it, because the refusal points somewhere else.
  cause: |
    A tuned constant was measured once, found wrong, and documented as
    disqualified IN PLACE -- but the row was never deleted, and one consumer
    still reads it. A sibling consumer was already fixed ("X stopped consulting
    it for exactly this reason"), which is what makes the survivor invisible:
    the codebase looks like it handled the problem. The doc block is the
    author's own analysis, written at the moment of discovery, and it degrades
    into decoration the instant it is not accompanied by the deletion it
    argues for.
  fix: |
    CHECK THE FALLBACK BEFORE YOU DELETE ANYTHING. This entry originally
    prescribed deletion outright; that was proven a no-op the day it was
    written. The lookup was `TABLE.get(key, DEFAULT)` and DEFAULT was the
    byte-identical tuple, so removing both rows changed no verdict, no
    computed ceiling and no exception -- the guard refused exactly as before,
    and the "fix" would have shipped as a green diff that fixed nothing. A
    disqualified row and a disqualified default are the same constant wearing
    two names. Establish the delete-vs-gate question by running the real call
    with the row removed, not by reading the table.
    So: if the value survives its own deletion, deletion is not the fix. GATE
    THE ENFORCEMENT INSTEAD. Route every refusal through one explicit
    predicate -- "may this row REFUSE?", never "does a row exist?" -- and make
    the disqualified value predict, log and return while refusing nothing.
    Grep for EVERY consumer first and gate them all: the failure that produced
    this entry was one authority wired to one of its two call sites, so the
    same constant was inert on the reviewed path and live one call away.
    Gate the WHOLE row, not the term you find least defensible. A row with a
    disqualified slope and a plausible-looking fixed overhead invites keeping
    half of it; resist that unless the evidence separates the terms, because
    "one row, one question" is what stops a second, unevidenced authority from
    appearing. Here the overhead looked unimpeached and was not -- it was
    derived from an ABSOLUTE peak while the comparison ran against FREE bytes,
    double-charging a baseline on every prediction.
    If a replacement is proposed from an existing bench or lab fit, check the
    project's own admission rule first: a standing "no bench may substitute for
    the real lifecycle" ruling forbids adopting those numbers however much
    closer to truth they look. Deletion remains right when the value has NO
    fallback behind it and the documentation says empty is correct.
  verify: |
    Compute the guard's verdict at the EXTREMES of its input, not at the
    observed value. Here the row priced a 125-frame request at 30,125 MB on a
    16 GB card -- it refused on an empty device, which proves the resource was
    never the variable. A guard that refuses at every input it can ever see is
    not a guard. Assert that: for each row, at maximum plausible resource, the
    largest request the planner can emit must still be affordable, or the row
    must not refuse. Grep for consumers before trusting that a fixed sibling
    means the value is dead.
    PIN THE NO-OP ITSELF, or the deletion gets proposed again. Assert that each
    row equals the fallback constant, with a message telling the next reader
    that the no-op argument must be re-derived if it ever stops being true.
    Then assert the disqualified value's behaviour from BOTH sides: the exact
    production inputs that were refused now return their full request, and a
    row that IS qualified still refuses them -- otherwise the test proves the
    guard is broken rather than gated. Keep validation OUT of the gate: a
    malformed or negative coefficient is a configuration error and must stay
    fatal for every engine, qualified or not. Finally, require that
    qualification names a row that actually exists, or a typo silently promotes
    the shared fallback into an enforcing guard.
  tags: [guards, tuned-constants, stale-config, dead-row, refusal-path, doc-vs-code-drift, cost-model, xref-12.73]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-13-frame-cost-model"
- id: "12.99"
  phase: 12
  area: a-generator-gone-stale-destroys-the-artifact-it-is-named-for
  symptom: |
    A file carries a header naming the script that generates it, so a
    maintainer edits the script and re-runs it. The run reports success and
    prints a plausible summary. The artifact silently loses most of its
    content, because contributors had been appending to the OUTPUT directly for
    weeks and the generator never learned about any of it.
  cause: |
    An append-only artifact acquires two write paths: the declared generator,
    and hand edits by whoever needed a row landed today. Hand edits are faster
    and always win in the moment, so the generator's own data drifts into a
    strict subset of what ships. Nothing detects this, because a generator is
    trusted precisely by being the declared source of truth -- the header
    saying "GENERATOR for X" is read as a guarantee rather than as an intention
    that stopped being true. A version field that the artifact bumps and the
    generator does not is the fingerprint, and nobody compares them because
    running the generator is supposed to be the safe operation.
  fix: |
    Make the generator FAIL CLOSED against its own output: read the shipped
    artifact's version before writing, and refuse when it is newer than the
    version this code produces. Say what would have been lost, and name the
    reconciliation as the work -- rows missing from the generator must be
    re-derived from their sources, never copied back out of the artifact, or
    the generator is laundering hand edits it cannot reproduce.
    Do NOT "fix" it by bumping the generator's version to match. That silences
    the guard and re-arms the trap. Do NOT delete the generator either while
    anything still cites it; an unrunnable generator with a loud refusal is
    strictly better than a runnable one that destroys evidence.
  verify: |
    Run the generator against the shipped artifact in CI and assert it either
    reproduces the file BYTE-FOR-BYTE or exits non-zero. Those are the only two
    honest outcomes; a generator that runs clean and produces something smaller
    is the bug. Diff the row/key COUNT, not just the bytes, so a reader sees
    scale ("123 lines, 9 lanes") rather than "file changed".
    Assert the refusal itself: stamp the artifact one version ahead and require
    a non-zero exit whose message names the version gap.
  tags: [generators, codegen, evidence, append-only, silent-data-loss, doc-vs-code-drift, fail-closed, xref-12.98]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-13-evidence-manifest-generator"

- id: "12.100"
  phase: 12
  area: a-length-ceiling-is-not-a-liveness-guard-and-can-hide-the-runaway-it-replaces
  symptom: |
    A schema-constrained local decode falls into a verbatim repetition loop
    inside ONE string field and never exits. It generates until the context
    ceiling -- measured: 13,912 tokens over 22 minutes, the same ~384-token
    paragraph about 36 times -- and every token is discarded. Nothing looks
    wrong while it happens: the GPU is busy, tokens flow at full rate, and
    every liveness signal is healthy.
    THEN the repair makes it quieter. After a max_length is added, the same
    runaway comes back as valid JSON that passes schema validation and ships a
    sentence cut off mid-word. And a detector built on "how long has this
    string been open" cannot tell a legitimately long field from a loop, so it
    is a second length cap wearing a liveness costume.
    A distinct termination-boundary specimen appeared in OTR's full canonical
    My Story pairlock_01 on 2c9d47f2, prompt
    19cba4cc-ceaa-43d6-833a-393854eb45f1: both attempts of one optional source
    correction produced complete JSON followed by 82 and 87 trailing newlines, then hit
    the liveness guard at 672 generated tokens. The main treatment separately
    exhausted three attempts on open-string repetition; the run ended without
    media after 400.67 seconds. The request, terminal history and failed ledger
    are archived under docs/2026-09-11-my-story-5080-qualification/ in OTR.
  cause: |
    Three separate mistakes that look like one:
    (a) Under grammar-constrained decoding the EOS token is masked until the
        document is complete, so the only exit from a string is its closing
        quote. Once a repetition loop locks, nucleus/min-p sampling prunes that
        quote to probability ZERO -- the model is not refusing to stop, it is
        never offered the option. A repetition penalty cannot help because the
        HF penalty is not frequency-aware: a token emitted 60 times is
        penalised exactly as much as one emitted once.
    (b) A grammar enforcer stops a string AT its max_length by admitting only
        progressively shorter tokens as the limit nears, so a forced closure
        lands anywhere within one MAX-TOKEN-LENGTH below the ceiling. An
        exact-hit detector therefore misses most real forced closures, and the
        truncated string sails through.
    (c) The ceiling only terminates a decode where a grammar ENFORCES it at
        token selection. On a post-validated lane -- schema described in the
        prompt, parsed after the call -- a max_length stops nothing: the
        runaway still burns the window, and the artifact is discarded
        afterwards. That is strictly worse than no ceiling.
    At the completed-document boundary, the grammar and native decoder can
    also disagree on what ends a response: the pairlock configuration readback
    found tokenizer/chat EOS 248046 versus native model-config EOS 248044,
    with no cached generation_config.json. Grammar admission, generation stopping
    and post-call completion classification had separate terminator owners.
    The exact hidden token sequence was not persisted, so the newline artifact
    establishes a termination gap, not proof of which EOS token was sampled.
    This evidence does not explain or resolve the separate open-string failure.
  fix: |
    TWO SIGNALS ARE REQUIRED, AND NEITHER SUBSUMES THE OTHER. This entry
    originally prescribed cycle detection alone; that was written from two
    captured specimens and a third existed. Corrected the same day.

    SIGNAL 1 -- DETECT REPETITION, NOT LENGTH, for the looping runaways. The
    signal is a run of tokens repeating back to back, which is independent of
    how long the output is allowed to be. Read TOKEN IDS, never decoded text --
    then the guard is tokenizer-independent (no isolated-token decoding, no
    byte-fallback or escape handling) and format-independent (it covers JSON,
    markup and free prose identically).

    SIGNAL 2 -- AN OPEN-STRUCTURE COUNTER, for the ELABORATION SPIRAL: a
    runaway that NEVER repeats. Measured: 15,355 tokens, 83k chars, every
    sentence new, every sentence well-formed, none of it ever arriving at an
    end. Repetition detection is structurally blind to it -- there is no cycle
    to find -- so the only cheap observable is that ONE structural unit (a JSON
    string, a section, a field) has stayed OPEN without closing. That is not a
    length cap: it does not measure the output, it measures one unit's failure
    to terminate, so an artifact of many complete units never approaches it.
    SCOPE IT to passes where the delimiter is STRUCTURE. A quote lexer on free
    prose opens on the first line of dialogue and counts the rest of the scene.

    STATE WHAT THE GUARD CAN AND CANNOT CLAIM. It is COST-BOUNDING, not a
    correctness oracle: non-termination cannot be proven from a prefix, since
    any prefix of a spiral is also a possible prefix of an output that closes
    later. Target a low false-abort rate, short detection latency and compute
    saved -- never "catches every runaway and nothing else".
    And distinguish LOCAL from GLOBAL length: a 15,000-token DOCUMENT can be
    valid while a 15,000-token single field is a runaway. Bounding the
    structural unit is what lets the artifact grow without weakening the guard.
    Use a LATCHED StoppingCriteria: detect, set a flag, return True, let
    generate() return normally, then classify and raise OUTSIDE. Raising from
    inside a criterion skips the caller's decode/evidence construction and the
    streamer's own teardown, and arrives unclassified.
    Raise a DISTINCT, REROLLABLE phase. Reusing the capacity-exhausted phase
    makes every diagnostic lie: a halted decode stops with most of its
    allowance unspent, so "ended at the provider capacity limit" sends the next
    reader hunting a budget defect that does not exist.
    Keep schema ceilings, but as a STRUCTURAL backstop sized far above anything
    real content produces, and reject at a GUARD BAND below them wider than the
    longest single token the tokenizer can emit. Route that rejection to a
    reroll, never to silently accepted text.
    DO NOT reach for no-repeat-n-grams when a grammar is active: the n-gram ban
    and the grammar mask both write -inf, prefix validation only checks that the
    grammar returned a non-empty list rather than that a token SURVIVED the ban,
    and the intersection can be empty -- which crashes sampling outright.
    Align the termination contract separately from liveness: resolve effective
    native generation-config terminators first, fall back to the decoder's
    nested text/model config when absent, and combine them with the tokenizer's
    chat terminators. Preserve multiple valid IDs and zero, deduplicate without
    mutating either configuration, exclude booleans/negative/non-integral values,
    and keep padding scalar. Use the same per-call set for every native factory,
    grammar admission and post-call completion classification. Rebuild cached
    grammar tokenizer data when its EOS signature changes, while keeping each
    parser/enforcer fresh and old in-flight histories unchanged. Do not weaken
    the liveness guard, parse-and-salvage halted output, or claim this contract
    fix has qualified an open-string failure; a fresh live run must establish
    the narrower observed termination behavior and remaining authoring outcome.
  verify: |
    Automatable, and the false-positive tests are the ones that matter:
    (0) Feed it a NON-REPEATING spiral -- one unit, thousands of tokens, every
        token new -- and assert signal 2 fires while signal 1 does not. A guard
        tested only against loops will pass while blind to a third of the
        known failures.
    (1) Feed the detector thousands of tokens of VARIED output and assert it
        does NOT fire. A test whose no-halt case never crosses the threshold
        proves nothing -- size the input far beyond any bound.
    (2) Feed it a cycle at the measured period and assert it fires.
    (3) Assert structural repetition is ignored: repeated keys with VARYING
        values (a real JSON array) must not trip it, while a perfectly periodic
        stream must.
    (4) Assert the rejection threshold sits at least one max-token-length below
        the schema ceiling, computed from the real tokenizer vocabulary.
    (5) Assert the halt reaches the retry ladder as RECOVERABLE, and that a
        deterministic prompt-side refusal stays non-recoverable.
    (6) Assert the guard is wired into the PRODUCTION entry point, not only
        callable in isolation -- delete the call site and the suite must go red.
    (7) Execute the actual native EOS resolver with scalar, multiple, zero,
        nested-config and absent-generation-config cases. Assert deterministic
        normalization, configured precedence, chat-ID inclusion and nonmutation.
        Drive writer, constrained, base and polish factories at exact capacity
        with each effective terminator using the real Transformers EOS criterion.
        Drive real LMFE through an unfinished and completed JSON document: no
        EOS before completion, all effective EOS IDs allowed afterward. Change
        configuration on a warm entry and require fresh tokenizer data without
        rewriting an earlier parser's history. OTR ownership regressions are
        test_all_native_routes_stop_on_configured_or_chat_eos_at_capacity,
        test_native_eos_respects_generation_precedence_and_nested_fallback,
        test_native_eos_collection_normalization_keeps_padding_scalar and
        test_real_lmfe_uses_shared_model_chat_eos_and_refreshes_without_mutating_history.
  tags: [llm, structured-json, grammar-constrained, lm-format-enforcer, degenerate-repetition, runaway-decode, stopping-criteria, liveness, silent-truncation, tokenizer-independence, no-repeat-ngram, fail-loud, xref-11.50]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-13-writer-decode-runaway"

- id: "12.101"
  phase: 12
  area: a-key-enumerating-normalizer-silently-drops-every-field-it-does-not-name
  symptom: |
    A shipped artifact carries a null identity field on EVERY row of one kind,
    while the PARALLEL rows in the same file carry the real value. Measured:
    all eleven spoken rows of a published episode ledger said
    `speaker: null`, and the beat rows beside them named the right characters.
    Nothing crashes, nothing logs, no test goes red -- the record simply does
    not say who says each line.
    It hides for a long time because ONE downstream consumer looks correct: it
    re-derives the name from a foreign key (`char_id` -> cast table) and never
    reads the dropped field, so the rendered output is right while the record
    is wrong. Every consumer that has no such fallback -- an audit, a export,
    a later pass reasoning about who spoke -- silently gets nothing.
  cause: |
    Two normalizers exist for two parallel row types, and each rebuilds its
    rows as a literal dict that ENUMERATES the keys it keeps. The beat
    normalizer names the field; the line normalizer does not. A caller that
    supplies the field on a line row has it discarded at the boundary, so the
    upstream can be entirely correct and the value still never reaches disk.
    THE TELL IS THE ASYMMETRY, NOT THE ABSENCE. An enumerating normalizer is a
    silent allowlist: it does not fail on an unknown key, it forgets it. That
    is fine while one normalizer exists; the moment there are two for parallel
    row types, any field only ONE of them names is a field the other drops.
    In the measured case the very same method had already lost a different
    shared field this way once before -- which is what makes this a defect
    CLASS rather than a typo. A normalizer that has dropped one field will
    drop the next one too.
    The compounding factor is that the producers were ALSO not supplying the
    field, so a reviewer looking at either half alone concludes the other half
    is the bug.
  fix: |
    FIX BOTH HALVES IN ONE CHANGE, and prove the symmetry rather than the
    field.
    (a) Add the field to the silent normalizer, mirroring its sibling exactly
        -- same coercion, same empty-to-null rule. Emit the key ALWAYS, with
        an explicit null when absent, so a consumer reads a value instead of
        guessing whether the key exists.
    (b) Supply the field at EVERY assembly site, sourced from the row that
        already owns it (here: the owning beat's speaker), never re-asked of a
        model that already answered it upstream. Where a helper builds the row
        and a sibling helper builds the parallel row, have the second READ the
        first rather than take the value a second time -- then the two cannot
        disagree by construction.
    (c) Do not stop at the lane that produced the artifact. The normalizer is
        SHARED, so a lane-only fix leaves every other producer broken.
    (d) Regenerate any golden fixture the row schema pins, DELIBERATELY, and
        say in the commit that the contract changed by design.
    DO NOT "fix" it downstream with another char_id -> name fallback. A second
    re-derivation makes the hole invisible to one more consumer and leaves the
    record still wrong.
  verify: |
    Automatable, and the symmetry assertion is the one that lasts:
    (1) Feed the same identity value to BOTH sibling normalizers and assert
        they return the same answer. A test that only asserts the new field
        survives one normalizer passes again the day a third row type is added
        without it.
    (2) Assert the key is PRESENT and null when the caller omits it -- absent
        key and null value are different bugs downstream.
    (3) Statically assert both normalizers enumerate the shared field. Parse
        the module, collect the dict keys each builds, and diff the two sets:
        this is the check that goes red when someone deletes the field again.
    (4) Run the real assembly for each producing lane and assert every spoken
        row's field equals its owning parent row's field -- not merely that it
        is non-null.
    (5) Grep the sealed on-disk artifact, not the in-memory object. The
        in-memory row can be right while the normalizer that writes it is not.
  tags: [schema, normalizer, allowlist, silent-data-loss, ledger, sibling-asymmetry, golden-fixture, fail-loud, xref-12.99]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260814-01-ledger-speaker-drop"

- id: "12.102"
  phase: 12
  area: a-schema-cap-must-never-sit-at-the-number-a-downstream-trim-already-enforces
  symptom: |
    A pipeline stage dies on a RICHER-THAN-USUAL input, and the model was
    right. Measured: a news story naming 11 places and 14 institutions was
    extracted correctly by the model, and the pydantic `max_length` on those
    list fields rejected the reply three times in a row. The retry ladder
    exhausted, the run raised, and the episode was lost -- twice, on two
    separate attempts, because a re-roll cannot make a source poorer.
    The tell that it is THIS bug and not a model failure: the rejected value
    is CORRECT, the count is barely over the cap, and the error is a schema
    violation rather than a content complaint.
  cause: |
    The same number was written in two places with two different meanings.
    Downstream, a merge already trimmed each list to N deterministically --
    that is the real, intended limit. Upstream, the parse-time schema carried
    `max_length=N` as well, which is not a limit but a REFUSAL, and it fires
    FIRST. So the trim that exists to handle exactly this case can never run.
    A cap and a trim look interchangeable when you write them and are opposite
    in effect: a trim accepts the input and shortens it, a cap rejects the
    input entirely.
    It hides because it only fires on inputs richer than the cap. Every test
    fixture and every ordinary source stays under it, so the code looks
    correct for months and fails the first time reality is generous.
    Note the interaction with the decoding mode, because it changes the
    SYMPTOM completely. Under a constrained/grammar decode the array ceiling
    is enforced during generation, so the model is cut off and the artifact is
    silently SHORT. Under parse-then-validate the same ceiling REFUSES and the
    run dies. Same defect, one loud and one silent, and the silent one is
    worse.
  fix: |
    SEPARATE THE TWO NUMBERS AND SAY WHICH IS WHICH.
    (a) The schema ceiling becomes a BACKSTOP, sized well above anything a
        real input produces. Its only job is to keep a degenerate decode
        finite. It must never be reachable by legitimate content.
    (b) The LIMIT stays exactly where it was -- in the deterministic trim that
        already knew how to apply it.
    (c) SWEEP THE WHOLE MODEL, not just the field that failed. One instance of
        this is rarely the only one: the same audit found three more list
        fields in the same schema with the same cap-equals-trim collision,
        each one a live failure waiting for a richer input.
    (d) Where the trim DISCARDS content, receipt what it dropped and why, so
        the loss is auditable rather than invisible.
    DO NOT "fix" it by raising the cap to a bigger number. That moves the
    cliff instead of removing it, and leaves the same contradiction in place.
    DO NOT fix it by loosening a downstream gate to tolerate the refusal --
    that trades a real protection for an imaginary one.
  verify: |
    (1) Construct the model with EACH bounded list one item OVER its trim
        limit and assert it parses. This is the regression that matters; it
        is exactly the input that killed the run.
    (2) Assert the trim still returns the limit for that same over-long
        input, so the cap change did not widen the real contract.
    (3) Assert the backstop still refuses at its own ceiling, so the guard
        against a degenerate decode survives.
    (4) STATICALLY DIFF the two numbers: enumerate every bounded list field in
        the schema and every trim limit applied downstream, and fail if any
        cap equals its trim. This is the check that catches the next one --
        a per-field test only ever covers the fields someone remembered.
    (5) Re-run the real pipeline on the input that failed and confirm it now
        passes the stage. A unit test proves the parse; only the live run
        proves the stage.
  tags: [schema, pydantic, validation, hard-cap, silent-truncation, retry-ladder, defect-class-sweep, grammar-constrained, fail-loud, xref-12.100, xref-12.101]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-14-dossier-cap-refuses-a-rich-source"

- id: "12.103"
  phase: 12
  area: a-model-rewrite-pass-must-never-hold-authority-over-a-field-another-owner-writes-deterministically
  symptom: |
    A voiced/spoken row ships shorter than what was composed -- a deterministic,
    Python-owned fact appended verbatim by an earlier assembly step is silently
    gone from the final row, even though the interpreter that produced the fact
    was correct and the ledger still carries the original sentence elsewhere in
    meta. Measured: a closing-announcer row that should read an authored bridge
    plus a factual news coda shipped with only the bridge; the durable meta
    fields that hold the true sentence (spoken_coda_source /
    provenance_coda_line) still carry it, so the RECEIPT and the SPOKEN ROW
    disagree. Rewrite rate measured at 9 of 14 voiced rows across three
    episodes in one overnight gate.
  cause: |
    A later cleanup/"clean stage" pass judges every voiced row for "anything
    that is not speech" and hands a MODEL the rewrite of what it flags. The pass
    has no concept of a protected/content-owned span within a row -- it treats
    the row as one undifferentiated block of text to judge and rewrite, not as
    a composite of an LLM-authored bridge plus a Python-appended fact. Whatever
    the model decides is "not speech" (or simply drops during rewrite) is gone,
    and nothing downstream distinguishes "the model paraphrased this" from "the
    model deleted this." It shipped unnoticed because the pass was proven safe
    on a planted-ledger measurement rig that never runs the writer tail and
    therefore never composes a coda -- the one row shape with a protected
    component was never in the harness's input space.
  fix: |
    Mark protected/content-owned spans at the point of composition -- the same
    call that appends the deterministic fact records that the row is now a
    composite -- and make the clean/rewrite pass read that mark BEFORE it ever
    hands a row to the model: rows carrying a protected span are either
    excluded from full-row model rewrite, or the rewrite is scoped to operate
    only on the non-protected portion and the protected substring is
    re-appended byte-identically afterward.
    The marker must be ONE shared constant imported by both the emitter and the
    skip reader, and it must be stamped INDEPENDENTLY of whether the authored
    half succeeded: a flag derived from the compose outcome is decided after
    the point where the clean pass needs to already know to keep its hands off,
    so it cannot identify the row in time.
    DO NOT "fix" it by asking the model nicely to preserve facts in the prompt
    -- an instructed model is still a model, and the guarantee must be
    structural (the protected span physically cannot enter the model's edit
    surface), not persuasive.
    DO NOT fix it with a post-hoc check that only logs a warning -- the row
    must ship correct, not merely diagnosed as wrong after the fact.
    DO NOT scope the exemption by speaker role or row type: the sibling rows of
    the same role are legitimately judged, and a role-keyed exemption silently
    drops them out of the pass.
  verify: |
    (1) For any ledger whose meta carries a Python-owned coda/fact value,
    assert that value appears byte-identically at least once in the
    corresponding final row's text -- anchor on the durable meta value with a
    substring/endswith test, never on a fixed separator string, since the
    separator itself is conditional on other flags.
    (2) Feed the clean stage a synthetic row built from a bridge + protected
    coda, same shape as production, and assert the protected row is never the
    SUBJECT of a judge call -- not merely that the text came back similar.
    Similarity cannot distinguish "protected" from "rewritten into something
    close", which is why the original defect survived a green suite. Assert on
    the row under judgement, not on the whole prompt: the protected text
    legitimately appears inside a NEIGHBOURING row's context window, and
    banning it from the prompt entirely fails on correct behaviour.
    (3) Prove the fixture is not self-fulfilling: run the identical ledger
    WITHOUT the marker and assert the fact is destroyed, so the test measures
    the guard rather than an uncleanable input.
    (4) Assert the ordinary rows of the same speaker role ARE still judged, so
    the guard cannot grow into a general exemption.
    (5) Statically assert the emitter imports the shared marker constant rather
    than hardcoding its string, walking the AST so a mention in a docstring
    cannot satisfy it.
    (6) Close the fixture gap directly: extend whatever measurement rig proved
    the clean stage safe so its planted ledgers include at least one row
    carrying a protected span -- the original miss was a rig that could not
    have caught this by construction.
  tags: [content-ownership, model-rewrite, field-authority, ledger-clean, protected-span, deterministic-fact, silent-deletion, receipt-mismatch, fixture-gap, producer-consumer-constant, xref-12.49, xref-12.54, xref-12.60, xref-12.86]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260815-01-clean-stage-deletes-source-attribution"

- id: "12.104"
  phase: 12
  area: a-proof-minted-before-an-authorized-mutator-must-learn-the-mutator-runs-and-reconcile-transactionally
  symptom: |
    A finished job dies at its own integrity audit, on its own legitimate
    output, long after the expensive work completed. Measured: a content-owned
    story lane raised "line receipt mismatch for l004" 13.6 minutes in --
    script written, cast built, score composed -- and l004 was provably the
    FIRST row a sanctioned repair pass had rewritten, while the rows that pass
    left alone (l001, l002) sailed through the same audit. Nothing is corrupt
    and the repair was correct; the receipt simply describes an instant that no
    longer exists. The failure rate tracks how dirty the draft was, so the lane
    fails intermittently and a clean draft hides the defect entirely -- which
    is also why a green suite says nothing about it.
  cause: |
    An acceptance proof hashes canonical content at mint time and the audit
    re-hashes the live content later. That contract is exact only while NOTHING
    runs in between, and it was written when nothing did. Sanctioned passes
    were then inserted into the window (a model-driven repair pass, and a
    cleanup pass that can blank an item out of the covered set entirely), and
    neither was ever taught to exist by the proof -- so every legitimate edit
    reads as tampering.
    THE SNAPSHOT'S VALIDITY WINDOW IS A PIPELINE FACT, NOT A CONSTANT. It
    shortens the moment anyone inserts a stage between the mint and the check,
    and nothing in the proof's own code changes when that happens: there is no
    compile error, no failing test, and no reviewer signal. The proof keeps
    asserting a contract the pipeline quietly stopped honouring.
    The second half is a failure-mode defect rather than a proof defect: the
    audit's only verb was "raise". A proof that could not be reconciled had no
    outcome available to it except killing work that was already finished.
  fix: |
    Teach the proof that a NAMED, CLOSED SET of authorized stages may run
    inside the window, and make the record DETECTIVE rather than permissive: a
    transition receipt naming the stages, exactly which ids moved, and the
    before AND after hash of each -- so an unattributed mutation still fails
    exactly as loudly as before. Widening the audit's tolerance (fuzzy
    matching, "close enough" comparison, skipping whatever the repair pass
    touched) is the WRONG fix: it hides real corruption along with the false
    positive, and the false positive is the only part that was wrong.
    CAPTURE ONE WINDOW AROUND EVERY AUTHORIZED STAGE, never one receipt per
    stage. Per-stage chaining is impossible by construction -- the second
    stage's before-state is the first stage's output, so it can never equal the
    acceptance the chain must start from. Stamp once, from one emission point,
    and refuse a second, differing stamp.
    Bind the chain to THIS acceptance using the parent receipt's digest, not
    merely to content that happens to hash the same: ids repeat across runs, so
    before-state equality alone lets a transition be lifted from one artifact
    onto another.
    Emit NOTHING on a no-op, so an untouched artifact keeps validating through
    the unchanged path and the marker's presence always means something
    happened.
    THEN MAKE THE WINDOW A TRANSACTION. Snapshot before the first authorized
    stage; reconcile after the last one and before anything derives from the
    final content. If the reseal cannot be PROVED, restore the accepted state,
    stamp a durable degradation receipt naming the cause, and CONTINUE. The
    repair is what gets sacrificed, never the finished work.
    Snapshot the CONTAINERS, not a hand-listed set of fields. A field list goes
    stale the first time a stage learns to write one more key, and a partial
    restore leaves a state that is neither the old one nor the new one. Include
    any IN-MEMORY proof state a stage holds: a document-only copy cannot
    restore an object attribute, and a finalizer left holding post-repair
    content will fail a perfectly good rollback.
    Restore IN PLACE. Callers hold aliases to those containers; rebinding them
    leaves every later write landing in a detached copy the artifact never
    sees -- a silent hole shaped exactly like a working artifact.
    Do not leave a rolled-back stage's own telemetry behind: a repair receipt
    describing edits that no longer exist tells every downstream reader that
    items were touched which were not. Move it into the degradation receipt so
    nothing is lost and nothing lies.
    DO NOT re-run the reseal to verify the rollback. The reseal is usually the
    thing that just failed, so calling it again reports the same error and
    labels a good rollback corrupt; verify with the read-only validator
    instead.
  verify: |
    (1) Mutate one item inside the window and assert the audit PASSES and the
    artifact still carries the mutated content. The regression is the death, so
    the test must exercise the whole mint -> mutate -> audit path, not the
    receipt in isolation.
    (2) Assert the transition names EXACTLY the ids that moved, in BOTH
    directions. An under-declared change is corruption; an over-declared one is
    a receipt that names innocent items, which is an audit trail nobody can
    trust in detail.
    (3) Assert an UNATTRIBUTED mutation still raises, calling the fail-loud
    primitive directly. This is the assertion that proves the fix is not a
    widened tolerance, and it is the one a future "simplification" will try to
    delete.
    (4) Assert a no-op emits no marker and leaves the artifact byte-identical.
    (5) Force the reseal to fail and assert ALL of: no exception escapes; the
    artifact is restored to the accepted state; the read-only validator passes
    on the restored state; the degradation receipt names the cause; no
    transition marker survives; and the rolled-back stage's telemetry is gone
    from the artifact.
    (6) Corrupt the in-memory proof state after the failure and assert the
    rollback brings it back -- the failure mode a document-only snapshot cannot
    even detect.
    (7) Assert the restore keeps the SAME container objects by IDENTITY, not
    equality, so an aliasing caller keeps writing into the live artifact.
    (8) Assert END TO END that the pipeline actually OPENS the transaction: run
    the real tail with a stage stubbed to mutate an item, then validate. A
    unit-tested transaction nobody calls is dead code, and this defect's whole
    shape is a stage that shipped and ran dormant.
    (9) Cover the sibling lane that holds NO in-memory proof object. One shared
    contract must cover both, or the lane without the object silently gets no
    protection and nothing fails to say so.
  tags: [acceptance-proof, receipt-validity-window, authorized-mutation, transition-receipt, transactional-rollback, degradation-receipt, in-memory-state, container-aliasing, detective-not-permissive, no-op-emission, dead-wiring, intermittent-failure, xref-12.49, xref-12.86, xref-12.103, xref-12.105]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260815-02a-authorized-clean-window-breaks-the-acceptance-receipt"

- id: "12.105"
  phase: 12
  area: a-repair-loop-diagnostic-must-state-the-required-shape-not-only-the-offense
  symptom: |
    A bounded repair/retry ladder exhausts all attempts re-emitting the SAME
    malformed token/output, even though the ladder mechanically does carry the
    rejected draft plus its defect list into the next turn. Measured: a
    terminal-delimiter parser required a literal `END.` (with period); the
    model wrote bare `END` on every rung across four attempts, because the two
    reported defects (a bad line shape, a missing terminal marker) describe
    what is wrong with zero indication of the one-character fix that would
    satisfy them.
  cause: |
    The defect/diagnostic text a repair turn receives states the OFFENSE
    ("this line's shape is bad", "the terminal marker is missing") but never
    states the REQUIRED SHAPE (the exact accepted string or pattern). A model
    has no way to infer "add a period" from being told its output is malformed
    and missing -- those are two symptoms of one omission, not two independent
    facts it can reason its way out of. Because the repair mechanism (carrying
    the rejected draft plus defects forward) LOOKS complete -- it does feed
    context forward -- the missing ingredient (the target) is easy to miss in
    review; the ladder's plumbing is not the defect, its payload is.
    The validator half compounds it: a delimiter regex that demands ornament
    the model has no reason to supply turns one plausible output into two
    reported defects, which reads in the log as a bigger problem than the
    single missing character it is.
  fix: |
    Every defect a repair loop reports must carry, alongside what is wrong, a
    positive statement of what would be accepted -- the exact required
    literal, an example of the accepted shape, or a regex/grammar description
    a small model can pattern-match against. Where the accepted set is small
    (a fixed terminal delimiter, a fixed enum), show the literal values
    directly in the defect text, not merely the pattern that validates them.
    Give the new diagnostic its OWN helper rather than adding a branch to an
    existing repair note that answers a different question from different
    defect data -- one function producing two unrelated diagnoses teaches the
    next reader the wrong rule for their defect. Make each note self-silencing
    so a repair turn carries exactly the rules its own defects call for.
    Source the advertised literals from the validator's own grammar, and
    assert the two cannot drift: a diagnostic that names a form its validator
    rejects teaches a wrong target, which is worse than saying nothing.
    Separately, widen the validator to the forms a model actually produces --
    but only to an enumerated set. Widening a terminal delimiter into "anything
    containing END" stops it being structural, and the parser must still be
    able to tell the end of the script from a line of dialogue about endings.
  verify: |
    (1) For every accepted terminal/delimiter form the parser recognizes,
    assert the repair-turn defect text contains that literal string verbatim,
    and assert the parser accepts every form the note advertises -- a
    drift test in both directions, not just one.
    (2) Construct a fixture whose only defect is the ONE-CHARACTER gap (bare
    `END` vs `END.`) and assert the repair ladder converges within its normal
    budget once the defect text names the required literal -- this is the
    regression that matters, it is exactly the input that exhausted the ladder
    live.
    (3) Assert the note is SILENT for unrelated defects, so it cannot pad every
    repair turn with every rule until the one that matters is lost.
    (4) Content-bearing, trailing-content and unpaired-bracket variants must
    still fail loud -- the fix must not turn the validator permissive.
    (5) BUILD THE FIXTURE FROM THE REAL DEFECT RECORD, not a hand-rolled stub.
    A stub that invents an attribute name the producer never sets (a `.kind`
    string where production sets a `.code` enum) lets a helper that can never
    fire in production pass a green suite -- the test agrees with the bug.
  tags: [repair-loop, retry-ladder, diagnostic-design, required-shape, unrepairable-by-construction, markup-parser, terminal-delimiter, defect-class-sweep, test-stub-drift, xref-11.26, xref-11.35, xref-12.103]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260815-03-fable2-end-delimiter-repair-loop"
- id: "12.106"
  phase: 12
  area: a-generation-request-sized-from-a-schema-ceiling-can-exceed-the-models-entire-context
  symptom: |
    A model pass fails 100% of the time on a given model with a transport-level
    budget error naming three numbers that do not add up -- "prompt requires
    1203 input tokens, requested_output=8320, context_cap=8192 leaves only 6989
    output tokens". Measured: a story lane died at 10:18 on a live headless
    render, after the expensive upstream passes had already succeeded. The
    prompt size in the message is a RED HERRING and sends readers off trimming
    prompts: 8320 exceeds the 8192 context outright, so the request could not
    have been served by an EMPTY prompt either. The suite is green throughout.
  cause: |
    The per-call `max_new_tokens` was a CONSTANT derived from the pass's SCHEMA
    ceiling -- max_containers x max_items_per_container x tokens_per_item +
    envelope -- where the schema ceiling deliberately carries a headroom
    multiplier (here 2x) so the SCHEMA can accept more than any legal artifact
    contains. That multiplier is correct for a GUARD and wrong for a REQUEST: it
    asks for exactly N times the output any real job needs, and once the
    doubled number crosses the model's context window the pass becomes
    unsatisfiable rather than merely wasteful. Nothing in the code says
    "request" versus "ceiling", so the same expression served both roles.
    IT WAS INVISIBLE BECAUSE THE TESTS PINNED THE CONSTANT. Assertions of the
    form `assert job["max_new_tokens"] == THE_CONSTANT` cannot fail when the
    constant is impossible -- they re-assert the defect. The suite agreed with
    the bug, so only a live render could find it.
  fix: |
    Size the request to the JOB IN HAND, not to the schema maximum: derive
    `max_new_tokens` from the actual item count this call was handed
    (`len(items) * tokens_per_item + envelope`). Leave the schema's own
    max_length guard exactly where it is -- right-size the job, never raise the
    guard.
    THAT ALONE IS NOT ENOUGH, AND THIS IS THE HALF THAT GETS MISSED. If the job
    SIZE is chosen by the model, sizing the request to it only moves the same
    death behind a data condition. Measured on the same defect: the first fix
    replaced the impossible constant with a per-job request and shipped -- but
    the schema accepted twice the intended items per container AND the upstream
    prompt literally instructed the model "at most <schema ceiling> items per
    container", so a legal, actively invited draft reproduced the exact
    unfittable number. It bites below the ceiling too: at 7/8ths of it the
    request plus a measured prompt already exceeded the window.
    So BOUND THE CALL, not just the estimate: split the job into chunks that fit
    by construction, sized from the REAL topology rather than the schema
    ceiling, and keep the full context visible in every chunk so only the
    RETURN set narrows and the model still sees what it is reasoning about. In
    the common case the whole job is one chunk and the behaviour is unchanged.
    DO NOT fix it by lowering the ceiling advertised in the prompt instead. The
    schema headroom exists precisely because models overshoot what they are
    told, so an advertised limit is guidance where this must be a guarantee --
    and narrowing it changes the artifact's shape for no correctness gain.
    DO NOT silently clamp the request to the context window. A clamp returns a
    truncated artifact that then fails a downstream completeness validator with
    a misleading message; a deterministic pre-flight refusal that names the
    three numbers is information. If the right-sized job still does not fit,
    that is a real capacity problem and it should say so.
    DO NOT "fix" it by raising the model's context or swapping models -- the
    request was wrong, not the model.
    Record the LARGEST request the run actually made in the call journal instead
    of the constant. A receipt naming a number no call ever used is precisely
    how an impossible 8320 sat in every journal unread.
    Then SWEEP THE CLASS ARITHMETICALLY rather than by inspection: enumerate
    every generation budget in the package and compare each against the smallest
    shipped context window. It is a two-minute computation and it converts "we
    think this was the only one" into a measurement (it was, out of four).
  verify: |
    (1) Assert the PROPERTY, never the constant: the request equals the
    job-sized formula for the items actually passed, AND is strictly less than
    the smallest shipped context window. The second half is the assertion that
    would have caught this; the first half alone would have been rewritten to
    match the bug.
    (2) Add a static sweep over every declared generation budget asserting each
    is below that window, so a NEW pass cannot reintroduce the class.
    (3) Prove the sweep is not vacuous: feed it a deliberately impossible budget
    in a fixture and assert it fails.
    (4) Exercise the pass at its LARGEST legal job size, not a two-item toy --
    the toy fits, which is why the schedule tests never noticed. Walk EVERY size
    from one to the schema ceiling and assert each one's request still fits;
    testing only the intended size passes on a fix that leaves the defect
    reachable by a legal draft.
    (4b) Drive the real splitting function with an over-large job and assert
    three things: it split, no item was lost or reordered, and every chunk still
    carries the whole job's context. A chunker that quietly drops the tail is a
    worse bug than the one being fixed.
    (4c) Assert the ORDINARY job still takes exactly ONE call, so the split
    stays invisible in production and cannot silently multiply model spend.
    (5) When a pre-flight refusal is the correct outcome, assert the message
    names input tokens, requested output AND the cap. A budget error that
    reports only one of the three sends the next reader to the wrong file.
  tags: [token-budget, context-window, schema-ceiling-vs-request, model-controlled-job-size, chunk-to-fit, max-new-tokens, unsatisfiable-request, test-pins-the-constant, suite-agrees-with-the-bug, arithmetic-sweep, call-journal-receipt, xref-12.86, xref-12.104, xref-12.105]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260815-10-scene-review-request-exceeds-context-window"
- id: "12.107"
  phase: 12
  area: a-configured-widget-that-is-never-forwarded-is-inert-while-every-mapping-test-passes
  symptom: |
    An operator-facing control does nothing on the code path that matters, and
    nothing anywhere says so. The widget exists, its choices are correct, its
    value-to-behaviour mapping has a passing unit test, and the feature it
    controls simply never happens. Measured: a cameo knob offering "always
    include" produced episodes with no cameo for months, while the graph, the
    dropdown and the mapping dict were all correct.
    THE TELL IS A SPLIT. The control works on one path and not another: a
    legacy in-line branch read the value directly from the entry function's
    local variable, so anyone exercising THAT path saw it work, while every
    dispatched lane received a resolved-inputs dict that never carried the key.
    Which path a reader happens to test decides whether the bug appears to
    exist.
  cause: |
    The value was resolved but never FORWARDED. The entry function accepted the
    widget, a resolver built the dict every downstream consumer reads, and the
    call site between them omitted that one keyword argument. Nothing raises:
    the resolver has a default, the consumers use `.get()`, and a missing key
    is indistinguishable from a deliberate "not requested".
    IT SURVIVES UNIT TESTS BY CONSTRUCTION. The natural test asserts the
    MAPPING -- `assert FORCE_MAP["always include"] is True` -- which is a
    statement about a dict literal and stays green no matter who reads it. A
    mocked resolver is worse than no test: mocks accept any keyword, so the
    omission is invisible precisely in the test written to cover it.
    A SECOND HAZARD RIDES ALONG, and it is why the failure is silent rather
    than loud: the mapping was read with `.get()`, which returns None for an
    unknown key -- and None was ALSO the encoding for "the default behaviour".
    So a TYPO in the widget value degraded to the default while looking like an
    explicit choice, and the two states were indistinguishable downstream.
  fix: |
    Resolve the control ONCE, in the resolver, and make every consumer -- the
    legacy path included -- read it from the resolved dict rather than from the
    entry function's local. One resolution site is what makes two paths
    incapable of disagreeing.
    Validate EXACT membership at that site and FAIL LOUD on an unknown value,
    naming the legal choices. Never map unknown to the default: an
    unrecognized input and a deliberate default must not share an encoding, or
    a typo is unobservable.
    Do this BEFORE any expensive work in the resolver (network fetch, model
    load), so a mistyped widget costs a millisecond rather than a round trip.
  verify: |
    (1) Assert ARRIVAL, not mapping: call the resolver with each legal choice
    and assert the resolved dict carries the expected value under the key the
    consumers actually read. A mapping-dict assertion cannot catch this class.
    (2) Prove the FORWARDING at the call site STRUCTURALLY rather than with a
    mock -- parse the module and assert the argument appears in that call's
    keywords. A mock resolver accepts anything and passes against the defect.
    Assert there is EXACTLY ONE such call site while you are there; a second is
    a second place to forget.
    (3) Assert an unknown value RAISES and that the message names every legal
    choice, so the failure is self-describing.
    (4) Assert the default path is unchanged: omitting the control entirely
    must behave exactly as the documented default, or an old saved graph with a
    short widgets_values vector silently changes behaviour.
    (5) Where the control has a live consumer, prove it END TO END on a real
    run -- force the non-default value and assert the artifact shows the
    effect. A green unit suite proved nothing here for months.
  tags: [widget-reaches-nothing, inert-control, missing-forwarding, resolved-inputs, one-resolution-site, get-returns-none, typo-degrades-to-default, mock-hides-the-omission, ast-call-site-assertion, legacy-path-split, xref-12.86, xref-12.104]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260811-03-lemmy-cameo-widget-inert-on-dispatched-lanes"
- id: "12.108"
  phase: 12
  area: an-engine-owned-negative-prompt-vetoes-the-style-the-config-selected
  symptom: |
    A pipeline that offers selectable visual styles renders one output that
    contradicts its own setting, and does so INCONSISTENTLY inside a single
    piece of work. Measured: a 74-second episode configured as "cartoon" cut
    between an animated short, a live-action photograph and a painting --
    bookends cartoon, one character beat photoreal, a later beat painterly.
    THE TELL IS THAT THE PROMPTS ALL AGREE. Every generated positive prompt
    correctly said "bright cartoon illustration", the style config was right,
    and the images diverged anyway -- so a text-level consistency audit passes
    while the pixels disagree. Any detector that reads prompts is blind here.
    It looks like a gradient rather than a switch: where the positive was
    style-worded the style won, and where the positive was dense with other
    detail (an anatomy-heavy face description) the veto won.
  cause: |
    The NEGATIVE prompt lived in the ENGINE as a hardcoded house-style constant
    and was blind to the selected style. On a cartoon render the engine sent
    positive "bright cartoon illustration" AND negative "cartoon, illustration"
    in the same sampler call: the engine suppressed exactly what the
    configuration asked for. The constant was tuned when one look existed and
    was never re-scoped when styles became selectable.
    IT IS INVISIBLE AT cfg 1.0 AND LIVE ABOVE IT. A negative has no effect on a
    guidance-distilled engine (no unconditional branch), so the same constant
    is inert on one engine and decisive on another -- which is why it survives
    review on whichever engine the reader happens to open.
    AN AMPLIFIER TURNS ONE BAD OUTPUT INTO MANY. The engine that accepted a
    reference image propagated the first vetoed result as the identity anchor
    for every later related render, so a single mis-conditioned still became
    the look of the whole work -- consistency machinery faithfully spreading
    the wrong answer.
    A SECOND DEFECT HID INSIDE THE FIRST: the request already carried a
    per-object negative field, the dispatcher populated it, and NO engine read
    it. The value was computed, merged, carried and silently discarded, so a
    reviewer greps the field, finds it populated, and concludes it is wired.
  fix: |
    Move the STYLE half of the negative to whatever owns style -- the style
    pack/profile -- and leave the engine only an anti-ARTIFACT floor with no
    style opinion in it. The default style keeps the historical string verbatim
    so the shipped look is byte-identical and the change is provably scoped.
    COMPOSE the style negative with any per-object negative; never let one win.
    They are orthogonal axes (a look versus a subject constraint), so any
    precedence order silently drops one of them.
    Make the new field KNOWN-but-OPTIONAL, not required, when frozen records
    embed a copy of the config and verify it by hash: a required key fails
    every historical record on the missing-key path, and injecting a default to
    compensate changes the bytes and trips the hash instead.
    Resolve SELF-CONFLICT mechanically rather than by editing one string: drop
    any negative phrase that the same profile's own positive surfaces ask for.
    That holds for profiles authored later and for runtime-composed ones, so
    the class cannot return. Compare comma-separated PHRASES, not bare words --
    "plastic skin" must not be dropped because a positive wants realistic skin.
    Keep a fallback: an empty composed negative must fall back to the artifact
    floor, never to nothing, or historical records lose protection they had.
  verify: |
    (1) Assert the engine constant carries NO style vocabulary and DOES carry
    the artifact terms. This is the regression that makes the whole fix
    cosmetic if it silently returns.
    (2) For every shipped style profile, assert its negative contains no phrase
    that its own positive surfaces request. Parametrize over the profiles
    discovered on disk, not a hardcoded list, or a profile added later escapes
    the check entirely.
    (3) Assert the engine HONOURS a request-supplied negative -- pass a
    non-empty value and assert it reaches the sampler. Grepping that the field
    is populated proves nothing; the defect was that nobody read it.
    (4) Assert an EMPTY composed negative falls back to the artifact floor
    rather than to an empty string.
    (5) Assert a profile WITHOUT the optional key still validates, and that a
    non-string value is still type-rejected -- optional must not mean
    unvalidated.
    (6) Record cfg per engine alongside the negative in any receipt: a negative
    logged without its guidance scale can be accurate and still misleading,
    because below the threshold it conditioned nothing.
  tags: [engine-owned-style-constant, negative-vetoes-positive, style-blind-default, cfg-1-inert-negative, reference-image-amplifier, request-field-never-read, compose-not-precedence, known-but-optional-schema, frozen-record-hash-forbids-defaults, self-veto-resolution, phrase-not-word-matching, prompt-audit-cannot-see-pixels]
