- id: "12.109"
  phase: 12
  area: hand-built-graph-never-adopted-the-family-input-convention
  symptom: |
    An engine/adapter that builds its inference graph in code renders
    successfully forever and nothing ever goes red. No exception, no dangling
    reference, no failing test -- and, unlike a regression, no operator saying
    "it used to look better", because it never did. Output is plausible but
    subtly off: weaker prompt adherence (a named material or subject attribute
    the prompt asked for does not show up), occasional spurious text-like
    marks, and background that resolves to mush where the prompt described a
    scene. Nobody can point at a good build, because there has never been one:
    the defect has been present since the adapter's first commit.
    THE DISCOVERY IS ALWAYS ACCIDENTAL. It surfaces when somebody reads the
    host framework's own node source for an unrelated reason, not from any
    test, review or audit the project runs on itself.
  cause: |
    The host framework ships a DEDICATED node for that model family precisely
    because the family needs special input handling -- most often an
    instruction-style wrapper the checkpoint was TRAINED on (a system line plus
    a separator token, prepended to the user prompt). A hand-built graph wires
    the GENERIC node instead, so every generation runs out-of-distribution.
    Nothing complains, and cannot: the generic node is a legal, correctly typed
    producer for that slot, returns a valid conditioning, and samples cleanly.
    The convention is a TRAINING fact, not a schema fact, so no type check,
    graph validator or dead-reference scan can see it. The dedicated node's
    existence is the only written record that the convention exists at all.
    THE ASYMMETRY IS WHAT KEEPS IT HIDDEN, AND IT ALSO CREATES FALSE POSITIVES.
    For some families the wrapper is applied INSIDE the tokenizer, so the
    generic node is already correct and the dedicated node adds only unrelated
    capability. An author who checks one such family concludes the generic node
    is fine and generalizes -- and an auditor who instead flags every unused
    dedicated node reports defects that are not there.
  fix: |
    Audit CONFORMANCE AGAINST THE RUNTIME, not against your own history. Read
    the live node inventory from the running host, list the dedicated
    per-family nodes in the conditioning space, and compare that list against
    the node classes each of your hand-built graphs actually wires. Scope the
    audit to the engines actually reachable in your shipped configuration;
    auditing dormant ones spends the same effort for no live risk.
    ADJUDICATE EACH HIT BY READING THE TOKENIZER, NEVER THE NODE NAME. Open the
    family's text-encoder implementation and determine whether it applies the
    wrapper itself. Wrapper inside the tokenizer -> the generic node is correct
    and the dedicated node is not owed. No wrapper -> the caller must supply
    it, and the generic node is a live defect. Skipping this step converts the
    audit into a false-positive generator.
    Prefer COMPOSING the trained string into the generic node over swapping to
    the dedicated node when the two are otherwise equivalent -- verify that by
    reading both implementations, which are usually the same two encoder calls
    with a concatenation in front. Composing keeps ONE graph shape (the two
    node classes take different input names, so supporting both forces the
    builder to branch on which class resolved), keeps the composition unit-
    testable with no framework import, and avoids depending on the dedicated
    node being installed. Make the composition IDEMPOTENT so a double-composed
    prompt cannot double-prefix.
    Compose on EVERY conditioning branch the sampler reads, positive and
    negative alike, whenever the dedicated node has no branch-specific mode --
    wiring one of those nodes into each side is what conformance means. Check
    the guidance scale first: below the threshold where an unconditional branch
    exists, the negative conditions nothing and the question is moot.
    KEEP THE COMPOSED STRING OUT OF ANY UPSTREAM IDENTITY. If a cache key,
    content hash or derived seed is computed from the prompt, it must keep
    reading the RAW request text; compose only inside the graph builder.
    BUMP THE ENGINE/ADAPTER VERSION IN THE SAME CHANGE. The fix alters encoder
    text, not prompt text, so every prompt-derived cache input is unchanged and
    a cached pre-fix result stays valid by every test the cache applies. Without
    the bump, a resumed job re-serves the out-of-distribution output forever and
    the fix is silently retroactive to nothing.
  verify: |
    (1) Assert the composed string is BYTE-IDENTICAL to what the host's
    dedicated node builds, with the framework's own system/wrapper constants
    reproduced verbatim in the assertion. This test is the drift detector: it
    is the only thing that will notice when the upstream constants change.
    (2) Assert composition on BOTH the positive and negative branches, and that
    each carries exactly ONE separator token.
    (3) Assert idempotence -- composing an already-composed string returns it
    unchanged -- and cover the empty-prompt edge, which must match what the
    dedicated node emits for empty input rather than being special-cased away.
    (4) Assert the raw request text survives in the params/request the
    dispatcher hashes, so cache keys and derived seeds provably do not move.
    (5) Assert the adapter version changed, or pin the cache key over a
    known input pair so a silent revert fails.
    (6) A/B ON PIXELS AT A FIXED SEED, and keep the pre-fix arm as a permanent
    flag on the smoke harness rather than deleting it. Structural divergence is
    not proof the output improved; this class is visible only in comparison.
    Treat a single pair as evidence of MATERIAL CHANGE and direction, never as
    a quality measurement across the lane.
    (7) Keep any harness that claims to mirror the engine recipe importing the
    real composition function rather than re-typing the string, or the mirror
    quietly stops being one.
  tags: [hand-built-graph, dedicated-family-node-unused, trained-input-convention, out-of-distribution-forever, no-good-build-to-diff, tokenizer-self-wrap-discriminator, false-positive-audit, compose-over-node-swap, idempotent-prompt-composition, raw-text-feeds-cache-key, adapter-version-bump, fixed-seed-pixel-ab, harness-must-import-not-retype]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260817-02-lumina-engine-skipped-trained-prompt-start-convention"
- id: "11.61"
  phase: 11
  area: two-naming-authorities-let-an-upstream-plan-name-leak-into-an-assigned-record
  symptom: |
    A per-record generated field describes the WRONG PERSON. The record's own
    identity is replaced by a name from an earlier planning artifact, and that
    other person's attributes follow it -- face, build, bearing, delivery, the
    whole portrait. Measured: a three-row cast in which two rows were
    contaminated, one of them reading "Late 50s, Seasoned yet vulnerable
    LUCILLE PENNY. Face: ... Voice: warm, calm urgency, like a mother in a
    storm" on a row named RICK STEINER and typed male. Across the archive, 28
    rows in 20 of the 124 record sets that carry an upstream named cast -- 16%
    -- and the most recent was produced the day before the sweep, so this is
    live behaviour and not a retired regime.
    Nothing errors and nothing is malformed. The field is non-empty, on-format,
    schema-valid and well written; it is simply about somebody else. The
    contaminated string is then copied VERBATIM into the downstream image
    prompt, so the picture is painted of the other person too, and the wrong
    record ships to every consumer that reads either surface.
  cause: |
    TWO AUTHORITIES NAME THE SAME ENTITY, and the pass that must choose between
    them is never told which one wins. An upstream generative planning step
    (pitch, brief, treatment, outline) invents entity names as part of its
    prose. A downstream deterministic assigner then overrides those names with
    its own -- pool draws, roster rows, ids. The per-record generation prompt
    is handed BOTH: the upstream text goes in as story context, the assigned
    name goes in as a labelled field, and no precedence is stated anywhere.
    The template makes it easy to get wrong. A format string of the shape
    "<age band>, <story-linked role>. <attributes>" reserves a free-text slot
    immediately after the age band, and when the surrounding context keeps
    naming people, a NAME is an entirely plausible filler for "story-linked
    role". The model is not malfunctioning; it is resolving an ambiguity the
    prompt created, in the direction the context weights.
    It survives every guard because it is a SEMANTIC mismatch wearing correct
    syntax: name-shape validators pass (it IS a well-formed name), length and
    schema validators pass, non-empty passes, and any per-field test passes.
    It also hides behind adjacent audits: this was found only because a GENDER
    audit tripped on a female-coded name inside a male-typed row, and had it
    been repaired as the gender defect it appeared to be, the wrong-person
    prose would have survived with the gender cue sanded off -- a visible
    defect converted into an invisible one.
  fix: |
    Declare ONE naming authority and enforce it at the boundary, not in the
    prompt. Decide which pass owns entity names. If the downstream assigner
    owns them, then every upstream text that will be shown to a later pass must
    have the superseded names REWRITTEN to the assigned ones (or removed)
    before it enters any per-record prompt. Reconcile the artifact; do not ask
    the model to arbitrate between two authorities you handed it.
    DO NOT fix it by instructing the model harder. "Use the Name field, not
    names in the story" is persuasion, and an instructed model is still a
    model; the guarantee has to be structural -- the conflicting name must not
    be in the context, or the output must be checked after.
    DO NOT fix it with fuzzy name repair. The reflex fix for stray names in
    generated text -- match them to the nearest roster entry and rewrite -- is
    ACTIVELY HARMFUL here: it rewrites the foreign name to the record's own and
    leaves that other person's face, bearing and delivery prose in place. The
    record still describes the wrong human and now nothing points at it.
    The structural gate is an ENSEMBLE-SCOPED FOREIGN-NAME CHECK: reject any
    record whose generated field contains a proper name that NO record in the
    ensemble owns. Scope matters and both obvious variants are wrong. "No
    record may name ANOTHER record" is wrong in both directions -- it flags
    correct relational prose ("foil to the Time Traveler", "Rosalind's
    confidante"), which is legitimate and desirable, and it MISSES this defect
    entirely, because the leaked name belongs to no record at all. On the
    corpus that produced this entry the sibling-name variant returned 47 hits
    of which roughly 45 were correct relational writing, and it did not flag
    the record the bug was reported from.
    Carry the repair to every DERIVED surface in the same change. The
    contaminated string had already been copied into the image prompt, so a fix
    that repairs only the source field leaves the rendered artifact wrong and
    the two surfaces disagreeing.
  verify: |
    (1) Sweep the ARCHIVE, not only new runs. For every record set that carries
    both an upstream named cast and an assigned roster, assert no generated
    field contains a name from the upstream cast that no roster row owns. This
    class ships silently for months, so a forward-only check measures nothing.
    (2) Pin the known-bad artifact and require the check to flag EXACTLY the
    contaminated records in it -- not the set, the rows. A detector that only
    reports "this episode is dirty" cannot be used to repair.
    (3) Prove the naive variant is not the fix: assert the sibling-name check
    passes on a legitimate relational description AND fails to flag the
    known-bad record. Without this, the cheap check gets adopted and the defect
    keeps shipping under a green gate.
    (4) Assert the DERIVED surface as well as the source field. Anywhere the
    generated text is copied verbatim downstream, the same assertion applies to
    the copy, or a green source check certifies a wrong artifact.
    (5) Prove the guard fires: disable the name reconciliation and require the
    sweep to go red. A check that stays green with the reconciler removed is
    measuring the generator, not the guard.
    (6) Statically assert the boundary. The function that builds the per-record
    prompt must receive reconciled upstream text -- walk to the call site and
    assert the raw upstream field is not what gets inlined, so a later refactor
    cannot quietly restore the two-authority prompt.
  tags: [llm, prompt-authority, two-naming-authorities, entity-identity, cross-record-contamination, upstream-plan-vs-assigner, free-text-slot, semantic-mismatch-correct-syntax, ensemble-scoped-check, relational-mention-false-positive, fuzzy-repair-launders-the-defect, derived-surface, archive-sweep, adjacent-audit-found-it, xref-10.03, xref-10.08, xref-12.103]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260817-03-upstream-plan-name-pasted-into-assigned-character-record"

- id: "12.110"
  phase: 12
  area: one-field-two-meanings-gate-the-consumer-on-the-lane-not-on-truthiness
  symptom: |
    A field is populated, correctly, on every lane -- and a consumer that reads
    it produces nonsense on some of them. Nothing is null, nothing is
    malformed, no schema complains, and the value is genuinely right for the
    lane that wrote it. Measured: a field naming "the work this episode adapts"
    was threaded to a narrator so it could announce the work by name. On the
    adaptation lanes it holds a play title and the announcement is correct. On a
    different lane the same field holds the PUBLICATION a post came from, so the
    narrator would have announced a scene from a magazine that has no scenes --
    on 56 of 98 live records, 57% of that lane. The consumer was new; the field
    and both its meanings had been correct and shipping for months.
  cause: |
    ONE FIELD ACQUIRED TWO MEANINGS, one per lane, and each was reasonable when
    it was added. The extractor has a per-lane branch -- lane A maps its title
    into the field, lane B maps its publisher into the same field -- and the
    field name describes only the first meaning. Nothing is wrong until a
    consumer arrives that means specifically the FIRST meaning and tests the
    field the obvious way: `if value:`. Truthiness cannot distinguish two
    meanings; it only distinguishes present from absent, and both meanings are
    present.
    The trap is that the field's own name argues for the mistake. A reviewer
    reading `work_title` reasonably concludes it holds the work's title, and the
    second meaning is visible only in the extractor's other branch, which nobody
    reads when wiring a new consumer.
    It also survives review by construction: a static reviewer that follows the
    producer finds the field always populated and declares the lane safe -- the
    error is not in either branch, it is in the ASSUMPTION that one name means
    one thing.
  fix: |
    GATE THE CONSUMER ON THE LANE, NEVER ON TRUTHINESS. Export the set of lanes
    for which the consumer's meaning actually holds, as a NAMED CONSTANT beside
    the extractor that creates the ambiguity, and have the consumer test
    membership in that set instead of testing the value. The name of the
    constant carries the distinction the field name could not.
    Record the MEASUREMENT beside the constant -- how many live records carry
    the other meaning -- so the next reader learns the field is overloaded
    without having to rediscover it.
    Do NOT rename or split the field as the first move if other consumers
    already read it correctly for their own lane: both meanings are load-bearing
    and a rename is a wider blast radius than the defect. Splitting is right
    only when a second consumer needs the second meaning explicitly.
    Do NOT special-case by matching the value's shape or content -- that is
    name-matching, it ships false positives, and it breaks the moment either
    lane's data changes.
  verify: |
    (1) Assert the gate is a MEMBERSHIP test against the exported constant, not
    a truthiness test on the value. Walk the AST if necessary -- an `if value:`
    reintroduced later looks harmless in review.
    (2) POSITIVE CONTROL, and this is the one that matters: assert a record from
    a lane where the meaning DOES hold passes the gate. Without it the gate is
    satisfied by a predicate that is false for everything, which is green and
    useless.
    (3) NEGATIVE CONTROL from real data: construct the other lane's record with
    its field populated, and assert the consumer declines it. Use the actual
    shape from the corpus, not an invented one -- the defect is that the value
    looks legitimate.
    (4) Sweep the corpus for how many records carry the second meaning and put
    that number in the test's message, so a future reader sees the blast radius
    without re-measuring.
    (5) Prove the consumer end to end on a real run of BOTH lanes. A static
    reviewer cleared the affected lane here by reading the producer module and
    finding nothing that wrote the field; the live records disagreed, and only
    the artifact settled it.
  tags: [overloaded-field, per-lane-meaning, truthiness-gate, named-constant, positive-control, static-audit-cannot-prove-behaviour, corpus-measurement, xref-11.61, xref-12.89]
  legacy_id: "OTR-item-F-work-title-means-two-things-across-lanes"
- id: "12.111"
  phase: 12
  area: an-instrument-that-overwrites-the-evidence-its-own-record-cites-by-hash
  symptom: |
    A qualification, approval or acceptance record cites an artifact BY SHA256 --
    a manifest, a key, a set of clips -- and the tool that produced that artifact
    writes to the same path every time it runs. Re-running it to refresh the
    evidence destroys the bytes the existing record names, so the record now
    points at a hash nothing on disk has. Nobody sees an error: the re-run is
    green, the files are present, and the citation is silently unverifiable.
    Measured: a voice-route qualification cited
    `<audition_dir>/MANIFEST.json` by sha256; the audition script defaulted to
    that directory and rewrote it. A second instrument in the same repo wrote a
    manifest cited by sha256 in THREE separate provisional records with no
    overwrite guard at all.
    The near-miss variant is worse than the miss: a re-run under CHANGED code
    can also produce BYTE-IDENTICAL artifacts, which looks like confirmation and
    is actually proof of nothing -- the instrument was bypassing the very code
    path that changed.
  cause: |
    THE INSTRUMENT AND THE RECORD HAVE DIFFERENT LIFETIMES AND NOBODY SAID SO.
    An audition script is written to be re-run; a qualification record is written
    to be permanent. The moment a record cites the script's output by hash, that
    output became an append-only archive, and the script's default output path
    became a destructive one. Nothing in either file states the dependency.
    A partial guard reads as a complete one. Refusing only when a specific file
    (`MANIFEST.json`) exists leaves every sibling artifact unprotected: the WAVs
    in a manifest-less directory, and -- the one that actually matters -- the
    separate `_KEY` directory holding the blinding map, which is what says which
    arm a human approved. A guard that names one filename teaches readers the
    hazard is handled.
  fix: |
    Make the instrument REFUSE to write into any non-empty output location, and
    make "output location" include every directory it touches, not just the one
    holding the file you thought of. Reject a path that exists and is not a
    directory; reject a directory that has any entry in it.
    Render to a NEW, dated or otherwise distinct directory per run, and let the
    record cite that directory. A new run is a new artifact; refreshing evidence
    in place is never the operation you want.
    Put the runtime INSIDE the artifact. A manifest a record cites by hash must
    carry the facts the record claims -- the live code fingerprint, the resolved
    parameters PER ARM, the reference id and its hash -- so the citation can be
    checked without trusting a second file that may have moved on.
    Do NOT solve this with a backup-before-overwrite step, a timestamp suffix
    bolted on inside the writer, or a `--force` that defaults on. The first two
    still destroy the cited path; the third makes the safe case opt-in.
  verify: |
    (1) Run the instrument twice against the SAME output directory and assert
    the second run exits non-zero and writes nothing. Assert on the exit code
    and on file mtimes, not on stdout text.
    (2) Assert the refusal covers a directory containing ONLY the sibling
    artifacts -- no manifest present, just clips -- and separately a
    key/secondary directory that exists while the primary does not. These are
    the two states a partial guard lets through.
    (3) Grep the repo for every writer of the cited filename and check each one
    has the guard. The defect class travels: fixing the instrument that bit you
    while its sibling stays unguarded is how the same outage recurs under a
    different name.
    (4) Re-hash every artifact a record cites and assert it still matches. This
    is the cheap standing check that catches a citation that has already rotted.
    (5) Prove that a re-run under CHANGED code produces DIFFERENT bytes. If the
    artifacts are byte-identical after a real code change, the instrument is
    bypassing the changed path and its output cannot qualify anything -- assert
    the instrument drives the production entry point, not a private shortcut.
  tags: [evidence-by-hash, append-only-artifact, overwrite-guard, partial-guard-reads-as-complete, sibling-instrument, audition-record, provenance-in-artifact, byte-identical-is-not-confirmation]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-18-audition-overwrites-cited-evidence"
- id: "12.112"
  phase: 12
  area: sequential-arm-loop-must-reset-total-state-not-assign-a-delta
  symptom: |
    A test harness or audition instrument runs N configurations ("arms") in
    sequence inside ONE process, blinded so the order is randomized. One arm
    means "use the defaults" and is written as an empty or partial override
    dict -- it names no keys, meaning to inherit whatever the code's own
    defaults are. Instead it silently inherits whatever the PREVIOUS arm in
    the shuffled order left set in process-global state (an env var, a module
    global, a singleton config). Because the shuffle decides which arm runs
    first, the contamination is non-deterministic across runs of the same
    harness -- it does not even fail the same way twice.
    Measured: a blinded audition rendered a "shipped defaults" arm
    immediately after a "ceiling disabled" control arm. The shipped arm's
    resolved settings matched the control's leftover value, not the code's
    actual defaults, and the qualification record built from that arm would
    have certified a build that was never rendered.
  cause: |
    The per-arm loop ASSIGNS only the keys the current arm's dict names:
    `for key, value in arm_env.items(): os.environ[key] = value`. An arm
    meaning "inherit the defaults" is naturally written by OMITTING those
    keys from its dict -- and omission does nothing to a key a PRIOR
    iteration already set. The loop is incremental (only ADDs), while the
    intended semantics are total (each arm's environment should be exactly
    what its own dict says, no more).
    This is invisible in code review because each arm's dict, read on its
    own, looks correct and complete -- the bug is only visible against the
    EXECUTION ORDER, which is exactly the thing a blinded/shuffled harness
    deliberately hides from the reader.
  fix: |
    Before applying an arm's overrides, POP every key any arm in the set is
    allowed to control -- not just the keys THIS arm happens to name -- then
    apply this arm's overrides on top of the now-clean state. Maintain the
    full set of controlled keys as one explicit constant the loop reads, so
    adding a new controllable variable to any arm's dict is impossible without
    also adding it to the reset list.
    Do NOT fix this by reordering arms, by re-running a specific arm first, or
    by memoizing "the previous arm's settings" to diff against -- all three
    leave the underlying incremental-assignment bug in place for the next
    added arm or the next shuffle seed.
  verify: |
    (1) Run the harness with a FIXED, non-default shuffle order that places
    the "inherit defaults" arm immediately after an arm with a NON-default
    override, and assert the inheriting arm's resolved/observed settings
    equal the code's actual defaults, not the preceding arm's values.
    (2) Repeat with the reverse order (defaults arm first) and assert the
    SAME result -- order-independence is the actual property being protected,
    not just one ordering.
    (3) If the harness records what it resolved (a manifest, a receipt), assert
    the recorded value for each arm matches what a fresh, isolated process
    would resolve for that arm's dict alone.
    (4) Grep every dict in the arm set for keys used by ANY arm; assert the
    reset list is a superset. A newly added controllable key that is not in
    the reset list reintroduces the bug silently.
  tags: [sequential-state, blinded-harness, env-contamination, incremental-vs-total-reset, shuffle-order-dependent, non-deterministic-across-runs, audition-instrument]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-18-audition-arm-env-contamination"
- id: "12.113"
  phase: 12
  area: a-downstream-passs-finished-output-fed-back-as-context-to-an-earlier-pass-forbidden-from-producing-it
  symptom: |
    A multi-pass LLM pipeline runs pass B (produces a short, factual,
    validated field) BEFORE pass A (writes a large free-text document, e.g.
    a whole script), then folds B's finished OUTPUT into A's INPUT context
    because A's prompt happens to serialize "everything known so far." A's
    system prompt separately instructs it never to produce content of B's
    kind ("a separate factual note is appended by the producer, you do not
    write it") -- but A can now SEE that exact content sitting in its own
    context. A small local model does not reliably honor "do not repeat this"
    when the thing not to repeat is right there to copy.
    Measured: pass A's output (a fictional bridge line) echoed pass B's
    output (a real-world fact) almost verbatim, word for word on the shared
    clause, even though pass A's prompt explicitly forbade stating any
    real-world fact.
  cause: |
    The prompt-assembly code does the generically correct thing --
    "serialize the full accumulating state object into the next prompt" --
    without asking whether every field in that state object is something
    THIS prompt wants the model to see. A field that is a LATER pass's
    finished, protected output looks, to the generic serializer, identical to
    every other field the model legitimately needs (cast, setting, premise).
    The negative instruction ("never state X") and the positive leak (X is
    sitting right there) are added independently, by different code paths, so
    nothing forces them to be checked against each other. A reviewer reading
    either the prompt TEXT or the state-passing CODE in isolation sees nothing
    wrong; the contradiction only exists in their combination at runtime.
  fix: |
    At the ONE call site that builds the forbidden pass's prompt, explicitly
    EXCLUDE the field(s) the model must not see from the serialized state
    (e.g. `model.model_dump(exclude={"the_field"})`), rather than trying to
    make the model resist a temptation you chose to put in front of it. Leave
    the field intact on the state object for every OTHER consumer (downstream
    assembly, receipts, audits) -- the exclusion is scoped to the one prompt
    that must not see it, not a deletion.
    Do NOT rely on strengthening the "never state X" instruction alone
    (already the first fix reached for, and it does not hold under a small
    local model once X is visible in context); do NOT rely on a downstream
    parser catching the leak after the fact (structural grammar checks do not
    inspect semantic content, so a leak of this shape parses as perfectly
    valid output).
  verify: |
    (1) Unit test: build the forbidden pass's prompt with a DISTINCTIVE marker
    string planted in the field that must be excluded, and assert the marker
    never appears anywhere in the built prompt.
    (2) Assert every OTHER field on the same state object DOES still reach the
    prompt, so the exclusion is proven scoped rather than accidentally wiping
    the whole object.
    (3) Assert the empty/not-yet-populated case (the excluded field's default)
    produces a byte-identical prompt to before the fix, so passes that never
    populate the field are unaffected.
    (4) On a live render, diff the forbidden pass's actual output against the
    other pass's output for shared substrings above a trivial length -- an
    echoed phrase is the concrete, observable symptom, not an inference from
    reading the prompt.
  tags: [prompt-contamination, pipeline-ordering, state-object-serialization, small-local-model, negative-instruction-not-sufficient, context-exclusion, live-echo-symptom, multi-pass-llm]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-18-scifi-news-pro-coda-leaks-the-news-read"
- id: "12.114"
  phase: 12
  area: a-reserved-identity-asset-is-reachable-from-the-general-selection-pool
  symptom: |
    An asset that MEANS one specific named identity -- a signature voice, a
    signature portrait, a character's theme, a branded style -- is handed to an
    ordinary subject who is not that identity. Nothing errors: the asset is
    valid, the selection is deterministic and auditable, and the output plays or
    renders normally. The damage is semantic and cumulative: an asset can only
    mean "this is X" while it is the only place it is ever encountered, so every
    wrong assignment quietly spends the identity that a qualification, audition
    or design decision established.
    Measured: a cameo character's operator-qualified voice reference was
    assigned to an ordinary character in an episode whose cast contained no such
    cameo, and that character spoke in the cameo's signature accent for the whole
    episode. The reference simply sat in the general character pool like any
    other row, so the seeded draw could hand it to anybody.
  cause: |
    THE RESERVATION EXISTS AS A CONVENTION, NOT AS A FILTER. The asset was
    qualified FOR one identity in one subsystem (a route, a policy, a casting
    record), and a DIFFERENT subsystem -- the general selector -- enumerates the
    same catalogue and never learns that some rows are spoken for. Both are
    individually correct; the coupling between them was never written down.
    The near-miss shape is a hand-kept exclusion list. It works the day it is
    written and drifts the first time somebody adds a route, because the list
    and the thing it describes have no mechanical relationship.
    The over-correction is equally real: reserving EVERY asset the identity is
    associated with sweeps in shared catalogue entries the identity merely
    borrows, and pulling those out of the general pool trades one defect for
    another (a preferred announcer voice, a house style, a stock portrait).
    THE DEEPER CAUSE, AND THE ONE THAT SURVIVED THE FIRST FIX: the guard was
    installed at ONE chokepoint -- the deterministic selector -- while production
    reached the same assignment through a SECOND path that never touches it.
    Measured: an LLM-proposed assignment was validated and stamped directly,
    skipping the selector entirely on 1871 of 1953 assignments (82 fell back).
    The scoring selector everyone reads as "the code that assigns voices" was
    running on 4% of real traffic. Neither the proposal builder nor its validator
    knew reserved ids existed, so the leak walked around a guard that was, in its
    own path, working perfectly.
    AND THERE WAS A THIRD PATH, WHICH THE FIRST FIX ALSO MISSED: the
    gender-agnostic FALLBACK used when the normal lookup cannot serve a row. It
    drew UNIFORMLY with no reserved filter and no quality filter at all -- a
    fallback is written to always return something, so it tends to be authored
    with fewer guards than the path it backs up, not more. What made it high
    traffic rather than a rare edge: a canonical category ("other") is 20% of
    every roll and the catalogue carries NO rows for it, so every one of those
    rows fell through to the unfiltered fallback. A category with no inventory
    silently promotes a fallback into a main path.
  fix: |
    Derive the reserved set FROM the authority that grants the identity, and
    apply it as a deterministic PRE-FILTER on the candidate pool -- never a score
    reweight, which only makes the wrong answer less likely rather than
    impossible.
    Reserve only assets the identity OWNS (a clone of its own recording, a
    bespoke asset), never the shared catalogue entries it is merely pointed at.
    Distinguish those by the KIND recorded in the grant, not by name matching.
    Make the reservation free to extend: adding a new grant must reserve its
    asset with no second edit, because a list maintained in parallel is a list
    that drifts.
    Fail SOFT in the helper (an unreadable authority reserves nothing and
    selection behaves exactly as before) and keep strictness where the grant is
    claimed. A helper that raises breaks every render on an import error.
    Confirm the reserved identity can still receive its own asset: the grant path
    must stamp it directly, never come through the filtered selector, or the fix
    starves the identity of the very thing being protected.
    ENUMERATE EVERY PATH THAT CAN ASSIGN THE ASSET BEFORE CHOOSING WHERE THE
    GUARD GOES, and put it on all of them or at the join below them. A guard on
    the path you happened to read is not a guard on the system. Grep for every
    writer of the assignment field, not just the function whose name says it
    selects -- an LLM-proposed or cached or operator-pinned assignment typically
    validates and stamps on its own path, and a validator that does not know
    about reservations will happily approve a reserved id.
  verify: |
    (1) Sweep the SELECTOR, not the helper. Assert across many seeds and every
    demographic branch that no reserved asset is ever returned for an ordinary
    subject. Tests that only check the reserved LIST prove the list is right and
    say nothing about the pool, which is where the defect lives.
    (1b) Count the traffic on each path before trusting any of them. The path
    whose name says it selects may carry a small minority of real assignments;
    measure from the corpus, not from reading the code. And test the FALLBACK
    explicitly -- it is the path least likely to carry the guard and most likely
    to be reached by a category the catalogue cannot serve.
    (2) Prove the test has teeth: assert the reserved asset IS present in the
    unfiltered candidate pool. If it is absent for some unrelated reason, the
    sweep passes for the wrong reason and keeps passing if the guard is deleted.
    (3) Assert the reserved identity still gets its own asset through the grant
    path, so the guard cannot starve what it protects.
    (4) Assert the shared/borrowed assets are NOT reserved, pinning the
    over-correction.
    (5) Count the corpus by ROLE before concluding anything about concentration.
    A pinned role (an announcer, a narrator) legitimately repeats one asset in
    every output and will dominate a naive frequency count, hiding or faking a
    pool problem in the rows that actually vary.
    (6) A POST-FIX SIGHTING HAS TWO EXPLANATIONS AND THEY LOOK IDENTICAL FROM
    THE OUTSIDE: a STALE PROCESS still running the pre-fix module, or an
    UNCOVERED PATH the fix never touched. Measured: the defect reappeared SIXTEEN
    HOURS after a correct fix, and the first diagnosis -- a long-running server
    that never tears down, holding the old import -- was plausible, consistent
    with the timeline, and WRONG. The real cause was a second assignment path
    that skipped the guarded selector entirely.
    Tell them apart by REPRODUCING ON A FRESHLY IMPORTED MODULE through the path
    production actually takes. A live call at HEAD either reproduces it (uncovered
    path -- the fix is incomplete) or does not (stale process -- the fix is fine).
    Do not settle this by reasoning about process start times against commit
    timestamps: that argument is available for both explanations and selects the
    comfortable one. Corpus silence is not proof either -- a defect at a ~1%
    assignment rate is absent from most samples while remaining fully live.
  tags: [reserved-identity, general-pool-leak, deterministic-pre-filter, derived-not-hand-kept, owns-versus-borrows, selector-not-helper, test-has-teeth, role-aware-corpus-count, uncovered-second-path, guard-at-one-chokepoint, unguarded-fallback-path, empty-category-routes-to-fallback, post-fix-sighting-two-explanations, reproduce-on-fresh-import, low-rate-defect-hides-in-corpus]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-17-lemmy-cameo-voice-cast-on-ordinary-character"
- id: "12.115"
  phase: 12
  area: a-live-feed-is-consumed-at-a-constant-index-so-the-newest-item-is-adapted-forever
  symptom: >-
    A lane that reads a live feed (RSS/Atom, an API listing, a directory scan)
    produces output about the SAME source item run after run, indefinitely.
    Nothing errors: the fetch succeeds, the feed is healthy, entries are fresh,
    and every artifact is well-formed. The repetition is only visible to a human
    who watches several outputs in a row and notices the subject never changes,
    which is why it survives green pipelines for weeks. Reading the code makes it
    look intentional -- there IS a selection expression, it just cannot vary.
  cause: >-
    SELECTION IS PARAMETERISED BUT THE PARAMETER NEVER MOVES. The selector is
    something like `entries[configured_index() % len(entries)]`, where the index
    comes from config or an environment variable and DEFAULTS TO 0. Feeds are
    conventionally newest-first, so the default silently means "always the newest
    item". There is no history, no dedup, no ranking, and nothing per-run in
    scope: the fetcher's signature often has no seed, no run id and no episode
    identity at all, so it is not merely unused variety -- the function has no
    input that could vary even in principle.
    A sibling lane in the same codebase frequently already solved this and the
    two were never joined up, so the defect is not "nobody built dedup" but
    "dedup exists one module over and this consumer does not use it".
  fix: >-
    Give the selector a reason to move, and prefer REUSING the mechanism a
    sibling lane already has over writing a second one -- a second history is a
    second thing to drift. A shared used-item record keyed on a stable identity
    (URL, guid, content hash) with a rolling cap and a TTL is enough: the TTL
    lets an item recycle once it has aged out, so the pool does not exhaust.
    Order the precedence deliberately and write it down: (a) an EXPLICIT operator
    override wins outright, because dedup must not fight a human who named an
    item; (b) otherwise prefer items not in the recent record; (c) otherwise fall
    back to the full list rather than raising -- a repeat is worse than nothing,
    but failing the run is worse still. Keep the whole mechanism ADVISORY: any
    failure reading or writing the record must leave selection exactly as it was,
    because a feed lane must never fail a render over a state file. Do NOT record
    the override path's choice; an explicit index is a deliberate repeat, and
    recording it lets a debugging run consume an item for the automatic path.
    Sharing one record across lanes is only free while their sources cannot
    collide -- assert that, do not assume it.
  verify: >-
    (1) THE CORE CHECK IS TWO CONSECUTIVE RUNS AGAINST A STABLE FEED SELECTING
    DIFFERENT ITEMS. Stub the feed so no network is involved.
    (2) Prove it walks the whole feed before repeating. Two runs differing is
    also satisfied by alternating between two items forever; assert N runs over
    an N-item feed yield N distinct selections.
    (3) TEST THE OVERRIDE GATE ON PARSEABILITY, NOT ON EMPTINESS -- this is the
    hole most likely to survive the fix, and it silently restores the exact
    default the fix removed. If the code asks "was the variable set?" by testing
    for a NON-EMPTY string, then a junk value ("abc", "2.5", "-") takes the
    override branch, the index helper swallows its own ValueError and returns 0,
    and the lane is back to always-newest with no error and no log line.
    Separate "did the operator choose?" from "which index should I use?" as two
    functions, warn on an unusable value, and parametrize the test over several
    kinds of junk.
    (4) Assert an exhausted record FALLS BACK rather than raising, and that a
    record that fails to load or save does not block the run.
    (5) Assert the record stays SHARED with the sibling lane rather than becoming
    a private copy -- inspect the source for the shared loader AND recorder.
    Reading a history nothing writes to is a no-op that looks like a feature, so
    check both halves.
    (6) If two lanes share one record, PIN THAT THEIR SOURCES CANNOT COLLIDE
    (disjoint feed domains, disjoint id namespaces) with a test that fails when
    the source lists start to overlap -- that is the moment sharing stops being
    free and one lane can consume an item out from under the other.
    (7) Grep for a test on the selection path before assuming one exists. This
    class is usually found in a module whose selector had NO coverage at all.
  tags: [feed-selection, constant-index-default, newest-first-convention, shared-used-record, ttl-recycles-pool, advisory-never-blocking, override-parseable-not-nonempty, silent-default-restoration, reuse-sibling-mechanism, disjoint-sources-assertion, untested-selection-path]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-15-media-archive-retells-newest-post-forever"
- id: "12.116"
  phase: 12
  area: an-exposed-widget-the-body-never-reads-whose-default-equals-the-hardcode-it-lost-to
  symptom: |
    A node exposes a control with a plausible range and a tooltip that promises
    what it does. Moving it changes nothing. No error, no warning, no log line
    saying the value was ignored -- every setting produces byte-identical
    output. Measured: an audio node offered `normalize_dbfs` from -12.0 to 0.0
    tooltipped "Peak normalization target dBFS", and the rendered episode was
    identical at every value.
    THIS IS NOT THE FORWARDING GAP OF 12.107. There the value never reached
    the consumer. Here the plumbing is COMPLETE: INPUT_TYPES declares it, the
    entry function accepts it by name, and the function BODY simply never
    references the parameter. Every wiring test passes because the wiring is
    genuinely correct.
  cause: |
    The work the control names was moved elsewhere and the control was left
    behind. In the measured case a comment in the body recorded the move --
    "peak normalize skipped, moved to EpisodeAssembler" -- while the widget
    stayed on the original node, and the real value became a hardcoded default
    in the function that inherited the work.
    THE REASON IT SURVIVED FOR MONTHS IS THE PART WORTH LEARNING, AND IT
    GENERALISES: the saved/default value of the dead control was EQUAL to the
    hardcode that superseded it. Both were -1.0. So the one configuration
    anybody ever shipped is precisely the configuration in which the defect
    produces no observable difference. A defect whose only invisible case is
    the default case cannot be found by using the product; it can only be found
    by reading the body or by deliberately moving the control and diffing.
    A rationalising comment can also form on the dead parameter -- here, that
    it was "consumed by the graph runtime, not the body" -- which reads as an
    explanation and is not one.
  verify: |
    AST, no runtime needed. For each node class, take every key of
    INPUT_TYPES (required + optional) and each parameter name of the execution
    method; for any name present in BOTH, assert the method body contains at
    least one Load of that name. A parameter that is declared, accepted, and
    never loaded is inert. Report it even when the default "looks right" --
    especially then, because a default equal to the superseding constant is
    what makes it invisible.
    Second, cheaper check for the same family: grep the body for a comment
    saying the work moved ("moved to", "deferred to", "now handled by") and
    confirm the control that named that work moved with it.
  fix: |
    Decide which of the two the control is, and never leave it a third thing:
    (1) WIRE IT -- pass the value through to whatever now does the work, so the
    tooltip becomes true. Prefer this when the knob is genuinely wanted; at the
    default value the output is unchanged, so it ships without altering any
    existing product.
    (2) REMOVE IT -- delete the declaration and the parameter. Note that
    widgets_values is POSITIONAL in saved workflows, so only a TRAILING widget
    can be removed without shifting every saved value beneath it.
    Do NOT "fix" it by correcting the tooltip to describe the inert behaviour:
    that documents the defect instead of removing it.
    And when wiring it, pass the value EXPLICITLY at the call site rather than
    relying on the inherited default -- a silent default is how the number
    became invisible to readers in the first place.
  tags: [inert-widget, declared-and-accepted-never-read, default-equals-hardcode, invisible-in-the-shipped-configuration, work-moved-control-left-behind, rationalising-comment, ast-parameter-load-check, positional-widgets-values, explicit-call-site-argument, xref-12.107]
  legacy_id: "OTR-PBUG-20260819-01-normalize-dbfs-widget-does-nothing"

- id: "12.117"
  phase: 12
  area: cached-handle-outlives-its-owner-because-the-release-hook-runs-more-often-than-the-lifetime
  symptom: |
    A heavy artifact -- a multi-gigabyte text encoder, a checkpoint, a tokenizer
    -- is re-read from disk on EVERY unit of work, even though the work is a
    long run of near-identical units that all want the same handle. The obvious
    cache is added and one of two things happens, both quiet.
    Either the cache never hits: every unit still reloads, the wall clock is
    unchanged, and the logs look perfect because the render is CORRECT -- a
    cache that misses degrades to exactly the pre-cache path, so nothing errors
    and nothing is slower than before. Or the cache never ENDS: the handle is
    still resident hours later, across unrelated runs and after the pipeline has
    switched to a different engine entirely, because nothing ever released it.
    A third, rarer shape: a documented "turn it off" escape hatch clears the
    cache, and the NEXT run then starts cold for no visible reason.
  cause: |
    The cache was placed on the object that was convenient rather than the
    object whose LIFETIME matches the reuse. Adapter registries commonly build
    each adapter ONCE at import and hand back that same instance forever, so
    "cache it on self" is not per-run scoping -- it is process-scoped, and there
    is no later moment at which it ends.
    The fix reflex is then to release in an existing per-unit hook, and every
    such hook is measurably WRONG because it fires more often than the lifetime
    being cached: a pre-load residue-freer runs per unit of work, a
    teardown/unload runs per group, a residency lease is taken and released per
    group. Releasing on any of them drops the cache immediately before the thing
    that needed it, which is the "never hits" shape -- and it is invisible
    precisely because the result is still correct.
    The ABA variant has no concurrency in it at all. If ownership is a bare
    flag or a plain counter and a global "release now" path zeroes it, an
    owner's still-outstanding close can land on a scope opened AFTERWARDS by
    somebody else. Add real concurrency -- worker threads serving requests -- and
    the counter also loses updates, because `owners += 1` is load/add/store.
  fix: |
    Put the cache where the LIFETIME is, not where the object is. Enumerate
    every candidate release hook and MEASURE how often each one actually fires
    against how long the handle must live; pick the one that matches and reject
    the rest by frequency, in a comment, so the next reader does not
    "simplify" it back onto the convenient object.
    Usually that boundary is one layer UP, in the driver that owns the run,
    rather than in the adapter that owns the handle. Open there, and release in
    a `finally` so a failed run cannot strand the memory -- the failure path is
    where a leak is most likely and least noticed.
    Make ownership a TOKEN, not a flag or a bare count. `begin` returns an
    opaque generation; the caller hands that exact token back to `end`; a global
    release BUMPS the generation so every outstanding token goes stale and a
    late close becomes a logged no-op. Guard the whole lifecycle with a lock if
    any request path is threaded.
    Do NOT escalate a failed close into a GLOBAL release as a belt-and-braces
    cure. A global release drops the cache for every owner, so one run's failing
    close destroys another run's live cache: losing your own share of a cache is
    a slow run, taking someone else's is a broken one. Leak the one claim, log
    it loudly, and move on.
  verify: |
    (1) The acceptance instrument must count the LOADER'S OWN evidence -- the
    line the loading library prints when it opens the file -- never the cache's
    own "HIT" line. A cache that believes it hit is not evidence that a file was
    not read.
    (2) Correlate PER SCOPE, not in aggregate. Summing every event in a log lets
    a healthy scope subsidise a broken one, and lets an unrelated diagnostic run
    in the same log fail a perfectly good one. Attribute events to the
    open..close interval they occurred in; an empty scope grants no allowance.
    (3) Require POSITIVE evidence of reuse before passing: a non-zero load
    count, a scope actually opened, at least two units of work, and at least one
    hit. A gate whose only check is "loads <= N" passes a log with ZERO matched
    lines, which is how a renamed log line or the wrong file reads as a receipt.
    (4) Assert the ABA case directly and WITHOUT threads: begin(A), global
    release, begin(B), end(A) -- then assert B's scope is still open. This is
    deterministic and is the failure most likely to be reintroduced.
    (5) Prove it on a real run, not only in unit tests. The question a unit test
    cannot answer is whether the cached handle SURVIVES the pipeline's own
    teardown between units -- a structurally valid but internally unusable
    handle passes every shape check and then fails at use.
  tags: [cache-lifetime, ownership-boundary, registry-singleton, release-hook-fires-too-often, silent-degradation-to-the-old-path, aba-stale-close, generation-token, global-release-evicts-other-owners, acceptance-gate-fails-open, per-scope-correlation, loader-evidence-not-self-report, xref-12.87, xref-12.110]
  legacy_id: "OTR-LTX25-encoder-reloaded-every-shot-cache-needs-episode-scoped-ownership"

- id: "12.118"
  phase: 12
  area: the-metric-is-inverted-and-a-value-that-never-moves-is-not-a-negative-result
  symptom: |
    A quality or defect metric returns the SAME NUMBER on every arm of every
    experiment -- control included -- and each run is written up as "no effect".
    Nothing errors. The instrument runs, prints a plausible figure, and the
    figure is stable to two significant figures across changes that were
    expected to matter. Measured: a spectral "lattice score" read 2.2-2.3 on
    five consecutive single-variable renders (sampler eta, negative prompt, an
    input compression knob, a replacement sigma schedule, decoder tile
    geometry) and 1.6-2.8 on every prior measurement across the whole archive.
    Five renders were spent, five candidates were recorded as eliminated, and
    the operator kept reporting that he could still SEE the artifact.
    The instrument was then pointed at two images the operator could tell apart
    by eye -- one with the artifact clearly visible, one with it visibly gone --
    and scored the CLEAN one HIGHER: 7.2 against 4.8. It was not insensitive.
    It was INVERTED, and every conclusion drawn from it was void.
  cause: |
    TWO INDEPENDENT DESIGN ERRORS, and the second is the fatal one.
    First, DIMENSIONALITY: the artifact was a two-dimensional lattice, which
    appears in a 2-D transform as an isolated PAIR OF POINTS. The metric
    averaged one-dimensional row transforms, which smears that pair into a band
    and discards the very structure that made it a lattice.
    Second, and decisively, IT REPORTED A RATIO -- peak divided by the median
    of its band. Any smoothing lowers the median floor, so the ratio RISES when
    texture is removed. The metric was really answering "how noisy is
    everything else", so a change that removed the defect improved the picture
    and worsened the score at the same time.
    The reason it survived so long is that BOTH failure modes look like a
    negative result. An inverted metric and a metric measuring nothing both
    produce "unchanged", and "unchanged" is exactly what a team hunting a cause
    expects to see most of the time. Each individual run looked like honest
    elimination; only the SEQUENCE was suspicious.
    A plausible mechanism was proposed and was WRONG: that a 9-pixel high-pass
    kernel was cancelling a ~9.5-pixel artifact. Kernels from 9 down to 1 were
    tested and every one stayed inverted. The kernel was never the problem, and
    fixing it would have produced a metric that was still inverted and now
    carried a story explaining why it was trustworthy.
  fix: |
    VALIDATE EVERY DETECTOR AGAINST A KNOWN-POSITIVE AND A KNOWN-NEGATIVE
    BEFORE TRUSTING ONE READING FROM IT. Two artifacts a human can tell apart
    are enough. If the metric does not separate them, in the right direction,
    it is not measuring the thing and no amount of care in the experiment
    design rescues it.
    TREAT AN UNMOVING NUMBER AS A DEFECT IN THE INSTRUMENT, NOT AS EVIDENCE.
    A metric that returns the same value across every arm of every experiment
    has failed a smell test that costs nothing to apply. State the rule as a
    threshold: after N arms with no movement, stop running arms and go validate
    the instrument.
    PREFER AN ABSOLUTE MEASURE TO A RATIO whenever a treatment can change the
    denominator. Denoising, blurring, sharpening and rescaling all move a noise
    floor, so any peak-over-floor statistic is confounded by exactly the
    interventions being tested.
    MEASURE IN THE ARTIFACT'S OWN DIMENSIONALITY. A 2-D structure needs a 2-D
    transform; collapsing an axis to make the maths convenient throws away the
    signal.
    SHIP THE VALIDATION WITH THE INSTRUMENT. The replacement carries a
    `--selftest` that re-scores the known ladder and REFUSES TO PASS unless the
    ordering still matches the human's, so it cannot silently rot back into a
    blind metric.
  verify: |
    (1) Score a known-positive and a known-negative artifact. Require the
    positive to score higher by a stated margin. A metric that cannot pass this
    is not permitted to produce a number in a report.
    (2) Rank a LADDER, not a pair. Three or more samples of increasing severity
    must come out monotonic. A pair can be separated by luck; an ordering
    cannot.
    (3) Assert a minimum dynamic range across that ladder. A metric that
    separates the extremes by a few percent will drown any real effect in
    scene-to-scene variation.
    (4) Make the validation EXECUTABLE and part of the tool, not a note in a
    document. A self-test that fails loudly is the only thing that survives the
    next person picking the instrument up.
    (5) Re-read every prior conclusion the broken metric produced. They are not
    "probably still right" -- they are unevaluated. Re-score the artifacts that
    already exist before spending anything new.
    (6) Prove the replacement disagrees with the original where it matters:
    assert that the old metric ranks the known pair backwards. Without that,
    nobody can tell whether the instrument changed or only its scale did.
  tags: [instrument-not-evidence, inverted-metric, unmoving-value-is-a-smell, ratio-confounded-by-denominator, peak-over-median, wrong-dimensionality, validate-against-known-positive-and-negative, monotonic-ladder, dynamic-range-floor, selftest-ships-with-the-tool, plausible-mechanism-was-wrong, re-read-prior-conclusions, xref-11.61, xref-12.87]
  legacy_id: "OTR-LTX25-grid-detector-was-inverted-five-renders-spent-on-a-blind-metric"
- id: "12.119"
  phase: 12
  area: a-validation-set-with-one-direction-of-change-cannot-validate-a-metric
  symptom: |
    A hand-built quality metric ships with a self-test that PASSES, and the
    metric is backwards. Measured three times on one project in one month.
    A first detector scored the visibly CLEAN image HIGHER than the degraded
    one (7.2 against 4.8); five renders were spent on its readings before
    anyone pointed it at a pair a human could separate by eye. Its replacement
    shipped a `--selftest` that ranked a five-rung ladder monotonically and
    still called the visibly BETTER frame worse (6.724 against 6.260) on
    matched-resolution inputs. A third instrument, a graph differ, silently
    returned an EMPTY parse for six of eight targets and printed
    "0 differences" -- a clean bill of health for subjects it never examined.
  cause: |
    THE VALIDATION SET ONLY MOVED IN ONE DIRECTION. Every rung of the detector's
    ladder was progressive SMOOTHING, so the defect and the real detail fell
    together on every rung. A metric that conflates "removed the artifact" with
    "removed all detail" cannot be caught by a ladder where those two always
    co-vary. The first sample that moved them in OPPOSITE directions -- more
    real detail, less artifact -- broke it instantly.
    The deeper error is framing: the defect was PERCEPTUAL and was attacked as a
    signal-processing problem. A scalar also cannot show its reasoning, so a
    wrong one is unfalsifiable; the differ's empty parse and the detector's
    inverted score are the same failure wearing different clothes -- an
    instrument reporting success while doing nothing.
  fix: |
    Add a fixture pair to the validation set where the defect and the wanted
    signal move in OPPOSITE directions, and make the self-test FAIL if the
    metric cannot separate that pair -- a ladder of progressive smoothing can
    never catch a metric that conflates artifact with detail. Make the
    instrument assert it actually consumed its input: fail on an empty parse or
    a zero-length comparison set instead of printing "0 differences". For a
    perceptual defect, keep the scalar only for stability over time and let a
    vision model POLICE it -- when the two disagree, a human looks.
  verify: |
    (1) The validation set must contain at least one pair where the defect and
    the wanted signal move in OPPOSITE directions; if every fixture degrades
    both together, the self-test proves nothing.
    (2) Assert the instrument actually consumed its input -- a non-empty parse,
    a non-zero compared-item count -- and FAIL when the comparison set is empty
    rather than reporting zero differences.
    (3) For a perceptual defect, cross-check the scalar against a vision model
    on matched NATIVE-PIXEL crops (full frames get downscaled by chat/vision
    transports, erasing the very acuity under test), asking COUNTABLE questions
    rather than "which is better", and sending the pair in both orders because
    vision models carry a position bias.
    (4) When scalar and vision judge disagree, a human looks. Do not ship the
    scalar's number as evidence until that disagreement is resolved.
  tags: [metric-inverted, selftest-passes-while-wrong, one-directional-validation-set,
    false-green-instrument, empty-parse-reports-clean, perceptual-defect-needs-perceptual-judge,
    vlm-as-metric-police, position-bias, native-pixel-crop, unfalsifiable-scalar]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-20-three-instruments-reported-success-while-doing-nothing"

- id: "12.120"
  phase: 12
  area: structural-support-does-not-prove-model-semantic-compatibility
  symptom: |
    A reference-conditioning branch exists, every named node is installed, the
    graph validates, and the executor runs it without an exception -- yet the
    referenced outputs are materially corrupted while otherwise matched
    unreferenced outputs are clean. In the live case, every character scene
    using one clean portrait acquired a square/noise lattice across clothing
    and walls; unreferenced scenes, portraits, and music cards did not. The
    generic conditioning node had therefore produced the strongest possible
    false green: structural support, successful execution, and bad pixels.
  cause: |
    NODE AVAILABILITY IS NOT A TRAINING CLAIM. A generic node can construct a
    tensor shape that a model architecture accepts, and checkpoint headers can
    contain compatible projection or padding keys, without the exact checkpoint
    ever having been trained to interpret that tensor as a reference identity.
    The capability was admitted from graph reachability and weight shape rather
    than from checkpoint-specific evidence. Validation could prove only that
    the path was executable; it could not prove what the path meant to the
    denoiser. Because the branch was advertised as a capability, the dispatcher
    then selected it automatically and cached its corrupted products under the
    same engine identity as the previously clean path.
  fix: |
    DENY SEMANTIC CAPABILITIES BY DEFAULT. A shipping engine must not set
    `accepts_reference_image = True` merely because a generic reference node is
    installed or its graph executes. Admit the capability only for an exact
    model artifact whose identity is pinned and whose approval record cites a
    matched-pixel A/B: same checkpoint, prompt, negative, seed, dimensions and
    delivery path, with only the reference branch changing. Keep the diagnostic
    graph/harness available even after rejection so a future checkpoint can be
    evaluated without silently re-enabling production routing.
    When evidence rejects a previously advertised capability, turn the public
    capability off, preserve any separately proven identity mechanism such as a
    portrait-derived seed, and bump the engine/cache version. Otherwise already
    cached corrupt artifacts survive the code fix and make the disabled path
    appear to remain live.
  verify: |
    (1) Static admission gate: scan class-level capability declarations. Every
    literal `accepts_reference_image = True` must be accompanied by a literal
    `reference_image_approval` mapping containing the exact checkpoint SHA-256
    and the repository-contained, tracked path to a matched-pixel A/B approval
    receipt. The receipt must repeat the checkpoint hash, identify the model
    filename and reference hash, record SUCCESS for both arms, prove OFF lacks
    and ON consumes the branch, hold a complete match signature equal across
    arms, record distinct graph and native-output pixel hashes, and carry an
    attributed APPROVED pixel verdict. Malformed, dynamic, missing, untracked,
    unmatched or negative evidence is a release failure; the presence of
    `ReferenceLatent` or an equivalent node is never sufficient evidence.
    (2) Pixel gate: run reference OFF and ON on separate fresh boots with every
    generation parameter and the delivered crop held equal. Record both graphs,
    model identity, seeds, native-pixel outputs, and an explicit human or
    perceptual verdict in the approval artifact.
    (3) Rejection gate: if ON loses, assert the shipping engine advertises the
    capability as false and the dispatcher does not supply the reference, while
    any independent proven identity seed still resolves.
    (4) Cache gate: assert the engine/cache version differs from the rejected
    version and an old cache key cannot hit. A clean fresh render is not enough
    if stale corrupted media can still be returned.
  tags: [reference-conditioning, semantic-compatibility, structural-support-is-not-training,
    model-specific-admission, matched-pixel-ab, capability-deny-by-default,
    cache-version-bump, false-green-graph, checkpoint-sha256, approval-receipt]
  legacy_id: "PBUG-20260820-01"
- id: "12.121"
  phase: 12
  area: an-uncontrolled-second-variable-sends-every-arm-of-a-visual-ab-to-the-same-wrong-verdict
  symptom: |
    A visual A/B (or a whole elimination campaign) returns the same verdict on
    every arm, and the verdict is about the wrong stage. Two live instances in
    one week on one project. (1) Six single-variable knob tests on a video lane
    all reported "grid clearly visible" -- because the GRID WAS IN THE
    CONDITIONING STILL, minted by the upstream image engine; an I2V lane is a
    preservation machine, so every arm faithfully reproduced the defect it was
    being tested for removing. Months of renders chased knobs that could never
    have worked. (2) An identity-drift probe swapped the conditioning still to
    a portrait but kept the recipe's text prompt describing a different scene;
    BOTH arms obediently walked away from the portrait to the prompted scene,
    and scoring it as written would have condemned a refine stage for
    executing its prompt correctly.
  cause: |
    The contrast was impure: the arms differed by the intended knob, but the
    SUBJECT carried a second uncontrolled variable that dominated the outcome
    -- an upstream-minted defect in instance (1), a prompt/conditioning
    mismatch in instance (2). A consuming stage cannot be isolated while its
    input already contains the effect (or a stronger conflicting instruction).
    Single-variable discipline is usually applied to the GRAPH DELTA between
    arms and forgotten for the INPUTS shared by both arms.
  fix: |
    Before any A/B on a consuming stage: (1) prove the defect ABSENT at the
    minting stage by inspecting the raw conditioning input at native pixels
    (eye or vision model) -- if the defect rides in, fix upstream first and
    rerun; (2) align every shared input with the test's question -- the prompt
    must describe the conditioning still when the question is preservation;
    (3) verify arm purity mechanically: diff the two staged graphs and assert
    the delta set equals exactly the intended knobs, nothing else -- a
    deterministic script, not a model pass.
  verify: |
    (1) A pre-A/B receipt exists showing the conditioning input was inspected
    and the defect under test is absent from it.
    (2) The staged arms carry a machine diff whose changed-parameter set
    equals the declared contrast set exactly.
    (3) If every arm of a campaign returns the same verdict, treat that as
    evidence of an uncontrolled shared variable and audit the inputs before
    spending another render.
  tags: [ab-contrast-purity, upstream-minted-defect, conditioning-still,
    prompt-conditioning-mismatch, i2v-preserves-defects, shared-input-variable,
    elimination-campaign-void, xref-12.118, xref-12.119, xref-12.120]

- id: "12.122"
  phase: 12
  area: an-in-process-graph-authoring-form-is-not-the-http-prompt-form
  symptom: |
    Every leg of an API-submitted campaign dies in seconds with
    "TypeError: <Node>.execute() missing 1 required positional argument"
    on one node, even though the graph came verbatim from a shipping
    engine that runs the same node in-process every day, and /prompt
    accepted the submission without complaint.
  cause: |
    The engine's builder output is an AUTHORING form for the in-process
    bridge, not an HTTP prompt. Three translation gaps hide in it.
    (1) V3 dynamic inputs (COMFY_DYNAMICCOMBO_V3, autogrow): the builder
    authors one nested dict, but the prompt format addresses sub-inputs
    by dot-joined prefixes ("resize_type": "scale dimensions" plus
    "resize_type.width": 832 as SIBLING keys -- finalize_prefix in
    comfy_api/latest/_io.py joins with "."). The nested dict validates,
    queues, then leaves the argument unmapped at execution.
    (2) Runtime class swaps (classes["x"] = subclass(...) inside
    render_clip) never serialize, so the API run silently executes the
    stock class with different device placement or behavior.
    (3) Nodes implemented as local Python classes are not registered and
    cannot be submitted at all.
  fix: |
    Translate, never transplant. Flatten dynamic dict inputs into
    dot-prefixed leaf keys (an inner key equal to the input's own name is
    the selector and keeps the bare name); fail LOUD on an empty dict
    rather than dropping the argument. Re-declare every runtime class
    swap as a registered equivalent applied identically to every arm and
    record it as a deliberate adaptation. Fail closed on local classes.
    Sweep BEFORE rendering: census every buildable engine graph for
    dict-valued inputs, grep render paths for class-map mutations, and
    the wall shows up one lane early instead of at the first dead leg.
  verify: |
    (1) Submitting the translated graph executes the formerly failing
    node (asset in /history), where the untranslated dict form raised
    TypeError.
    (2) A census script exists that enumerates dynamic inputs from
    /object_info and dict-valued literals across engine builders, and its
    receipt is committed.
    (3) Any graph-conversion helper raises on an empty dict input instead
    of emitting nothing.
  tags: [api-vs-inprocess, dynamic-inputs, dot-flattening, prompt-format,
    runtime-class-swap, local-class-fail-closed, unmapped-argument,
    xref-09.06, xref-12.121]

- id: "12.123"
  phase: 12
  area: a-harness-that-hardcodes-one-graph-builder-measures-the-path-production-does-not-use
  symptom: |
    An A/B campaign runs clean end to end -- every leg succeeds, purity
    passes, the null is receipted -- and only afterwards does someone
    notice the staged graphs are missing a node class that production
    renders with every day. The verdict is not wrong about what it
    measured; it measured the wrong configuration, and the whole lane has
    to be re-bounded or re-run.
  cause: |
    An engine may ship SEVERAL graph builders for one model, chosen at
    runtime by a flag, and the harness introspects only the conventionally
    named one. Two things then conspire. (1) The flag's DEFAULT decides
    what production actually renders, and a default of "on" means the
    non-default builder is the real shipping path -- so the obvious
    builder name is the wrong one. (2) The fixture that feeds the builder
    happily satisfies both signatures, so nothing raises: the alternate
    builder needs one extra argument the fixture already supplies, and
    the wrong graph builds silently. Node-count and purity checks pass
    because they compare the two ARMS against each other, never the arm
    against production.
  fix: |
    Name the builder at the call site instead of defaulting to it. Make
    the harness take an explicit builder argument, and have the lane
    receipt record which builder it used alongside the engine id. Then
    assert the staged graph against the CONFIGURATION, not only against
    the sibling arm: if the shipping flag implies image conditioning,
    require the conditioning node classes to be present and fail the
    staging when they are absent. Read the flag's default from the source
    rather than assuming the unflagged path is the shipped one.
  verify: |
    (1) Staging through the alternate builder produces a graph whose node
    set differs from the default builder's (the conditioning wrapper and
    its image loader are present), and the receipt names the builder.
    (2) A staging assertion fails loudly when the required nodes for the
    declared configuration are missing.
    (3) The flag's default is quoted from source in the lane receipt, so
    "which path does production use" is answered by evidence rather than
    by the builder's name.
  tags: [multiple-builders, runtime-flag-default, wrong-path-measured,
    harness-introspection, silent-fallback, ab-validity,
    xref-12.121, xref-12.122]
  legacy_id: "OTR-2026-08-21-lane3-screened-text-only-while-i2v-default-on"

- id: "12.124"
  phase: 12
  area: a-vram-compromise-frozen-without-a-quality-test-can-be-painting-an-artifact-into-every-render
  symptom: |
    A memory-saving mode is enabled by default, has been for the life of
    the recipe, and is assumed free because it is "just" a memory
    technique with no picture consequence. Then a side-by-side against
    the same content decoded the other way shows a discrete visible
    defect -- measured: a soft blue blob, thousands of pixels, flaring for
    a few frames and fading, present ONLY in the memory-saving arm. It had
    been in every clip that engine had ever produced. Nobody had looked,
    because the mode was chosen for VRAM and never scored for quality.
    Worse, it looked deliberate: the operator's first reading was "I was
    thinking the blobs were an animated test feature."
  cause: |
    THREE THINGS COMPOUND, and the third is why it survives review.
    (1) The compromise was frozen on evidence from a DIFFERENT engine.
    The recipe comment cited a sibling tier's measurement and said so
    honestly -- "that is context for a future sweep and not a claim about
    these numbers" -- and the sweep was never run.
    (2) Because it was on by default, EVERY render carried it, so it
    appeared in both arms of every prior A/B. A defect present in both
    arms is invisible to a comparison and reads as the content's own
    texture. Earlier lanes were scored over it.
    (3) The obvious failure mode was looked for and is absent. Tiled
    decode is expected to leave SEAMS on the tile lattice; there were
    none -- the strongest lattice concentration was 1.09 against a 1.15
    threshold and the arms were 98% identical spatially. Checking for the
    textbook artifact and finding it absent is easily mistaken for
    "the mode is clean".
  fix: |
    Treat "chosen for memory" as UNTESTED FOR QUALITY, not as neutral.
    When a compromise is frozen on a sibling's evidence, record it as an
    owed measurement rather than a settled choice. Score the compromise
    against its own alternative on the same content, and do not let the
    absence of the textbook artifact stand in for a quality result --
    look for what is actually there, including with a human eye, because
    a defect that looks intentional is exactly the one instruments framed
    around the expected artifact will miss. When the comparison lands,
    prefer a discrete visible defect over an aggregate statistic as the
    justification for changing it.
  verify: |
    (1) Render identical content in both modes and diff them; a defect
    present in one arm only is countable (pixel-region excess, frames
    affected) without a judge.
    (2) Sweep the archive: if the defect appears in EVERY prior render of
    that engine and in none from a different engine, the mode is the
    cause and the card/content is not.
    (3) Show a human a blind side-by-side with sides alternating per
    cell. An eye that picks the defective arm unprompted, and ties where
    the instruments say tie, is the confirmation an aggregate cannot give.
    (4) Re-read the frozen comment's own justification against the new
    measurement; a justification borrowed from another engine may simply
    not hold.
  tags: [vram-compromise-untested-for-quality, defect-in-both-arms,
    absent-textbook-artifact-mistaken-for-clean, borrowed-justification,
    operator-eye-beats-the-aggregate, looks-deliberate,
    tiled-vae-decode, recipe-freeze, xref-12.121, xref-12.118]

- id: "12.125"
  phase: 12
  area: a-model-refusal-can-arrive-as-a-successful-render-and-pass-every-generic-guard
  symptom: |
    A generation request is refused by a model's built-in content filter,
    and NOTHING in the pipeline notices. The job reports success, a real

    time.
  cause: |

    no exception, no error field and no status to inspect -- the refusal
    IS the output. Every generic integrity check therefore passes: exit
    status, file existence, file size, array shape, and the usual
    "is it all black" degenerate-output guard, since a placeholder card
    is mid-grey rather than empty. On a pipeline where a missing asset is
    a hard failure by design, this is worse than a crash: the crash would
    have been caught.
  fix: |
    Do not attempt to classify a refusal before you have SEEN one.
    Deliberately trigger it, capture the artifact, and derive the
    detector from its measured statistics rather than from a guessed
    string match -- there is no string to match. A placeholder card is
    flat and floor-lifted: measured, min 69-80 and std 10.1-10.7 against

    high minimum" separates them cleanly. Arm that detector before any
    candidate output is accepted, and treat a refusal as a named failure
    rather than as an image. Test with the pipeline's OWN content: a
    synthetic probe string may render fine where every real line refuses.
  verify: |
    (1) A deliberately refused request is classified as a refusal, not as
    a successful render, and raises the pipeline's named error.
    (2) The detector is derived from a captured refusal artifact and its
    thresholds are recorded, not guessed.
    (3) The all-black / empty-output guard is shown to MISS the refusal,
    so the new detector is demonstrably not redundant.
    (4) Refusal rate is measured on real production text, not on a
    hand-written probe.
