- id: "12.126"
  phase: 12
  area: on-a-zeroed-negative-topology-a-prohibition-is-positive-conditioning
  symptom: |
    A structured JSON prompt is extended with extra fields by analogy with
    other APIs -- a trailing "negative_instruction" guard, a
    "style_description" block -- and the model PAINTS the extra field's
    words into the picture. Measured: a title card whose guard read "no
    logos, no captions" came back with "NO MISCOS," rendered as gold
    corner lettering, on three of three renders. The main quoted line was
    spelled perfectly; only the invented furniture betrayed the defect.
  cause: |
    The text encoder consumes the WHOLE prompt string as conditioning.
    There is no parser and no schema validator between the JSON and the
    model, so an unknown key is not skipped -- it is more caption. The
    schema that IS honoured was never guessed at correctly because it was
    never looked up: the vendor's authoritative statement of it shipped
    INSIDE the workflow template, as a magic-prompt system text in a
    hidden subgraph, stating exactly three top-level keys in a fixed
    order, single-line minified output, and bbox normalized 0-1000.
    The probe payload violated all three (five keys, indent=2, pixel
    bboxes out of range) and additionally invented a negative field for
    a topology that has no text-negative input at all -- the guider's
    negative is wired from ConditioningZeroOut, the zeroed positive.
  fix: |
    FIRST establish whether the graph has a negative channel at all. Read
    the guider's negative input: if it is fed by ConditioningZeroOut of the
    positive (or any zero tensor), there is NO negative channel, every
    token is positive conditioning, and a prohibition can only ADD its own
    nouns. Then AUDIT WHAT YOU ARE ALREADY SENDING before blaming the
    model -- including boilerplate spliced in upstream by your own prompt
    composer, which is the likeliest source because nobody reads it. Strip
    prohibition clauses at the engine boundary rather than editing the
    shared composer, since other engines DO have a negative channel and
    still need theirs. Express suppression as a positive statement about
    what occupies the space instead ("the lower band is unbroken matte
    ground"), and leave no region of the canvas unclaimed. Second, and
    only then, EXTRACT the vendor's own schema statement -- open the
    shipped template's subgraph definitions
    and read the embedded magic-prompt/system text, which is the contract
    the checkpoint was tuned against. Byte-diff the payload against it:
    carry exactly the documented keys in the documented order and form,
    and express suppression ("nothing else appears") through the
    documented prose fields' own vocabulary rather than through an
    invented negative field. Check the graph before assuming a negative
    channel exists; in a zeroed-negative topology there is nowhere for
    negative TEXT to act. When an output defect appears, isolate ONE
    variable per rerun -- this failure was first misread as a content
    refusal because prompt shape and canvas moved together.
  verify: |
    (0) The graph's negative input is traced and recorded: a zeroed
    negative means prohibitions are provably additive, and the fix is
    deletion, not relocation. Moving a guard from a JSON key into prose
    does NOT fix it.
    (1) The conforming payload renders with no invented text where the
    foreign-key payload demonstrably painted its guard words.
    (2) The payload's top-level keys are pinned by test to the schema the
    template's embedded system text states, so a drive-by "helpful"
    field cannot return.
    (3) Any claim that the model refuses or degrades on some content
    names the single variable that was isolated to show it.
  tags: [structured-prompt-schema, foreign-key-rendered, magic-prompt-subgraph,
    zeroed-negative-means-prohibitions-add, audit-your-own-prompt-first,
    upstream-composer-boilerplate, positive-statement-beats-prohibition,
    schema-lives-in-the-template, zeroed-negative-topology,
    no-text-negative-channel, single-variable-isolation,
    prompt-shape-not-content, xref-12.125, xref-12.121]

- id: "12.127"
  phase: 12
  area: an-optional-input-fixture-only-tests-the-path-where-the-option-is-present
  symptom: |
    A budget, cap or trim rule is covered by a large, parameterised,
    entirely-green test suite -- and it fails on the first live run, on
    ordinary content, at a value the tests never produced. The failure is
    LOUD and correct (the code refuses rather than shipping a bad result),
    which is the only reason it is cheap: it fires before any expensive
    stage. Re-running the same tests after the failure still shows green.
  cause: |
    Every fixture supplied the OPTIONAL input, and supplying it
    SHORT-CIRCUITS the branch under test. Production leaves that input
    absent by default, so production takes the other branch -- the one that
    falls back to large config-authored values -- and no test ever composed
    it. Measured: a prompt composer capped at 320 characters was tested with
    an authored motion clause in every case; the authored clause bypasses
    the style pack's own register entirely. With the optional pass OFF (the
    default), the composer instead pulled the pack's subject surface
    (163-178 chars across nine packs) AND its motion register (130-209,
    budgeted at 240 by the loader) and composed 474 characters.
    A SECOND defect hid behind the first and is the mechanical half: the
    trimmer dropped whole COMMA-SEPARATED phrases, and the config surfaces
    are long comma-FREE prose. On those the trimmer is a NO-OP -- it finds
    one phrase, cannot drop it, and returns the text at its original length.
    A trimmer that cannot shrink its most likely input is not a trimmer.
  fix: |
    Parameterise the budget tests over the OPTION-ABSENT path explicitly,
    and over every shipped config value rather than one convenient default:
    role x pack x register key, with the optional input withheld. A fixture
    that always supplies an optional input is testing the caller, not the
    default. State the arithmetic in the test's own failure message (which
    surface contributed how many characters) so the next failure names its
    cause instead of only its total.
    For the trimmer: make it fall back from phrase boundaries to WORD
    boundaries so it can shrink comma-free prose, and never let a cut end on
    a dangling function word ("...and a", "...of the") -- a trailing
    fragment reads to a downstream model as an unfinished list. Then decide
    explicitly which fields are PROTECTED (never shrunk) and which are
    config-derived and therefore shrinkable, shrink the latter
    proportionally with a floor, and keep failing loudly if the protected
    set alone exceeds the budget. Runtime truncation must not become the
    preservation mechanism for the fields the feature exists to guarantee.
  verify: |
    (0) A test exists that composes with the optional input WITHHELD, over
    every shipped config value, and it fails on the pre-fix code.
    (1) The trimmer, given comma-free prose longer than the ceiling, returns
    something at or under the ceiling -- and the pre-fix trimmer returns it
    unchanged, which is the regression in one assertion.
    (2) No trimmed output ends on a function word from a checked-in list.
    (3) The protected fields survive every trim, and a protected set that
    cannot fit still raises rather than truncating.
  tags: [optional-input-fixture-hides-the-default-path, short-circuiting-input,
    budget-cap, trimmer-noop-on-its-likeliest-input, comma-free-prose,
    parameterise-over-shipped-config, loud-refusal-is-cheap,
    dangling-function-word, protected-vs-shrinkable-fields,
    truncation-is-not-a-preservation-mechanism]
- id: "12.128"
  phase: 12
  area: a-per-artifact-constant-a-sibling-silently-inherits
  symptom: |
    A newly added sibling lane/adapter refuses a PERFECT artifact. The error
    is specific and confident -- "X is only 1673262583 bytes (< the
    1700000000 floor) -- that is a truncated or wrong file, re-fetch it" --
    and re-downloading changes nothing because the file was never wrong. The
    parent lane runs fine with the identical code path.
  cause: |
    When the sibling was created, the obviously-varying constant (the model
    FILENAME) was correctly moved to a class attribute, and a second
    constant sized for the SAME artifact -- a byte floor, a quant token, a
    recipe receipt id -- was left as a module-level constant read from
    inside a method. The subclass therefore overrides one and silently
    inherits the other. The inherited value is not merely stale, it is
    calibrated for a DIFFERENT file, so it is a false accusation against
    every artifact except the parent's.
    The same shape has the opposite failure mode when the constant is a
    recipe rather than a guard: the sibling renders with the PARENT's recipe
    and stamps its OWN receipt -- wrong output under a confident label, with
    nothing raising at all.
  fix: |
    Any per-artifact constant travels WITH the lane that owns it, as a class
    attribute a sibling can override -- never a module-level constant read
    inside a method. Audit by asking, for each new sibling: what ELSE was
    sized, named or tuned for the parent?
    For a size guard, state BOTH directions or you produce the opposite bug:
    the floor must sit BELOW its own artifact (or it refuses a perfect file)
    and within ~15% of it (or a badly truncated fetch slips through -- a
    floor of 1 byte passes everything and guards nothing).
  verify: |
    (1) A test asserts, per lane, that the guard value sits below that
    lane's real artifact size, and a second asserts it is within 15% of it.
    Both fail on the pre-fix code for the sibling.
    (2) A test proves each lane resolves its OWN artifact token through
    `self`, and greps the render path for any module-level read of the same
    constant.
    (3) Each lane stamps a receipt naming its own recipe, and the set of
    receipts across siblings is distinct.
  tags: [per-artifact-constant, subclass-inherits-parents-calibration,
    byte-floor, guard-calibrated-for-one-artifact, false-accusation,
    wrong-output-under-a-confident-label, state-both-directions,
    class-attribute-not-module-constant]

- id: "12.129"
  phase: 12
  area: a-weight-on-disk-is-not-a-weight-the-server-can-see
  symptom: |
    A render fails deep into a long run -- after the script pass, the whole
    voice pass, and into the video pass -- reporting a model file missing.
    The file is unambiguously present on disk with a correct hash. Copying
    it again changes nothing, and the same weight loads fine for a different
    tool on the same machine.
  cause: |
    Two independent traps, and they compound.
    (a) A headless/managed server is launched with its OWN model-paths
    configuration naming specific roots. A weight dropped under the "obvious"
    models directory is invisible if that root is not the one the server was
    started with. Auditing the filesystem cannot detect this, because the
    file really is there.
    (b) `folder_paths` caches its filename listings at boot, so even placing
    the file under a correct root does not help a server that is already
    running.
    The failure surfaces late because artifact presence is checked at first
    USE -- correctly, and loudly -- rather than before the job is submitted.
  fix: |
    Gate the run on artifact visibility BEFORE submitting, and ask the
    RUNNING SERVER what it can see rather than asking the filesystem: walk
    /object_info and collect every filename offered in any node's combo.
    That needs no knowledge of the paths config or of which category a weight
    belongs to. Refuse with a message naming the missing file, the paths
    config to check, and the fact that the listing is cached at boot so a
    restart is required.
    Keep the deep guard as well. It is the thing that stops a silent wrong
    render; the preflight only makes it cheap.
  verify: |
    (1) With the weight moved outside every configured root, the run refuses
    in seconds and names the file; pre-fix it fails minutes in.
    (2) The refusal names the model-paths config and the boot-cache
    requirement, not just the filename.
    (3) A run whose weights are all visible logs the checked set, so a
    passing preflight is auditable rather than silent.
  tags: [model-paths-config, folder_paths-caches-at-boot, headless-server,
    present-on-disk-invisible-to-the-server, gate-before-submit,
    ask-the-server-not-the-filesystem, late-failure-is-expensive]

- id: "12.130"
  phase: 12
  area: waiting-on-process-absence-reads-the-gap-between-jobs-as-done
  symptom: |
    A queued job starts while an earlier one is still running and resets
    shared state out from under it. The victim dies with a killed-process
    exit code and NO verdict line, minutes or tens of minutes in, having
    been perfectly healthy. It looks like a crash in the victim; nothing is
    wrong with the victim.
  cause: |
    The waiting queue polls for the ABSENCE of a process ("no runner is
    running, therefore the other queue has finished"). Between one job
    exiting and the next booting there is a gap of seconds in which that
    predicate is TRUE and the queue is not finished. The waiter starts,
    performs its own environment reset, and kills the next job.
    A second, compounding trap: killing the runner does not kill the SERVER
    it booted. The orphaned server stays resident holding GPU/host memory
    and starves whatever runs next -- which presents as the next job being
    mysteriously slow rather than as a leak.
    On Windows the naive predicate is often broken anyway: `Get-Process`
    does not populate CommandLine, and `.Path` is blank for a half-booted
    process, so a filter on those matches NOTHING and the wait falls
    straight through on the first poll.
  fix: |
    Wait on a SENTINEL THE OTHER QUEUE WRITES -- a completion token appended
    to its results file exactly once, after its last job. A sentinel cannot
    appear during a gap; an absence can.
    Prefer one queue over two coordinating queues. When a job must be
    cancelled, kill the runner AND the server it booted, identified by port
    or command line, then confirm the memory came back.
    If a process predicate is unavoidable, filter on CommandLine via the
    OS process table (`Win32_Process`), never on a field the process
    enumerator does not populate.
  verify: |
    (1) With one queue mid-run between jobs, the waiter is provably still
    blocked -- its output directory is absent or empty.
    (2) After a cancellation, the count of resident servers returns to
    baseline and free memory returns to baseline.
    (3) The wait predicate is exercised against a real running job and
    matches it, proving the filter is not silently matching nothing.
  tags: [queue-coordination, absence-is-not-completion, sentinel-not-predicate,
    gap-between-jobs, orphaned-server-holds-memory,
    kill-the-runner-and-its-server, get-process-lacks-commandline,
    one-queue-beats-two]

- id: "12.131"
  phase: 12
  area: a-locked-slot-has-no-obligation-to-be-served
  symptom: |
    A cast/roster member is legitimately allocated a slot by an earlier
    stage, but a downstream generation pass -- composing under a tight
    budget, a small model, a short window -- serves some slots and not
    others. Nothing objects until a LATE, generic completeness gate refuses
    the whole unit ("member X has no content"), by which point a portrait,
    a credit, a caption or a proof may already reference the silent member.
  cause: |
    The allocation-time validator checks only that nothing UNPLANNED snuck
    in (used - locked == invented), never the reverse (locked - used ==
    starved). A member can be locked and receive zero output, and nothing
    between allocation and the generic completeness gate ever notices.
    The generic gate is not the bug -- it is working exactly as designed,
    refusing loudly rather than shipping a silent ghost. The bug is that
    nothing upstream ever tried to REPAIR the gap before the gate had to
    fire, so every occurrence became a hard failure instead of a self-heal.
  fix: |
    Add a REPAIR pass between allocation and the completeness gate, not a
    change to the gate itself -- the gate must stay refuse-only (repair
    belongs to the producer, per the "gates refuse, producers repair"
    convention). Extract the gate's own gap-finding predicate into a pure,
    reusable function so the repair pass and the gate can never drift on
    what counts as "served". For each gap: retry the existing slot if one
    exists (composition ran, produced nothing usable); otherwise mint a
    minimal new slot using the exact allocation-time row shape, entirely
    within the mutable ledger/state object -- never appended to an already-
    validated immutable/pydantic structure a later stage assumes is closed.
    Reuse the SAME seeded generation call every other slot already goes
    through -- no new sampling mechanism. On failure, leave the slot BYTE-
    IDENTICAL to the pre-repair state; only the success case may change
    anything. Gate the whole repair pass on whatever config already decides
    "this producer owns its own completeness" (never a hardcoded allow-list)
    so a producer with its own earlier, stricter gate is never double-served
    or forced to fill a slot it deliberately left empty.
  verify: |
    (1) A fixture shaped exactly like the historical failure -- a locked
    member, zero output, the completeness predicate returning a gap -- is
    repaired: the predicate returns empty afterward, output is non-trivial
    (not a placeholder token).
    (2) A member allocated an existing-but-empty slot and a member allocated
    NO slot at all both repair correctly (two mechanically different cases,
    same predicate).
    (3) A minted new slot's id/key never collides with an existing one,
    checked against the real allocation scheme, not assumed.
    (4) When the underlying generation call is exhausted/fails, the slot is
    provably unchanged from its pre-repair state, and the completeness
    predicate still reports the gap -- the repair must never mask a genuine
    failure as success.
    (5) A producer gated OUT of repair (its own earlier gate already covers
    it) is a byte-identical no-op: nothing in its output changes.
  tags: [allocate-then-serve-gap, one-directional-membership-check,
    completeness-gate-is-refuse-only, repair-upstream-of-the-gate,
    never-post-freeze-surgery, shared-gap-predicate-not-duplicated,
    ledger-only-mint-never-touch-the-validated-structure,
    same-seeded-slot-no-new-sampling, failure-leaves-state-untouched]

- id: "12.132"
  phase: 12
  area: a-valid-token-rejected-for-its-formatting
  symptom: |
    A structured-output parser rejects a token that IS valid, reporting it as
    unknown/unrecognized, because the model wrapped it in markdown emphasis --
    `**NAME`, `*NAME*`, `__NAME__`, a stray backtick, a trailing colon. The
    error names the token as the problem ("unknown speaker: **ANNOUNCER"),
    which sends the reader hunting for a missing roster entry that is
    actually present and correctly spelled.
    Because the wrapper is intermittent -- the same prompt emits a bare token
    most runs and a decorated one sometimes -- the failure is flaky, looks
    like a content problem, and survives many green runs before anyone
    believes it is mechanical.
  cause: |
    Instruction-tuned models emit markdown by habit, especially for anything
    that reads like a heading, a label, or a speaker cue, and especially
    under a system prompt that itself contains markdown. The parser matched
    on the raw line and compared the token to a roster by exact string, so
    a single pair of asterisks turned a known token into an unknown one.
    The deeper trap: the diagnostic is written in terms of the ROSTER
    ("speaker not in cast") rather than the TRANSPORT ("token had markup"),
    so the message actively misdirects. Every layer downstream then inherits
    a wrong theory of the failure -- a structural gate reports a skeleton
    break for a line whose only defect was two asterisks.
  fix: |
    Normalize transport decoration OUT before any identity comparison:
    strip paired emphasis markers, backticks, and trailing punctuation from
    the candidate token, then match. Do it in ONE place the parser and every
    validator share, so a second matcher cannot drift.
    Keep the raw token for the ERROR MESSAGE. When a match still fails,
    report both -- the raw token and its normalized form -- so the reader can
    tell a formatting artifact from a genuinely absent roster entry at a
    glance.
    If refusal on decorated tokens is deliberate policy (some formats are
    strict on purpose), say so IN the refusal: name the formatting as the
    reason rather than reporting the token as unknown.
    Do not "fix" this by asking the prompt to stop emitting markdown. That
    is an instruction a model will follow most of the time, which is exactly
    the failure profile that makes this expensive to diagnose.
  verify: |
    (1) Feed the parser the same token bare and wrapped in each emphasis form
    the target models actually emit (`**X`, `*X*`, `__X__`, `` `X` ``, `X:`);
    all resolve to the same identity, or all refuse with a message that names
    the FORMATTING.
    (2) A genuinely unknown token still refuses, and its message is
    distinguishable from the formatting case -- the two failures must not
    share one string.
    (3) The normalization lives in exactly one function; grep proves no
    second matcher compares roster tokens by raw string.
  tags: [markdown-leaks-into-structured-output, normalize-before-compare,
    error-message-names-the-wrong-layer, intermittent-looks-like-content,
    strip-emphasis-keep-raw-for-the-message, one-matcher-not-two,
    do-not-fix-it-in-the-prompt]
- id: "12.133"
  phase: 12
  area: a-seal-that-verifies-how-the-artifact-was-derived-instead-of-what-it-is
  symptom: |
    A build produces an artifact, records a hash or seal beside it, and a
    later integrity check RE-DERIVES the artifact and compares. The check
    fails -- "seal is stale", "artifact does not match", "checksum mismatch"
    -- for a document that nothing has touched, and the bytes on disk are
    provably unchanged.
    The failure is intermittent in the worst possible pattern: it fires only
    when some optional input actually influenced the derivation. Every
    ordinary case re-derives identically and stays green, so a full test
    suite passes while the feature that USES the optional input is exactly
    what breaks.
  cause: |
    Two independent mistakes that produce one symptom, and the second is the
    one people miss.
    (1) The conditions the build used -- flags, options, a lookup table, a
    mode -- are not stored with the artifact, so the verifier re-derives
    under DIFFERENT conditions than the producer used. It is not
    re-verifying the artifact; it is deriving a different one and calling
    the difference corruption.
    (2) The sealed payload includes DERIVATION METADATA rather than only the
    artifact: a receipt, a trace, a "how we got here" note, a list of which
    rule fired. Two runs can produce a byte-identical RESULT by different
    routes, and the seal then rejects a correct artifact because the route
    differed. This survives fix (1): store every condition faithfully and a
    seal over derivation metadata still breaks whenever the verifier's
    inputs differ in any way that changes the route but not the result.
  fix: |
    Seal WHAT THE THING IS, never HOW IT WAS MADE. Split the payload: the
    artifact fields go under the hash; receipts, traces and provenance notes
    live beside it, reported and never compared.
    Then make re-derivation faithful: persist the producing conditions ON the
    artifact and have the verifier read them back, so producer and verifier
    are the same function of the same inputs. Watch for a verifier that
    reconstructs an input "equivalently" -- deriving the roster/config/table
    from the artifact instead of storing it is the usual way conditions drift
    apart while looking rigorous.
    Prefer a verifier that CANNOT choose its own inputs.
  verify: |
    (1) Round-trip an artifact built with every optional input ENGAGED --
    not just the default path. Ordinary inputs re-derive identically and hide
    the defect.
    (2) NEGATIVE CONTROL, and this is the one that matters: strip the stored
    conditions back off the SAME artifact and assert verification now FAILS.
    Without it the test passes whether or not the fix works, because the
    happy path was already green.
    (3) Grep the payload builder for any field that describes the PROCESS
    rather than the RESULT -- receipts, traces, "normalizations", "applied
    rules" -- and prove none is inside the hash.
    (4) Confirm the producer and the verifier obtain their inputs from the
    same place, by reading, not by reasoning.
  tags: [seal-the-result-not-the-route, derivation-metadata-must-not-be-hashed,
    verifier-must-re-derive-under-the-producers-conditions,
    green-suite-hides-it-because-only-the-optional-path-breaks,
    negative-control-or-the-test-is-vacuous,
    it-breaks-exactly-the-feature-it-was-written-for]

- id: "12.134"
  phase: 12
  area: diagnostics
  symptom: |
    A load-verification tripwire fails a run LOUD -- "the quantized load did
    not materialize" -- on a load that in fact succeeded. Its own evidence
    line contradicts itself: the structural counters all look right
    (linear4bit_count=592, is_loaded_in_4bit=True) while the memory figure it
    actually gates on reads ~0.00 or negative. Restarting the app "fixes" it,
    which reads as flaky hardware or a library bug and sends the next reader
    hunting the loader.
  cause: |
    The tripwire measured a PROCESS-GLOBAL counter and reported the verdict as
    if it were a fact about ONE model: it sampled
    `torch.cuda.memory_allocated()` before and after the load and treated the
    delta as that model's footprint. Any other activity on the same device
    moves that number -- and in the observed incident an abandoned worker
    thread from an earlier timed-out phase (deliberately left draining,
    because generation is not cancellable mid-token) was freeing tensors
    concurrently, so the delta went negative while the model loaded
    perfectly. The guard did not have a false-positive threshold problem; it
    was reading the wrong instrument. Note the shape: the check LOOKS
    model-scoped because it brackets one model's load, but the quantity it
    samples belongs to the whole process.
  fix: |
    Measure the thing you are asserting about, at the granularity you assert
    it. Ask the MODEL where its weights are: walk `model.named_modules()`,
    find the quantized module type, and read each one's own
    `.weight.device` -- a fact no unrelated thread can move. Keep the global
    figure if it is useful, but demote it in both the code and the error
    message to explicitly-labelled telemetry that no branch reads.
    The general rule: a diagnostic that gates on a shared, process-wide
    quantity cannot make a claim about one component of that process. Before
    trusting any such guard, ask what else in the process can move the number
    while it is being sampled -- and if the answer is "another thread we
    deliberately do not join", the guard is unsound by construction, not
    merely noisy.
  verify: |
    (1) Extract the measurement into a pure function over the model object
    and unit-test it with fakes -- a module whose weights are on the device,
    one that is off it, and a model with no quantized modules at all. If the
    check can only be exercised by loading a real model on a real GPU, it
    cannot be regression-tested and will rot.
    (2) NEGATIVE CONTROL: assert the function reports FAILURE for the
    genuinely-unmaterialized case. A residency check that returns "fine" for
    every input passes the happy path and proves nothing.
    (3) Grep the guard's own branch conditions for any process-scoped
    reading -- allocator totals, free-memory queries, device-wide counters.
    Each one is either demoted to telemetry or justified in a comment.
    (4) Confirm the error message no longer presents the global figure as
    the reason for the verdict; a message that leads with a number the check
    no longer gates on will be re-diagnosed as the cause by the next reader.
  tags: [process-global-measurement-cannot-prove-a-component-fact,
    diagnostic-reads-the-wrong-instrument, false-failure-on-a-successful-load,
    concurrent-thread-moves-the-number-you-are-sampling,
    ask-the-object-not-the-allocator, demote-global-figures-to-telemetry,
    guard-must-be-unit-testable-without-a-gpu, negative-control-or-vacuous,
    restart-appears-to-fix-it, xref-12.46]
  legacy_id: BUG-LOCAL-098

- id: "12.135"
  phase: 12
  area: regression
  symptom: |
    A derived, invisible value silently stops being produced after an
    optimisation removes the work it was incidentally computed by. Nothing
    errors, no test fails, and the artifact still renders -- it is simply
    wrong in a way only a human eye catches. Measured instance: 71 of 75
    character stills across 17 episodes lost their identity seed, so the same
    character was drawn with a different face in every beat of an episode.
  cause: |
    One switch was doing two jobs. A per-lane flag suppressed MINTING a
    portrait image -- correct, because an unused portrait was costing a render
    on an engine that refuses face close-ups -- and the same flag silently
    emptied the prompt hash that a downstream seed derived a character's face
    from. The two commits were 17 days apart, so neither looked wrong on its
    own and no test connected them.
    The trap inside the fix is worse than the bug and is the reason this entry
    exists. The obvious repair is to transport the producer's raw prompt hash.
    That is WRONG: the consumer mutates the prompt (a safety clause, a style
    front-anchor, an image transform) and re-hashes it, so the value the
    consumer actually keys on is not the value the producer computed. Four
    independent readings -- a driver anchor, two review lanes and a synthesis
    pass -- all asserted the two hashes were "byte-for-byte identical" before
    one reviewer read the recomputation and refuted it. An equality test would
    have settled it in milliseconds.
  fix: |
    Separate the two jobs the switch was doing. Suppressing WORK must not
    suppress a DERIVED VALUE that work happened to carry: give the value its
    own owner, computed independently of whether the expensive artifact is
    produced. Here the identity basis is a text hash, so it costs nothing when
    the pixels are skipped.
    Transport the SOURCE TEXT, never the hash, and derive the basis in exactly
    ONE place -- the consumer's own normalise-then-hash chain. Two copies of
    that chain drift, and the symptom is faces moving.
    Make the safe direction the default: a lane that declares nothing should
    inherit the anchored behaviour, so a lane written tomorrow is correct for
    free and forgetting fails safe. Declaring the exception is cheaper than
    remembering the rule.
    When lanes share one declarative plan object, write the exception onto a
    COPY. Mutating the shared tuple in place strips the behaviour from every
    sibling lane -- including, in the measured instance, the three cinematic
    lanes carrying the defect and the one the production workflow renders with.
  verify: |
    (1) Assert the producer's hash and the consumer's hash DIFFER. That
    inverted test is the one that catches the plausible-but-wrong fix; if it
    ever passes, a mutation was removed and the transport design needs
    revisiting.
    (2) Structural guard, not a re-measurement: assert the producer emits the
    source TEXT and stamps no hash-shaped field at all, and that the consumer
    derives through the shared helper. This prevents the regression rather
    than detecting it later.
    (3) Pin the bug itself: with an empty basis, N beats of one character must
    produce N DISTINCT seeds; with a basis, exactly ONE. Both directions, or
    the guard is vacuous.
    (4) Assert the shared plan object was COPIED: the opting-out lane declares
    the exception AND its siblings still carry the default, compared by
    identity (`is not`) as well as by value.
    (5) Receipts must record whether the basis PARTICIPATED, not merely
    whether one was available -- otherwise a receipt claims an anchor the
    consumer never used.
  tags: [one-switch-two-jobs, suppressing-work-suppressed-a-derived-value,
    producer-hash-is-not-the-consumer-hash, transport-text-never-a-hash,
    derive-in-exactly-one-place, default-to-the-safe-direction,
    shared-plan-object-mutated-in-place, silent-no-error-no-failing-test,
    seventeen-days-between-the-two-commits, equality-test-beats-four-readings,
    xref-12.46]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260826-02

- id: "12.136"
  phase: 12
  area: prompting
  symptom: A backend renders an instruction it cannot physically honour, doing the nearest thing it can instead of failing. Nothing errors, no test fails, and the defect ships looking like a different subsystem's fault -- typically the one the instruction was ABOUT.
  cause: The prompt composer is a function of the CONTENT (the beat, the character, the line) but not of the ROUTED ENGINE'S CAPABILITY. One composer serves every backend, so a clause written for a capable lane is emitted verbatim to an incapable one. In the reference case a shared composer prepended "face visible, speaking to camera" plus the literal dialogue to every character beat; on a video engine that decodes no audio the only available response was to draw a mouth in motion, which read as broken lip-sync against the separately-muxed voice track and sent readers hunting an audio-routing fault that did not exist.
  fix: Make the prompt class a function of the engine, resolved from the SAME policy object the execution plan reads so the two cannot disagree about which backend renders. Classify by declared FAMILY or capability, never by engine-name string matching. Refuse to CLASSIFY an unknown id rather than defaulting -- a default family is a silent wrong answer in both directions. Where the composer feeds a model a truncated context, filter its OUTPUT against the SAME truncated value, not the full source; comparing against a longer sequence than the model ever saw is a filter that cannot fire.
  verify: A beat routed to an incapable lane contains neither the forbidden content nor the capability-specific instruction; a beat routed to a capable lane is byte-identical to before the change; an unregistered engine id raises by name instead of classifying.
  tags: [prompting, engine-capability, multi-backend, lip-sync, silent-degrade, composer]

- id: "12.137"
  phase: 12
  area: audio
  symptom: >-
    A generated audio bed is mixed, levelled, and reported green by every
    receipt, and is completely inaudible on playback. The logs say the stems
    decoded, the mix ran, and the loudness pass hit its target, so nothing looks
    wrong anywhere in the pipeline.
  cause: >-
    A FIXED GAIN MULTIPLIER applied to audio whose level was never measured. A
    ratio such as 0.20 bed over 0.80 programme reads as a mix balance and
    silently assumes the bed arrives near full scale, the way a recorded stem
    would. Model-GENERATED audio does not. Measured across one episode, LTX 2.5
    foley stems arrived at -30 to -55 dBFS RMS, so multiplying by 0.20 subtracted
    a further 14 dB and left the bed 37 to 58 dB under the programme (median 45;
    audible is 15 to 25 under). Every receipt was truthful, because the bed
    really was decoded, placed and levelled. None of them measured the bed
    AGAINST the programme, which is the only number that answers whether you can
    hear it.
  fix: >-
    Never apply a bare multiplier to generated audio of unknown level. Either
    reference the stem (peak, or gated active RMS) before applying the ratio, or
    state the ratio as a TARGET relative to the programme rather than as a
    scalar. Where the fixed ratio is a standing decision, amend it explicitly
    rather than reinterpreting it in code. Add the bed-versus-programme delta to
    the receipt, so an inaudible bed cannot look green.
  verify: >-
    Per positioned beat, RMS of the bed in its window minus RMS of the programme
    in the same window falls inside the intended band. A natural A/B settles it
    quickly when two lanes share the path at different gains -- the 1.00 lane was
    audible on identical stems while the 0.20 lane was not.
  tags: [audio, mix, gain-staging, generated-audio, silent-failure, receipts]

- id: "12.138"
  phase: 12
  area: prompting
  symptom: >-
    Renders do not move and look like stills, on video lanes that are
    demonstrably capable of motion. No error and no failing test; the footage is
    simply inert, and the instinct is to blame the model, the recipe, or the
    sampler.
  cause: >-
    The prompt itself is asking for stillness. Damping adjectives such as subtle,
    restrained, stable and comfortably composed accumulate in per-engine
    defaults, in fallback floors, and in the instruction given to the model that
    WRITES the motion, and each one looks locally reasonable. A clause writer
    told to be subtle cannot describe a body that moves, and an appended stable
    centered subject is an instruction to hold still. Measured on one episode, 8
    of 12 character beats rendered on a fallback floor reading restrained visible
    reaction, subtle natural body motion, stable mid-shot, so the floor WAS the
    picture.
  fix: >-
    Treat damping adjectives as a defect class and audit for them the way
    registry ids are audited. Ask for the motion VECTOR rather than a pose, and
    scale it to the material, so a calm line earns small motion and an urgent
    line earns real movement. Give each lane its own dialect instead of one
    universal prompt, because a length-limited lane wants brevity where a prose
    lane wants flowing prose. CRITICAL EXCEPTION -- on audio-driven lip-sync
    lanes the steadying language is PROTECTIVE, since it keeps the mouth in
    frame, so split the policy by FAMILY and never by engine name.
  verify: >-
    Grep every live engine default and prompt floor for damping adjectives and
    expect zero hits outside audio-driven lanes. A character beat's composed
    prompt names an action, and each lane's own published prompt directive is
    obeyed.

- id: "12.139"
  phase: 12
  area: preflight
  symptom: >-
    A preflight gate refuses every profile in an entire class with "model
    file(s) the running server cannot see", while the live server's own
    introspection endpoint lists the exact weight the whole time. The refusal
    is unconditional -- no state of the disk would ever satisfy it -- so the
    affected profiles sit permanently unexercised and look broken rather than
    misconfigured.
  cause: >-
    The gate's required-model list mixes two different id vocabularies and
    exact-matches against a server enumeration that can only speak one of
    them. A model-introspection endpoint enumerates FILENAMES on disk; some
    required-model entries are filenames and some are logical or HF-repo ids
    (a dotted version string, a GGUF-suffixed id, an org/repo path). For the
    filename entries the match is real verification. For the id entries no
    filename on the server's list can ever equal a logical id, so the gate is
    not reporting a missing weight -- it is reporting that an enumeration
    which structurally cannot contain the thing does not contain it, and
    scoring that as refutation. The gate was correct when it shipped, because
    it was validated only against profiles whose required entries happened to
    all be filenames; the defect was latent until the first profile carrying
    a logical id reached it.
  fix: >-
    Classify each required-model entry by what it actually is before
    comparing it to anything -- a closed suffix/shape test separating real
    filenames from logical or repo-style ids is enough, since the two forms
    do not overlap. Enforce only what the available enumeration can verify;
    for everything else, report rather than refuse -- an id the gate cannot
    check is not evidence of absence. Where a profile's declared requirement
    is a logical id but a real filename is knowable (the engine that consumes
    it names one), have the profile declare the filename so it gets real
    verification instead of an unverifiable id sitting in the enforcement
    path.
  verify: >-
    A profile whose required entries are legitimate filenames still fails
    when that filename is genuinely absent from the server's enumeration --
    the gate keeps its teeth. A profile whose required entries are logical or
    repo-style ids no longer fails on that account alone, and its dry-run
    reports the model as visible rather than refusing. A pinned filename<->
    engine mapping test catches a future profile that reintroduces an id where
    a filename belongs.
  tags: [preflight, gate-vocabulary-collision, id-namespace-mismatch, enumeration-cannot-contain-it, false-refusal, model-verification, enforce-vs-report]
  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260823-01
- id: "12.140"
  phase: 12
  area: watcher-timeout
  symptom: >-
    A long-running job's watcher prints a terminal-sounding failure --
    "TIMEOUT", "DEAD", "did not complete" -- and exits non-zero while the job
    itself is healthy: the server reports the work RUNNING, the GPU is
    saturated, and progress counters are still climbing. The job goes on to
    finish and publish successfully minutes later. The reader's natural
    response to a terminal message is to kill and re-run, which would destroy
    a healthy render that was minutes from completing.
  cause: >-
    One message is used for two OPPOSITE states: the watcher gave up waiting,
    and the work actually died. A watcher timeout is a fact about the
    WATCHER'S patience, not a fact about the work, but a bare "TIMEOUT" reads
    as the latter. The false case is common on any job whose real duration
    varies far more than its configured timeout was set for -- a fixed
    default sized for a typical run silently under-times a job class that
    legitimately runs several times longer.
  fix: >-
    On a timeout, DO NOT declare the outcome from the timeout alone -- ask the
    system the job is actually running on (a queue endpoint, a process table,
    a heartbeat file) and report one of at least three verdicts: STILL ALIVE
    (with live progress evidence and how to keep waiting), REALLY ENDED, or
    UNKNOWN (state itself unreadable) -- stated as what was checked, never
    guessed. Keep the classification function pure and unit-test each
    verdict as a distinct case so the three outcomes cannot collapse back
    into one string.
  verify: >-
    Against a live mid-run job, a timeout at the watcher's deadline resolves
    to STILL ALIVE, cites real progress evidence read from the system, and
    exits in a way that does not tear down the job. A genuinely dead job still
    resolves to REALLY ENDED. An unreadable state resolves to UNKNOWN rather
    than defaulting to either terminal verdict.
  tags: [watcher-timeout, false-failure, long-running-job, terminal-message-ambiguity, queue-introspection, dont-guess-ask]
  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260823-02
- id: "12.141"
  phase: 12
  area: provisioning-safety-boundary
  symptom: >-
    A pinned installer refuses a clean cloud image before dependency or model
    work, claiming the core has tracked changes even though every tracked and
    staged path is clean. The image's normal in-tree virtual environment,
    cache, or sibling links are untracked, so every fresh instance of that
    template is rejected by construction.
  cause: >-
    The installer's safety check observes a broader population than the
    destructive operation it guards. `git status --porcelain
    --untracked-files=all` mixes tracked edits, staged edits, and untracked
    template-owned paths into one non-empty string, while the stated contract
    is only to avoid replacing tracked work during a pinned checkout. A
    conservative-sounding wider gate is not safer when the extra population is
    normal input state; it makes the supported template permanently
    un-installable and mislabels the cause.
  fix: >-
    Match the check to the operation's real overwrite boundary: inspect tracked
    and staged state while excluding untracked entries. Preserve Git checkout
    itself as the second fail-closed guard, because Git refuses when an
    untracked path would actually be overwritten by the target commit. Name the
    states the diagnostic really checks. Never clean, move, or delete untracked
    template paths merely to satisfy the preflight.
  verify: >-
    In a real temporary Git checkout, commit one core file, add an untracked
    in-tree virtual environment, and require the production status command to
    report clean. Modify the tracked file and require the same command to report
    dirty; stage it and require dirty again. Pin the provisioning script to the
    tracked-only flag and reject the all-untracked flag. Finally rerun the
    installer on the live template that originally failed and require it to
    pass this boundary without removing the template-owned paths.
  tags: [provisioning, safety-gate, tracked-vs-untracked, cloud-template, false-refusal, git-status, pinned-checkout, input-population]

- id: "12.142"
  phase: 12
  area: gpu-runtime-compatibility
  symptom: >-
    A GPU template advertises one CUDA generation and its virtual-environment
    name advertises another, while the Python actually selected by the
    application imports a third-party system torch build. Importing torch and
    enumerating an NVIDIA device look plausible, but the first real CUDA
    initialisation fails because the host driver cannot execute that wheel.
    Reinstalling a compatible wheel appears to work, then a process-wide pip
    constraint silently restores the incompatible build during the next
    requirements install.
  cause: >-
    Labels were treated as runtime evidence. The image tag, venv directory
    name, `torch.__version__`, host driver and executable CUDA capability are
    distinct facts. A venv with system site packages can import outside its
    own directory, and a template-owned constraint can override a later pip
    command. No single label proves that the exact application interpreter can
    execute a CUDA kernel on the attached card and driver.
  fix: >-
    Probe the exact Python the application will run and require real device
    work -- allocate a CUDA tensor, perform an operation, synchronise, and
    inspect the result. Automatic repair must be a closed, measured tuple of
    current torch build and driver range with an exact replacement trio and
    package index; unknown tuples fail closed. Escape a conflicting template
    constraint for the repair, probe again, and prevent that same constraint
    from undoing the repair during subsequent dependency installation. Do not
    misclassify a runtime ABI failure as VRAM pressure or change the workload
    to hide it.
  verify: >-
    On the originally failing host, the application's exact interpreter must
    report the pinned replacement torch/vision/audio trio and complete a real
    CUDA operation. Then install the normal core and pack requirements and
    repeat the probe, proving no inherited constraint reverted the runtime.
    An unmatched torch/driver tuple must refuse automatic mutation by name.

- id: "12.145"
  phase: 12
  area: vram-residency
  symptom: >-
    An image (or video) engine that drives ComfyUI node classes in-process
    renders correctly on the development card but takes 10-50x longer per
    sampler step on a smaller card, with no error: the log shows the text
    encoder "loaded completely" (or staged for dynamic VRAM), then the
    diffusion model "loaded partially; 0.00 MB usable ... offloaded" after
    "0 models unloaded", and the GPU sits at full memory and full utilisation
    while every step runs from host memory. Stock launch flags and the
    low-VRAM flag pair behave the same.
  cause: >-
    The in-process graph executor kept every node's output alive until the
    whole graph finished, so the text-encoder object stayed referenced (and
    resident) through sampling. On the big card the encoder and the diffusion
    model co-reside and nothing shows; on the small card the encoder alone
    fills it, and the host's smart-memory logic does not evict it because the
    diffusion model's minimum partial load technically fits in the sliver
    that is left. The engine had been written without the free-after-use
    discipline its sibling video engines already used.
  fix: >-
    Drop each node's output the moment its last consumer has run and soft-
    empty the cache, keeping only the terminal and the diffusion MODEL node --
    AND, for the text-encoder node, unload its model patcher through the host's
    own registry (comfy.model_management.unload_model_and_clones) at that drop,
    while it is still registered. Under the host's dynamic-VRAM mode a dropped
    reference is not enough: the patcher keeps its weights in a host-managed
    virtual buffer owned by the module, the registry forgets it, and the pages
    stay allocated until another dynamic model applies pressure -- which a
    classic (e.g. GGUF) diffusion model never does. Name the node ids to evict
    explicitly (a sampling node's output can be a clone of the diffusion model
    and the VAE also carries a patcher), unload only dynamic patchers (classic
    ones are released by the drop), and guard the host API so a rename degrades
    to the drop instead of failing the render.
  verify: >-
    Render one fixed-seed still per engine in-process on the card where both
    models fit, before and after the change, with the host's dynamic-VRAM mode
    on AND off, and require byte-identical output files (same sha256) with a
    lower or equal peak VRAM and no slowdown; then render on the small card
    with the SHIPPED launch flags and require the diffusion model to load with
    usable memory ("loaded completely" or a non-zero "MB usable"), the per-step
    time in the normal range, and more than one still in the same process. A
    unit test stubs the graph executor and asserts the eviction fires for the
    named node at its drop, before the sampler runs, and never for an unnamed
    or classic one.
  tags: [text-encoder-residency, free-after-use, in-process-executor,
    low-vram, dynamic-vram, orphaned-vbar, unload-through-registry,
    partial-load, byte-identical-before-after]
  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260902-01

- id: "12.146"
  phase: 12
  area: vram-residency
  symptom: >-
    A GPU stage (image or video) that runs right after an out-of-band model
    stage -- a transformers LLM writing prompts, a TTS -- loads its diffusion
    model with "0 MB usable" on the small card and runs every step from host
    memory, while an in-process probe of the same engine, started from an
    empty card, works perfectly. On the big card the same episode renders but
    slower than it should, with more models on the card than it can hold.
  cause: >-
    The out-of-band model lives in the pack's own cache, outside the host's
    model registry, so the host's eviction logic never sees it and never
    frees it; the pack released it after one earlier stage but reloaded it
    for a later prompt-writing pass and never released it again before the
    render. Every in-process probe missed the defect because the probe never
    had the writer loaded. A generic "free every registered model" call is
    also blind to it for the same reason.
  fix: >-
    Give the pipeline ONE canonical residue-freer that releases the
    out-of-band caches (LLM, TTS) first, then surgically detaches any
    host-tracked patcher, then flushes the allocator, and call it in every
    GPU stage's load preflight -- image dispatch included, once per dispatch

- id: "12.148"
  phase: 12
  area: metadata-provenance
  symptom: >-
    A resume, replay, or "re-run from a saved bundle" path completes every
    expensive stage correctly and then refuses at the very last node, saying a
    required receipt or metadata key is missing. The same job run normally
    produces that key without trouble, and every unit test of the import or
    clone passes. The cost is the whole job: the work is on disk, the product
    is not.
  cause: >-
    A "clear the previous run's telemetry" list -- the set of keys a clone
    wipes so the source's measurements are not republished as if they described
    this run -- contained a key that only the SKIPPED stage ever writes. The
    reasoning behind the list is sound for every member that the new run
    re-stamps, and silently wrong for any member it does not: a resume path
    exists precisely to avoid re-running stages, so a key whose sole author is
    one of those stages is cleared and never rebuilt. The downstream requirement
    is usually correct to refuse (refusing beats inventing a placeholder); the
    clearing is the defect.
  fix: >-
    Make the list's invariant explicit and enforce it: every member must be
    something the new run demonstrably re-stamps. Remove any key whose only
    author is a stage the resume path skips, and carry it forward -- for
    imported artifacts the source's value is the CORRECT value, not a stale
    one, because the artifacts really were produced by it. Write the rule into
    the constant's own comment so the next person adding a member has to answer
    it.
  verify: >-
    Test what SURVIVES the clone, not only what is cleared. Assert the entire
    requirement SET the terminal consumer demands, not the single key that
    happened to break, because the next key added to the volatile list fails
    identically. Prove the test bites: re-insert the key into the list and
    confirm the test fails, remove it and confirm it passes. A test that
    asserts only "the clear happened" cannot catch this class at all.
  tags: [resume, replay, clone, state-reset, metadata-provenance, receipts,
    volatile-keys, terminal-consumer, fail-loud, test-what-survives]

    from a file. That reasoning held for the consumers that read the FILE and
    failed for the consumer that measures the WIRE: a downstream stage derived
    its own output length from the buffer's length, and a later combining stage
    took the shorter of its two inputs. Nothing raised, because every value was
    individually valid; only the DIMENSION was wrong.
  fix: >-
    A pass-through must pass the real thing through. Read the frozen artifact
    the path is short-circuiting to and emit its actual samples, so downstream
    sees exactly what a normal run produces because it IS what the normal run

    frame budget from a wire rather than from a file, and assert on the
    property rather than the literal: the emitted wire must be longer than any
    placeholder and must track the source artifact's length. Beware the test
    that ENCODES the bug -- in the incident the existing test asserted the
    placeholder's exact shape as the contract, and its fixture was a byte blob
    no decoder could open, so a correct implementation would also have fallen
    back and passed. Build fixtures that a real reader can actually read.

  cause: >-
    A FIXED output-token budget behind a VARIABLE-size request. The pass batched

    reply stopped mid-object, the parser returned {} for "empty", "malformed"
    AND "truncated" alike, and the reseed loop retried the IDENTICAL prompt under
    the IDENTICAL ceiling -- a guaranteed-identical failure that cost two more
    model calls per batch. The warning text named none of this: "unparseable"
    covered three unrelated causes, and the one number that mattered, the budget,
    never appeared in any log.
  fix: >-
    Size the budget to the request (per-item cost times item count, floored and
    ceilinged). Make the retry CHANGE something -- re-bind a doubled budget on a
    truncation-shaped failure, without re-resolving the model. Classify the
    failed reply (empty / truncated / malformed) and print the parsed count, the
    reply length and the budget in the warning, so the next occurrence names
    itself.

    same prompt; a reply that ends mid-JSON at the first and closes cleanly at the
    second is the proof (here: 0 of 4 rows parsed at 300, 4 of 4 at 1200). A
    green unit suite does NOT catch this class -- the failure is a parse miss that
    gets blamed on the model. Two portable rules: a fixed output budget behind a
    request that scales with a count is a defect even when nothing errors; and a
    retry that repeats a deterministic shortfall unchanged is not a retry.

- id: "12.156"

  area: io

  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260904-06
- id: "12.157"
  phase: 12
  area: huggingface
  symptom: >-
    Every model download through `snapshot_download` dies instantly with
    `AttributeError: '<YourClass>' object has no attribute 'total'` (or `n`,
    or `refresh`) the first time a cold cache needs a repo, while single-file
    downloads through `hf_hub_download` in the same process keep working. The
    failure appears only after a progress bar is wired up, so the feature added
    to make downloads visible is what makes them impossible.
  cause: >-
    A hand-rolled stand-in was passed as `tqdm_class=`. `tqdm_class` is not a
    narrow callback interface -- huggingface_hub constructs REAL bars from it
    and then manipulates them as tqdm objects. `snapshot_download` builds two
    parent bars and, once per file, runs
    `parent.total = (parent.total or 0) + total` followed by `parent.refresh()`:
    an attribute READ, an attribute WRITE, and a method call. A look-alike that
    keeps counters private (`self._total`) and implements only
    update/close/__enter__/__exit__ satisfies none of them. The surface is
    whatever the library decides to touch, so it also grows across versions.
    Any download lane that does not hand the library a `tqdm_class` is
    untouched, which is exactly what makes the bug look intermittent and
    feature-specific: in the originating case a second lane moved 36.8 GB
    successfully in the same run because it never used the library's downloader
    at all -- it pinned revisions with `hf_hub_url`/`get_hf_file_metadata` and
    streamed the bytes itself over urllib with a plain `(done, total)` callback.
    When one download path dies and another thrives, compare which one hands the
    library an object, not which one looks busier.
  fix: >-
    SUBCLASS the real `tqdm.std.tqdm` and override only the display hook; do not
    reimplement the protocol. Every attribute then exists and stays writable,
    and it costs no new dependency because tqdm is already required by
    huggingface_hub. Two traps: do NOT pass `disable=True` to keep the console
    quiet -- tqdm short-circuits `update()` when disabled so the display hook
    never runs and the bar silently forwards nothing while looking correct; give
    it a private `io.StringIO` as `file` instead. And pop unexpected kwargs
    (`name=`) defensively, since the library injects extras only for its own
    subclass today. THE HOOK MUST CATCH BaseException, NOT Exception -- this is
    the trap that looks like over-catching and is not. tqdm's `refresh()` is
    `self._lock.acquire(); self.display(); self._lock.release()` with no
    try/finally, and `TqdmDefaultWriteLock` keeps its `th_lock` as a CLASS
    attribute, so ANYTHING the hook raises strands the lock and the next
    `refresh()` on any tqdm anywhere in the process blocks forever. A
    KeyboardInterrupt inside the sink deadlocks the whole application, not the
    download. Losing one Ctrl-C during a progress paint is far cheaper.
    Finally, WRITE COMPLETION EXPLICITLY when the download call returns: files
    already in the cache return before any progress object is built, so a
    resumed transfer aggregates only the missing pieces and a fully cached one
    aggregates nothing, leaving the bar part-way while the work is done.
  verify: >-
    Drive the LIBRARY'S OWN constructor, not your class directly. Call
    `huggingface_hub.utils.tqdm._create_progress_bar(cls=YourAdapter, **kwargs)`
    with the exact keyword arguments the caller passes, then replay the
    aggregation lines verbatim and assert the total accumulates. A test that
    builds the adapter by hand and asserts on its own mock PASSES against the
    broken version -- this is a CONTRACT bug, not a logic bug, so the test is
    worthless unless the real library is in the loop. Also assert progress
    actually reaches the sink (a bar that forwards nothing is the disable=True
    trap) and that a sink which throws does not propagate.
  tags: [huggingface, snapshot_download, tqdm, tqdm-class, progress-bar,
    duck-typing, protocol-surface, contract-test, cold-cache, comfyui]
  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260906-08
- id: "12.159"
  phase: 12
  area: rendering
  symptom: >-
    Text is drawn correctly but POSITIONED wrong -- a centred title sits off to
    one side or runs past the frame edge -- on one platform only, while the same
    build centres perfectly on another. Nothing errors, no test fails, and the
    glyphs themselves look right, which is why it reads as a cosmetic layout bug
    rather than a platform gap. Other text on the SAME frame is placed correctly.
  cause: >-
    TWO INDEPENDENT FONT RESOLVERS, joined only by arithmetic. One subsystem
    MEASURES text to compute a position (here PIL opening a font FILE by
    absolute path, to do `x = centre - text_width // 2`); a different subsystem
    DRAWS it (here libass, handed a font FAMILY NAME in an ASS style, under an
    alignment that anchors at the given coordinate rather than centring itself).
    Nothing passes a "centre" flag anywhere -- the arithmetic IS the centring,
    and it is only as good as the measured width. The two resolvers had separate
    per-platform tables and one of them had no entry for the platform, so
    measurement silently fell back to a bitmap font that IGNORES the requested
    size: a 21-character string measured 131px instead of 1218px at size 96, and
    the real glyphs were drawn from that wrong origin. The correctly-placed text
    on the same frame used an alignment where the DRAWING library did the
    centring, so it never consulted the broken measurement.
  fix: >-
    Make the two sides agree BY CONSTRUCTION, not by two hand-kept tables.
    Iterate FAMILY-MAJOR: complete one family's entire search -- its exact paths
    AND the drawing library's own name-based lookup -- before considering the
    next family, so preference cannot leak between search stages. (Splitting it
    into "all paths, then all names" reintroduces the bug: an exact-path hit on
    the second-choice family returns before the first-choice family's name
    search ever runs.) Put the family the DRAWING side names first. Never let a
    silent fallback stand in for a real face -- log it, because that silence is
    what lets the defect reach a shipped artifact. Provide a path override for
    hosts whose fonts are only reachable through a mechanism the measuring
    library does not consult (fontconfig-only systems such as NixOS: the drawing
    side finds the font and the measuring side cannot).
  verify: >-
    Assert the measured advance-per-character SCALES with the requested size
    (a real face gives roughly 0.6 of the point size; a bitmap fallback gives
    roughly 0.065 and is constant across sizes). Do NOT assert that the centred
    string fits the frame -- `(w/2 - tw/2) + tw <= w` is a TAUTOLOGY for any
    tw <= w and passes against the broken code; that exact test shipped green
    while the bug was live. Assert the measured family MATCHES the family the
    drawing side names, resolved through the drawing side's own accessor so an
    override mismatch fails in CI rather than on a frame. Drive the real
    resolver rather than re-implementing its comparison in the test. CRITICAL
    for any resolver cached in a MODULE GLOBAL: reset that cache between tests,
    or whichever test runs first warms it and every later test is served the
    cached answer without executing the discovery code at all -- twelve source
    mutants survived a QA pass for exactly this reason, including one that
    deleted the entire fallback tier. Key the cache on the CONFIGURATION that
    produced it, not on a "have we resolved yet" boolean, or an override set by
    one run poisons every later run in the same process; and invalidate the
    size-keyed object cache with it, since a stale font object is exactly as
    wrong as a stale path. Publish the cache key AFTER the value, under a lock,
    or a concurrent cold start reads "resolved" with the value still empty.
  tags: [rendering, fonts, text-measurement, two-resolvers, platform-gap,
    silent-fallback, module-global-cache, cache-invalidation, mutation-testing,
    tautological-test, subtitles, comfyui]
  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260909-04

- id: "12.158"
  phase: 12
  area: workflow-json
  symptom: >-
    The application downloads, loads, or runs a different model, engine or
    backend than the one the code calls its default. Nothing errors and no test
    fails; the only symptom is a first-run user paying for a choice that was
    supposedly reversed -- here a 23.9 GB download where the settled default was
    8.7 GB.
  cause: >-
    The default was changed in CODE (a `DEFAULT_*` constant) but the shipped
    graph/preset/template still had the OLD value saved in its widget values.
    A saved graph does not consult the constant; the constant is only a fallback
    for a graph that omits the field, and a shipped template never omits it.
    The two disagreed and the graph won, because the graph is what executes.
  fix: >-
    Change the constant and every SHIPPED artifact that pins it in the same
    commit, then prove the edit is surgical: diff against HEAD and assert links,
    input descriptors and widget COUNT are identical, with only the intended
    values changed. Resolve the widget slots BY NAME through the same helper the
    generator uses -- saved widget arrays are POSITIONAL, so a hardcoded index
    silently addresses the wrong slot the first time a widget is inserted above
    it. Distinguish artifacts that INHERIT the default from ones that carry
    their own per-profile value; only the former are fixed by fixing the master.
  verify: >-
    A test that loads every SHIPPED template, resolves the slot by name, and
    asserts it equals the constant -- plus a second assertion that the saved
    label is an exact member of the live dropdown, since a near-miss string
    fails validation at load rather than falling back. Portable rule: a constant
    the shipped graph disagrees with is DEAD, and "we decided X" is not true
    until the artifact that ships says X. Any decision recorded only in code
    needs a test that ties the artifact to it, or it will drift back silently.
  tags: [workflow-json, widgets-values, positional-widgets, shipped-default,
    dead-constant, template-drift, first-run-cost, comfyui]
  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260906-09

- id: "11.62"
  phase: 11
  area: generated-plan-boundary-count
  symptom: >-
    A generated plan promises between-section events for a one-section request,
    even though deterministic assembly correctly emits no such events.
  cause: >-
    The prompt projects an enabled checkbox as an unconditional affirmative.
    The assembler derives the actual count from topology. These two projections
    disagree at the one-section boundary: enabled does not imply any boundaries.
  fix: >-
    Derive the boundary count once from the selected topology and the enable
    control, then use that same rule for planning, framing and assembly. For
    adjacent sections the count is max(0, section_count - 1) when enabled,
    otherwise zero. State the numeric count to the model. Preserve unused
    proposals as evidence; do not add prose keyword bans to hide a false prompt.
  verify: >-
    Exercise the real prompt builders and assembler together for one, two, three
    and six sections with the enable control on and off. Assert the prompt count,
    exact emitted boundary anchors, zero-boundary surplus disposition and final
    structural validation. A helper-only arithmetic test misses divergent callers.
    OTR coverage: test_the_music_cues_anchor_to_real_sentinel_rows and
    test_zero_boundaries_preserves_all_unused_cue_proposals.
  tags: [llm, prompt-projection, topology, boundary-count, source-fidelity]
  legacy_id: OTR-PBUG-20260910-04

- id: "11.63"
  phase: 11
  area: structured-pass-schema-binder-routing
  symptom: >-
    A local structured pass repeatedly emits undecodable output even though its
    transport already exposes a schema-binding capability. Parsing and repair
    are present, but generation never receives the grammar.
    The same bypass appeared in the live pairlock_02 cleanup scope authorizer:
    spans:null twice failed its nonnullable list schema despite an available
    native binder, so valid dialogue retained an unresolved cleanup flag.
  cause: >-
    The caller supplies a schema to post-generation validation while invoking
    the unbound callable. A schema argument in the retry helper does not itself
    connect that schema to token selection in the transport. This differs from
    missing prompt/schema contracts (11.38) and unsupported grammars (11.55).
  fix: >-
    Bind the affected pass's local callable once to its production schema when
    the capability is available, and use that callable for every existing retry.
    Preserve neighboring passes, slot ownership/accounting and transports without
    the capability. Keep ordinary parsing and bounded typed repair after decoding.
  verify: >-
    Exercise the actual pass with a recording binder. Require one binding to the
    correct schema, every attempt through the returned callable, unchanged
    neighboring passes and failure after malformed output exhausts the ladder.
    Verify the scheduler forwards the schema to the real local generation owner
    and the owner supplies its prefix constraint to model.generate. OTR anchors:
    test_only_p1_binds_once_and_all_its_retries_use_that_callable,
    test_scheduler_local_schema_binding_reaches_truncating_generator and
    test_generate_invoked_with_prefix_fn. Repeated real-model qualification is
    separate from this executable wiring proof.
    Include the public cleaner's binder-once/reused-retry behavior and actual
    grammar admission of omitted/empty spans versus exclusion of explicit null.
  tags: [llm, structured-json, schema-binding, call-routing, retry]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260910-05

- id: "11.64"
  phase: 11
  area: model-cleanup-original-scope-conservation
  symptom: >-
    A cleanup model condemns valid spoken text and replaces it with different
    dialogue. On the 4060 My Story Step 120, Until next time became That's a
    wrap. The ledger committed the rewrite rather than retaining a flagged row.
  cause: >-
    A complaint about a passage was treated as permission to rewrite the whole
    row. Display summaries do not preserve exact offsets, repeated quotes do
    not identify an occurrence, and several partial complaints can collectively
    cover the entire row. A clean reread or reduced complaint count proves none
    of the original text outside the complaint was conserved.
  fix: >-
    Freeze exact original intervals once. Pattern matches retain raw offsets;
    model quotes need unique exact grounding or bounded localization. Require
    dedicated model authorization for collective whole-row scope. For partial
    repairs validate one model replacement per approved ID and interleave it
    with unchanged original slices. Keep whitespace and punctuation exactly,
    never widen scope on retry, and require a validated model reread before
    clean or best-progress acceptance. Unresolved authorization or reread
    retains the original with a receipt;
    actual provider, capacity, cancellation and memory failures keep their type.
  verify: >-
    Exercise the shared cleaner on the live coda false accusation, a genuine
    whole-row direction, repeated quotes and disjoint edits. Require exact
    outside-scope conservation, reject collective whole-row bypasses and invalid
    replacement IDs, malformed reread, and show the candidate in reread context. OTR executable
    regressions live in tests/test_ledger_clean_stage.py; the portable Bible
    guard exercises exact occurrence grounding, original-slice construction
    and collective coverage without importing model or GPU runtimes. A fresh
    canonical model run is separately required for live qualification.
  tags: [llm, cleanup, source-fidelity, scoped-repair, ledger]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260829-14

- id: "11.65"
  phase: 11
  area: sparse-rewrite-materializes-author-defaults-over-accepted-data
  symptom: >-
    A correction changes accepted metadata even though its raw JSON explicitly
    changed none of those values. Live OTR My Story pairlock_02 omitted fields;

    The operation was recorded as an applied rewrite.
  cause: >-
    A permissive schema appropriate for first authorship is reused as a full
    replacement contract. After validation materializes defaults, model_dump

    overlays can transfer metadata between different identities when reordered.

    Reconstruct omitted fields from an immutable accepted candidate, using only
    explicit unique stable identities for nested lists. Explicit values and

    object before returning, persisting or hashing it. Each failed attempt must
    reuse the original baseline. Do not apply full-artifact overlay to edit-script
    responses with a different shape, or add another model pass/retry budget.

    reordered/removed/added/ambiguous identities and explicit empty values. Test

    response must stay unchanged without extra calls. OTR regressions are in

- id: "12.160"
  phase: 12
  area: rendered-text-container-containment
  symptom: >-
    A long credits title crosses its own column and collides with the adjacent
    cast heading although a shorter title fits. The Mac canonical publication
    The Lantern Burns Bright While Slander Hides on 2026-09-10 exhibited this
    in its saved credits frame; the episode completed and was accepted with
    the appearance defect parked. Evidence is OTR
    docs/2026-09-10-mac-final/README.md and images/credits.png, publication
    SHA-256 986d2cb71576ce8ac73cf14850f5fecd85dbf460dc7e4fc875c107348f8b88d4.
  cause: >-
    Autoshrink stopped at the minimum readable size, then drew the entire
    unwrapped title without a container-width guarantee. A size floor does not
    imply a width bound. Measuring advance alone also misses negative bearings
    and glyph overhang. This is distinct from 12.159: the measuring and drawing
    fonts can agree perfectly and still overflow when no wrapping owns the
    container or when the next block assumes a single title line.
  fix: >-
    Preserve short-title typography, then wrap the complete title at its existing
    legibility floor. Split overlong words between display clusters, retaining
    combining marks, modifiers, flag pairs and joined symbols. Use the drawing
    library's glyph bounds, account for bearings at the origin, and advance the
    following block below all title lines. Share this exact layout between
    scratch measurement and paint. Feed the measured height into the existing
    footer-spacing and explicitly marked abridgment policy; do not silently
    ellipsize the title or drop reproducibility records. A canvas smaller than
    an indivisible glyph or the complete minimum card needs an explicit layout
    limitation, not a false containment claim or destruction of finished media.
  verify: >-
    Exercise actual font drawing at each supported card size with the published
    Lantern title, a fitting short title, a long unbroken token, combining marks,
    modified emoji, flag pairs and joined symbols. Assert all displayed title
    characters survive, actual painted bounds remain inside the column, lines
    do not overlap, and the subtitle follows the lowest title glyph. Assert
    scratch and paint heights agree and the live title respects the footer
    policy. Include a real italic font with negative bearings. OTR anchors:
    test_hero_complete_glyphs_stay_in_column_and_subtitle_follows,
    test_live_lantern_title_preserves_footer_policy,
    test_hero_negative_bearing_uses_actual_font_bounds and
    test_hero_wrap_keeps_modified_and_joined_symbols_together in
    tests/test_credits_roll_spec.py. The portable guard executes the production
    wrapping helpers and requires these real-font regressions to remain present.
  tags: [rendering, credits, typography, glyph-bounds, unicode, wrapping, containment]

- id: "12.161"
  phase: 12
  area: health-diagnostics-frozen-route-intent
  symptom: >-
    A successful intentionally all-stills render emits failed-LTX health
    warnings although no LTX route was requested. The 4060 My Story Step 121
    published signal_lost_los_angeles_dinner_pair_lock_visual_leg_20260910_193857
    with 13 still_pan clips and logged five LTX-OPEN HEALTH warnings. The
    committed live receipts are OTR docs/4060_DRILL_LOG.md Step 121 and
    docs/2026-09-10-my-story-4060/README.md. This is a false diagnostic on a
    completed media route, not evidence that LTX or the stills render failed.
  cause: >-
    The health check inferred a required engine family from a role name rather
    than consulting the frozen effective route. It compared every opener to
    LTX even when the operator chose a registered non-LTX engine. Conversely,
    simply suppressing warnings when route evidence is absent would confuse
    unknown applicability with a verified healthy requested path.
  fix: >-
    Copy frozen effective route intent into the artifact manifest and compare
    it with delivered engine identity and artifact existence. Report a requested
    LTX route with an existing accepted-family artifact as healthy; a requested
    route with a wrong or absent artifact as degraded; an intentional registered
    non-LTX route as not requested; and missing or unmapped intent as unknown,
    even when an actual LTX artifact exists. Preserve sanctioned-gap handling
    separately. Return these primitive distinctions in the durable report;
    an empty offending-row list alone is not a health claim. Keep the default
    checker non-mutating, strict failure scoped to real requested degradation,
    and fallback, audio and routing behavior unchanged.
  verify: >-
    Exercise each accepted LTX engine with an existing artifact, an intended
    non-LTX route, absent and unmapped intent, missing artifacts, sanctioned
    gaps, and strict-mode failures. Prove actual LTX alone cannot turn unknown
    intent into health. Build a real manifest from the frozen effective map,
    verify a selected or mutated shot route does not replace it, and mutate
    the source map afterward to prove the manifest owns a copy. OTR regressions
    live in tests/test_ltx_open_health.py and test_ltx_open_health_stdlib.py;
    the portable guard directly exercises the production classifier and pins
    the manifest-persistence and non-mutation regression owners.
  tags: [diagnostics, telemetry, frozen-route, provenance, false-warning, applicability]
  legacy_id: PBUG-20260906-03
